### Implicit Actor Critic Coupling via a Supervised Learning Framework for RLVR
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02522v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaming Li, Longze Chen, Ze Gong, Yukun Chen, Lu Wang, Wanwei He, Run Luo, Min Yang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) have empowered large language models (LLMs) to tackle challenging reasoning tasks such as mathematics and programming. RLVR leverages verifiable outcome rewards to guide policy optimization, enabling LLMs to progressively improve output quality in a grounded and reliable manner. Despite its promise, the RLVR paradigm poses significant challenges, as existing methods often suffer from sparse reward signals and unstable policy gradient updates, particularly in RL-based approaches. To address the challenges, we propose $\textbf{PACS}$, a novel RLVR framework that achieves im$\textbf{P}$licit $\textbf{A}$ctor $\textbf{C}$ritic coupling via a $\textbf{S}$upervised learning framework. By treating the outcome reward as a predictable label, we reformulate the RLVR problem into a supervised learning task over a score function parameterized by the policy model and optimized using cross-entropy loss. A detailed gradient analysis shows that this supervised formulation inherently recovers the classical policy gradient update while implicitly coupling actor and critic roles, yielding more stable and efficient training. Benchmarking on challenging mathematical reasoning tasks, PACS outperforms strong RLVR baselines, such as PPO and GRPO, achieving superior reasoning performance. For instance, PACS achieves 59.78\% at pass@256 on AIME 2025, representing improvements of 13.32 and 14.36 points over PPO and GRPO. This simple yet powerful framework offers a promising avenue for LLMs post-training with verifiable rewards. Our code and data are available as open source at https://github.com/ritzz-ai/PACS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PACS (imPlicit Actor Critic coupling via a Supervised learning framework), a novel approach for Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) designed to train Large Language Models (LLMs) for complex reasoning tasks. Instead of using RL to optimize the policy with sparse outcome rewards, PACS reframes the problem as supervised learning, where the outcome reward is treated as a predictable label.  A cross-entropy loss is used to train a score function, parameterized by the policy model, to predict this reward. The paper argues, through gradient analysis, that this supervised learning setup recovers policy gradient updates while implicitly coupling actor and critic roles via shared parameterization, resulting in more stable and efficient training.  Experiments on mathematical reasoning tasks demonstrate PACS's superior performance compared to RLVR baselines like PPO and GRPO.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of reformulating RLVR as a supervised learning problem by treating the outcome reward as a label has merit and is fairly novel within the specific RLVR context. Prior work certainly has used supervised learning *in conjunction* with RL, but the direct replacement is less common. The implicit actor-critic coupling through a shared parameterization is also a valuable contribution, simplifying training and reducing the temporal mismatch between reward estimation and policy updates found in more traditional actor-critic methods.  However, the use of RLOO estimator and periodic reference policy resets are borrowed from existing work. Also the idea of using cross-entropy and supervised learning to estimate rewards for reinforcement learning is not entirely novel.

*   **Significance:**  The paper presents compelling empirical results, demonstrating significant performance gains over strong baselines (PPO, GRPO) on challenging mathematical reasoning benchmarks.  The improvement in reasoning performance, particularly on tasks like AIME 2025, positions PACS as a potentially impactful approach for LLM fine-tuning in domains requiring verifiable outcomes. The detailed gradient analysis provides solid theoretical justification for the approach, and the ablation studies offer valuable insights into the importance of specific components like the weighting mechanism. The training dynamics plots also suggest advantages in exploration-exploitation balance, an important area for LLM optimization. This could potentially lead to better training paradigms for mathematical reasoning and other domains.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The supervised learning reformulation simplifies the RLVR process and potentially avoids issues with sparse rewards and unstable policy gradients that plague traditional RL approaches.
    *   The implicit actor-critic coupling is a strong design choice, promoting efficient training and removing the need for separate actor and critic networks.
    *   The empirical results are convincing, demonstrating significant gains on complex mathematical reasoning tasks.
    *   The gradient analysis provides a clear theoretical understanding of why PACS works.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While novel in the combination and the specific RLVR context, the supervised learning re-framing and the shared parameterization are not completely groundbreaking individually. Some elements of the method, like RLOO estimation, rely heavily on existing techniques.
    *   The paper could benefit from a broader evaluation across a wider range of tasks and reward structures. The focus on mathematical reasoning, while valuable, may limit the generalizability of the findings. The paper would also be stronger with more rigorous statistical analysis to confirm the significance of the results.
    *   There isn't a full discussion on limitations. When wouldn't it apply? Does it require very high quality reward functions?

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the RLVR literature. The reframing of the problem as supervised learning, alongside the implicit actor-critic approach, represents a meaningful advancement, evidenced by strong empirical performance. While the individual components are not entirely novel, the combination and application within the RLVR setting are compelling.  Given that a majority of the components used are taken from other works and PACS builds upon existing work, combined with the relatively narrow focus of the empirical evaluation.

### Is RL fine-tuning harder than regression? A PDE learning approach for diffusion models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02528v1)
- **Authors**: Wenlong Mou
- **Abstract**: We study the problem of learning the optimal control policy for fine-tuning a given diffusion process, using general value function approximation. We develop a new class of algorithms by solving a variational inequality problem based on the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman (HJB) equations. We prove sharp statistical rates for the learned value function and control policy, depending on the complexity and approximation errors of the function class. In contrast to generic reinforcement learning problems, our approach shows that fine-tuning can be achieved via supervised regression, with faster statistical rate guarantees.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper tackles the problem of reinforcement learning (RL) for fine-tuning diffusion models. It proposes a new class of algorithms based on solving a variational inequality derived from the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman (HJB) equations. The key contribution is proving sharp statistical rates for the learned value function and control policy, showing that fine-tuning, in this specific context, can be achieved with rates comparable to supervised regression, thus circumventing the typical difficulties associated with general RL problems (e.g., high variance, instability, and unrealistic assumptions). The approach involves a self-mitigating property in the complexity term, and an efficient computational method based on iteratively solving least-square regression problems. The paper further discusses connections to classifier guidance, deep Ritz/Galerkin methods, and provides sub-optimality gap guarantees.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in adapting the machinery of continuous-time RL and PDE-based approaches to the specific problem of fine-tuning diffusion models. It demonstrates that under certain conditions (uniform ellipticity, moment bounds on coefficients, regularity of the density), and with the use of a careful variational formulation, the statistical limitations typical of RL can be bypassed. The self-mitigating error property is a valuable and non-standard result. The reduction to a sequence of least-squares regression problems is also a useful algorithmic contribution. While connections to path integral formulations and classifier guidance exist in the literature, the precise theoretical guarantees derived in this paper for the proposed method are novel.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the growing importance of diffusion models and the need for efficient and robust methods to fine-tune them. The paper's theoretical results provide a compelling argument that fine-tuning, unlike general RL, can be achieved with supervised learning-like guarantees.  This has the potential to guide the development of more efficient RL algorithms for diffusion model applications and shed light on the problem's inherent structure.  The derived error bounds and analysis relating approximation and statistical error provide a deeper theoretical understanding of the problem space.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Theoretical Analysis:** The paper provides detailed proofs and statistical guarantees, including oracle inequalities and sub-optimality gap bounds.
    *   **Problem-Specific Approach:**  The methodology is tailored to the structure of diffusion model fine-tuning, leveraging the HJB equation and connections to parabolic PDEs.
    *   **Efficient Algorithm:** The iterative algorithm reduces the problem to solving a sequence of least-squares regressions, a well-studied and computationally efficient task.
    *   **Self-Mitigating Property:** The demonstration of the self-mitigating error phenomenon provides valuable insight and can lead to faster convergence rates.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Assumptions:**  The analysis relies on assumptions like uniform ellipticity, moment bounds on coefficients, and regularity conditions on the density of the diffusion process. These assumptions may not hold in all practical scenarios, and the paper needs to discuss this issue carefully.
    *   **Practical Implementation:**  While the algorithm is theoretically sound, the paper could benefit from a discussion of the practical challenges in implementing it with deep neural networks, particularly in high-dimensional settings.  Empirical validation would significantly bolster the paper's claims, especially comparing against KL or entropy regularization methods.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The results are specific to control-affine diffusion processes and may not generalize directly to other types of generative models or RL problems.
    *   **Constant Dependencies:** The reliance on problem-dependent constants is a source of concern. How these constants scale with key parameters is needed.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's insights could significantly impact the design of RL algorithms for fine-tuning generative models and potentially influence the development of new ML-based PDE solvers. It also opens up new avenues for research in understanding the theoretical limitations of RL in specific contexts.

*   **Conclusion:**
Given the strong theoretical foundation, novel insights regarding the problem structure, and potential to impact the field, but also considering the limitations related to assumptions, limited scope and need for empirical validation, a score of 7 seems appropriate. The theoretical result is solid and important for future research, but practical limitations may diminish its broad impacts.

### Jointly Reinforcing Diversity and Quality in Language Model Generations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02534v1)
- **Authors**: Tianjian Li, Yiming Zhang, Ping Yu, Swarnadeep Saha, Daniel Khashabi, Jason Weston, Jack Lanchantin, Tianlu Wang
- **Abstract**: Post-training of Large Language Models (LMs) often prioritizes accuracy and helpfulness at the expense of diversity. This creates a tension: while post-training improves response quality, it also sharpens output distributions and reduces the range of ideas, limiting the usefulness of LMs in creative and exploratory tasks such as brainstorming, storytelling, or problem solving. We address this challenge with Diversity-Aware Reinforcement Learning (DARLING), a framework that jointly optimizes for response quality and semantic diversity. At its core, DARLING introduces a learned partition function to measure diversity beyond surface-level lexical variations. This diversity signal is then combined with a quality reward during online reinforcement learning, encouraging models to generate outputs that are both high-quality and distinct. Experiments across multiple model families and sizes show that DARLING generalizes to two regimes: non-verifiable tasks (instruction following and creative writing) and verifiable tasks (competition math). On five benchmarks in the first setting, DARLING consistently outperforms quality-only RL baselines, producing outputs that are simultaneously of higher quality and novelty. In the second setting, DARLING achieves higher pass@1 (solution quality) and pass@k (solution variety). Most strikingly, explicitly optimizing for diversity catalyzes exploration in online RL, which manifests itself as higher-quality responses.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Diversity-Aware Reinforcement Learning (DARLING), a framework designed to counteract the tendency of large language model (LLM) post-training to reduce output diversity. DARLING employs a learned partition function that measures semantic diversity beyond surface-level lexical variations, then integrates this diversity signal with a quality reward during online reinforcement learning.  This encourages models to generate outputs that are both high-quality and distinct. The authors demonstrate the efficacy of DARLING across various model families and sizes, in both non-verifiable tasks (instruction following, creative writing) and verifiable tasks (competition math). The results indicate DARLING outperforms quality-only RL baselines, improving both quality and novelty of outputs.  Specifically, they demonstrate improved pass@1 (solution quality) and pass@k (solution variety) in verifiable tasks and improvements on AlpacaEval, ArenaHard and EQ-bench in non-verifiable tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of addressing diversity loss in LLM post-training isn't entirely new. Previous works have attempted to tackle this challenge using various techniques such as modified loss functions, diverse decoding strategies, and alternative training data. The **key novelty of this paper lies in its specific approach:** using a learned semantic classifier for diversity measurement *within a reinforcement learning framework*, combined multiplicatively with a quality reward.  The use of a learned partition function to measure diversity at a semantic level *rather* than relying solely on lexical variations or heuristics is a significant step forward, although the underlying concept of clustering responses based on similarity exists in prior work. The multiplication of diversity and quality is a simple but effective idea that addresses the differing reward scales.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in LLM development: the trade-off between quality and diversity. The diminishing diversity resulting from post-training limits the applicability of LLMs in creative and exploratory tasks. By presenting a framework that effectively maintains (and potentially enhances) diversity without sacrificing quality, the paper offers a valuable contribution. The empirical results, demonstrating improved performance across different tasks and models, strengthen the paper's significance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The method is clearly described and relatively simple to implement (given access to the pre-trained semantic classifier).
    *   The empirical evaluation is comprehensive, covering multiple model families, sizes, and task types.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of different design choices within the DARLING framework (e.g., multiplicative vs. additive reward aggregation, semantic vs. lexical diversity metrics).
    *   The observation that explicitly optimizing for diversity can *catalyze* exploration in online RL is particularly interesting and could have broader implications for RL in language models.
    *   The paper validates the method against benchmarks used in prior-work.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on a pre-trained semantic classifier adds a layer of complexity and dependence. The performance of DARLING is intrinsically linked to the effectiveness of this classifier. While the authors demonstrate good classifier performance, the potential limitations of the classifier (e.g., its bias, domain specificity) are not explored in depth. The computational overhead of the classifier is also not discussed, particularly for very large model sizes.
    * The learned semantic classifier is trained with data sampled from the *output* of several smaller LMs. It is possible that the LMs have a strong bias and may result in the classifier exhibiting these biases as well, which might make the results seem "novel" but are actually just the avoidance of previous bias.
    *   While the authors present a thorough evaluation, the benefits of DARLING may be more pronounced in specific application scenarios where diversity is paramount. Further investigation into the application-specific advantages of DARLING would be valuable.
    *   The improvements, although consistent and demonstrable, are not necessarily *massive* increases in overall performance. Further work may need to explore ways of significantly increasing overall performance.
*   **Potential Influence:** DARLING could potentially influence the development of future RL-based post-training methods for LLMs, inspiring new approaches to diversity maintenance and exploration. The finding that diversity optimization can improve exploration could lead to novel RL strategies beyond the scope of this paper. Other researchers could directly utilize the DARING technique for their own models.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to a significant problem in LLM post-training. The use of a learned semantic classifier for diversity measurement within a RL framework is a valuable contribution. The empirical results are convincing, and the ablation studies provide useful insights. While the dependence on a pre-trained classifier and the moderate improvements observed represent limitations, the overall significance and potential influence of the work justify a score of 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Landscape of Agentic Reinforcement Learning for LLMs: A Survey
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02547v1)
- **Authors**: Guibin Zhang, Hejia Geng, Xiaohang Yu, Zhenfei Yin, Zaibin Zhang, Zelin Tan, Heng Zhou, Zhongzhi Li, Xiangyuan Xue, Yijiang Li, Yifan Zhou, Yang Chen, Chen Zhang, Yutao Fan, Zihu Wang, Songtao Huang, Yue Liao, Hongru Wang, Mengyue Yang, Heng Ji, Michael Littman, Jun Wang, Shuicheng Yan, Philip Torr, Lei Bai
- **Abstract**: The emergence of agentic reinforcement learning (Agentic RL) marks a paradigm shift from conventional reinforcement learning applied to large language models (LLM RL), reframing LLMs from passive sequence generators into autonomous, decision-making agents embedded in complex, dynamic worlds. This survey formalizes this conceptual shift by contrasting the degenerate single-step Markov Decision Processes (MDPs) of LLM-RL with the temporally extended, partially observable Markov decision processes (POMDPs) that define Agentic RL. Building on this foundation, we propose a comprehensive twofold taxonomy: one organized around core agentic capabilities, including planning, tool use, memory, reasoning, self-improvement, and perception, and the other around their applications across diverse task domains. Central to our thesis is that reinforcement learning serves as the critical mechanism for transforming these capabilities from static, heuristic modules into adaptive, robust agentic behavior. To support and accelerate future research, we consolidate the landscape of open-source environments, benchmarks, and frameworks into a practical compendium. By synthesizing over five hundred recent works, this survey charts the contours of this rapidly evolving field and highlights the opportunities and challenges that will shape the development of scalable, general-purpose AI agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper abstract:

**Summary:**

This paper presents a survey on "Agentic Reinforcement Learning (RL) for LLMs," an emerging paradigm that shifts LLMs from passive sequence generators to autonomous, decision-making agents in complex environments.  It contrasts traditional LLM RL (single-step MDPs) with Agentic RL (partially observable, temporally extended POMDPs). The survey proposes a two-fold taxonomy: (1) core agentic capabilities (planning, tool use, memory, reasoning, self-improvement, perception) and (2) their applications across various tasks. It posits that RL is crucial for transforming static capabilities into adaptive agentic behavior. The survey consolidates open-source environments, benchmarks, and frameworks.  Synthesizing over 500 works, the paper aims to chart the course of this field and identify opportunities and challenges for scalable, general-purpose AI agents.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in formalizing and defining "Agentic RL" as a distinct paradigm shift.  While LLM agents and RL applied to LLMs are independently studied, explicitly framing LLMs as learnable *policies* within a POMDP framework and emphasizing the *agentic* capabilities that RL unlocks offers a new perspective. The proposed taxonomy, combining capability-centric and task-centric views, provides a structured way to organize existing work.

*   **Significance:** The survey addresses a timely and crucial direction in AI research.  As LLMs become increasingly integrated into interactive and dynamic environments, moving beyond static generation is essential. This survey serves to unify terminology, identify common themes, and consolidate resources, which can accelerate research in this area. By synthesizing a large number of works, the survey helps to establish a broader perspective. The identification of open challenges (trustworthiness, scalability) is also a valuable contribution, pointing toward important areas for future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear definition of Agentic RL and its distinction from traditional LLM RL.
    *   Comprehensive taxonomy covering both agentic capabilities and task domains.
    *   Extensive synthesis of a large volume of recent research (500+ works).
    *   Practical compendium of environments, benchmarks, and frameworks.
    *   Focus on the role of RL as a mechanism for achieving adaptive agentic behavior.
    *   Addresses open challenges.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The abstract, naturally, lacks detail on the specific methodological contributions of each surveyed work. The value will depend on the depth and insight provided in the full paper when classifying and synthesizing research.
    *   The claim of "consolidating the landscape" needs to be validated by the comprehensiveness and critical assessment of the survey. There could be biases in the selection or interpretation of included works.
    *   The emphasis on a "paradigm shift" might be overstated.  It needs to be justified by demonstrating that Agentic RL truly represents a fundamentally different approach from existing LLM RL techniques, rather than just an extension or evolution.

*   **Potential Influence:** This survey has the potential to significantly influence the field by:
    *   Providing a common language and understanding of Agentic RL.
    *   Identifying gaps in current research and guiding future directions.
    *   Facilitating collaboration and knowledge sharing among researchers.
    *   Establishing a benchmark for evaluating progress in the development of scalable, general-purpose AI agents.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for assigned Score:**  The assigned score reflects the survey's potential to organize and advance a rapidly evolving field. While the core concepts (LLMs, RL, agents) are not inherently novel, the synthesis and framing in terms of Agentic RL and its implications *are*. The survey's value hinges on the quality of its analysis and its ability to identify future research directions. Based on the abstract, this justifies a score that leans towards higher significance, but acknowledging the reliance on detailed information within the full paper:

### PalmX 2025: The First Shared Task on Benchmarking LLMs on Arabic and Islamic Culture
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02550v1)
- **Authors**: Fakhraddin Alwajih, Abdellah El Mekki, Hamdy Mubarak, Majd Hawasly, Abubakr Mohamed, Muhammad Abdul-Mageed
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) inherently reflect the vast data distributions they encounter during their pre-training phase. As this data is predominantly sourced from the web, there is a high chance it will be skewed towards high-resourced languages and cultures, such as those of the West. Consequently, LLMs often exhibit a diminished understanding of certain communities, a gap that is particularly evident in their knowledge of Arabic and Islamic cultures. This issue becomes even more pronounced with increasingly under-represented topics. To address this critical challenge, we introduce PalmX 2025, the first shared task designed to benchmark the cultural competence of LLMs in these specific domains. The task is composed of two subtasks featuring multiple-choice questions (MCQs) in Modern Standard Arabic (MSA): General Arabic Culture and General Islamic Culture. These subtasks cover a wide range of topics, including traditions, food, history, religious practices, and language expressions from across 22 Arab countries. The initiative drew considerable interest, with 26 teams registering for Subtask 1 and 19 for Subtask 2, culminating in nine and six valid submissions, respectively. Our findings reveal that task-specific fine-tuning substantially boosts performance over baseline models. The top-performing systems achieved an accuracy of 72.15% on cultural questions and 84.22% on Islamic knowledge. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning emerged as the predominant and most effective approach among participants, while the utility of data augmentation was found to be domain-dependent.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the PalmX 2025 paper:

**Summary:**

The PalmX 2025 paper introduces a novel shared task designed to benchmark the cultural competence of Large Language Models (LLMs) in Arabic and Islamic contexts. The task is comprised of two subtasks featuring multiple-choice questions in Modern Standard Arabic: General Arabic Culture and General Islamic Culture. The paper details the data collection process, which involves both sourcing from existing datasets and crawling diverse online resources. It also outlines the rules for participation, including constraints on model size and the prohibition of real-time data retrieval. The paper presents the results of the shared task, highlighting the performance of various participating teams and analyzes the methodological approaches used. It concludes by discussing the domain-specific challenges and the limitations of the benchmark.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its novelty. To the best of our knowledge, PalmX 2025 represents the *first* shared task specifically designed to evaluate LLMs' cultural understanding within the Arabic and Islamic domains. This fills a significant gap in the field, as most existing benchmarks focus on general language abilities or other specific tasks without addressing cultural nuances. The creation of a specific cultural benchmark is a valuable contribution in itself.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the increasing recognition of the importance of culturally aligned AI systems. As LLMs are deployed in diverse cultural contexts, it becomes crucial to ensure that they possess a solid understanding of local norms, values, and traditions. PalmX 2025 offers a standardized evaluation framework to guide the development of more culturally grounded and competent Arabic LLMs. The paper's finding that task-specific fine-tuning significantly improves performance underscores the need for targeted cultural adaptation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly defined task and subtasks with rationale.
    *   Rigorous data collection methodology, including multiple review stages.
    *   Appropriate constraints imposed on participants to ensure fair evaluation.
    *   Comprehensive analysis of the results and methodological approaches.
    *   Discussion of ethical considerations related to culturally-aware AI.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Evaluation Metric Limitations:** The reliance on multiple-choice questions in MSA, while ensuring clarity and reproducibility, has a limited scope of assessing cultural and linguistic abilities and nuances. The inability to asses subtleties, open-ended responses, and dialectical variations.
    *   **Dataset Imbalances:** Even though acknowledged in the paper, imbalances still affect the dataset regarding region representations.
    *   **Reliance on Machine Translation and LLM for Data Generation:** Even though accompanied with manual verification, there's still a chance for translated content and content generated via LLMs to introduce subtle biases.
    *   **Limited Cultural Scope:** Even though specifically intended for Arab/Islamic culture, these are not monoliths. The variations of Islam outside of Arabic speakers are excluded from the dataset. Similarly, within Arabic speakers, more localized and regional variations might be missing.
    *   The relatively modest absolute scores highlight that models struggle with general cultural and Islamic knowledge.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a considerable influence on the field by:

    *   Stimulating further research on cultural bias and alignment in LLMs.
    *   Providing a benchmark for evaluating Arabic LLMs.
    *   Encouraging the development of more culturally sensitive data collection methods.
    *   Informing the design of culturally grounded model architectures.

*   **Rigor:** The paper provides a detailed description of the methodology, data collection process, and analysis, supporting its claims with empirical evidence. However, while the paper demonstrates the effectiveness of task-specific fine-tuning, it does not delve deeply into the specific cultural concepts or knowledge points that LLMs struggle with the most. It remains unclear which types of cultural knowledge are easiest or hardest for models to acquire. Additionally, there's room for exploration of more advanced evaluation metrics that go beyond simple accuracy, such as metrics that specifically reward culturally appropriate or nuanced responses.

Overall, the PalmX 2025 paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by introducing the first shared task focused on the cultural competence of LLMs in the Arabic and Islamic domains. While some limitations exist, the paper is significant and likely to influence future research.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Lighting the Way for BRIGHT: Reproducible Baselines with Anserini, Pyserini, and RankLLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02558v1)
- **Authors**: Yijun Ge, Sahel Sharifymoghaddam, Jimmy Lin
- **Abstract**: The BRIGHT benchmark is a dataset consisting of reasoning-intensive queries over diverse domains. We explore retrieval results on BRIGHT using a range of retrieval techniques, including sparse, dense, and fusion methods, and establish reproducible baselines. We then apply listwise reranking with large language models (LLMs) to further investigate the impact of reranking on reasoning-intensive queries. These baselines are integrated into popular retrieval and reranking toolkits Anserini, Pyserini, and RankLLM, with two-click reproducibility that makes them easy to build upon and convenient for further development. While attempting to reproduce the results reported in the original BRIGHT paper, we find that the provided BM25 scores differ notably from those that we obtain using Anserini and Pyserini. We discover that this difference is due to BRIGHT's implementation of BM25, which applies BM25 on the query rather than using the standard bag-of-words approach, as in Anserini, to construct query vectors. This difference has become increasingly relevant due to the rise of longer queries, with BRIGHT's lengthy reasoning-intensive queries being a prime example, and further accentuated by the increasing usage of retrieval-augmented generation, where LLM prompts can grow to be much longer than ''traditional'' search engine queries. Our observation signifies that it may be time to reconsider BM25 approaches going forward in order to better accommodate emerging applications. To facilitate this, we integrate query-side BM25 into both Anserini and Pyserini.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper focuses on establishing reproducible baselines for the BRIGHT benchmark, a dataset designed to evaluate retrieval systems' performance on reasoning-intensive queries, particularly relevant for retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). The authors integrate several retrieval methods (BM25, BGE-large-en-v1.5, SPLADE-v3) into the Anserini, Pyserini, and RankLLM toolkits, enhancing their usability and reproducibility.  A key finding is a discrepancy in BM25 scores compared to the original BRIGHT paper due to the latter's use of "query-side BM25" (applying BM25 to query terms rather than the standard bag-of-words approach). The authors also investigate listwise reranking with LLMs and observe performance improvements. Finally, the paper identifies potential improvements to the BRIGHT dataset regarding duplicate documents and very short documents.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its careful reproduction of existing methods and their integration into widely used IR toolkits.  While integrating benchmarks and baselines into existing toolkits is valuable and contributes to the community, the individual retrieval methods are not novel. The discovery of the BM25 implementation difference in the original BRIGHT paper is a significant and noteworthy observation.  The exploration of this discrepancy and its potential implications for modern IR (with longer queries from LLMs) has novelty. However, the paper’s reranking aspect uses existing models (Qwen, gpt-oss), and simply integrates them with RankLLM and BRIGHT, which represents relatively less novelty.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is threefold:

    1.  *Reproducibility and Usability:*  Providing reproducible baselines for BRIGHT in Anserini, Pyserini, and RankLLM is a significant service to the IR community, facilitating easier experimentation and comparison of new techniques.
    2.  *BM25 Implementation Insight:* Identifying the difference in BM25 implementation sheds light on a potentially overlooked aspect of the algorithm, especially concerning long queries, which are becoming more prevalent in the LLM era. This encourages a re-evaluation of existing BM25 implementations.
    3.  *Dataset Observations:* The identification of issues within the BRIGHT dataset contributes to its improvement.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   *Rigorous Methodology:* The paper carefully details the experimental setup, making it easy to reproduce the results.
    *   *Practical Contribution:*  The integration of baselines into existing toolkits provides immediate practical value to researchers.
    *   *Clearly Written and Well-Organized:* The paper is easy to follow and clearly presents the key findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Limited Exploration of BM25's Query-Side Implementation:* While identifying the "query-side BM25" discrepancy is good, the paper could have explored its theoretical underpinnings and conducted a more in-depth analysis of its performance compared to bag-of-words BM25 across a wider range of datasets.
    *   *Incremental Contribution in Reranking:*  The reranking experiments feel somewhat incremental, simply applying existing LLM rerankers. A more novel approach or a deeper analysis of the interaction between reranking and the specific characteristics of the BRIGHT dataset would have strengthened this section.
    *   *Limited Insight into Dataset Problems:* While the paper points out duplicates, a more in-depth quantification of the effect on evaluation would be stronger.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has a good potential to influence research on reasoning-intensive retrieval and the application of LLMs to information retrieval. The observations on BM25 could lead to future research on adapting lexical retrieval algorithms for the LLM era. The integration into common toolkits ensures a broad reach.

**Overall:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by improving the accessibility and reproducibility of research on the BRIGHT benchmark. The insight on the BRIGHT's nonstandard usage of BM25 is valuable. The primary weakness is that it mostly integrates existing methods, and lacks novel research aspects.
**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### DynaGuard: A Dynamic Guardrail Model With User-Defined Policies
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02563v1)
- **Authors**: Monte Hoover, Vatsal Baherwani, Neel Jain, Khalid Saifullah, Joseph Vincent, Chirag Jain, Melissa Kazemi Rad, C. Bayan Bruss, Ashwinee Panda, Tom Goldstein
- **Abstract**: Guardian models are used to supervise and moderate the outputs of user-facing chatbots, enforcing guardrails and detecting bad behaviors. Standard guardian models like LlamaGuard detect predefined, static categories of harms. We propose dynamic guardian models that evaluate text based on user-defined policies, making them useful for different application domains that are not addressed by standard guardian models. Our dynamic guardian models can be used for fast detection of policy violations or with chain-of-thought reasoning that articulates and justifies the model outputs. Our dynamic guardian models match static models in detection accuracy for static harm categories while identifying violations of free-form policies with accuracy comparable to frontier reasoning models in a fraction of the time.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the DynaGuard paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DynaGuard, a dynamic guardrail model for large language models (LLMs) that allows users to define custom policies for moderating chatbot outputs, moving beyond the static harm categories found in standard guardian models like LlamaGuard. DynaGuard can provide pass/fail judgments on policy violations along with natural language explanations to help LLM agents recover and correct policy-violating behavior. The authors also release DynaBench, a dataset of 40K bespoke guardrail policies paired with simulated chatbot conversations for training and evaluation. The paper demonstrates that DynaGuard achieves comparable accuracy to static models in detecting standard harm categories while significantly improving accuracy in identifying violations of free-form policies. It also shows that training on DynaBench improves a model's ability to act as a guardian, leading to better performance than GPT-4o-mini.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in its approach to dynamic, user-defined policies for LLM guardrails. This is a significant departure from existing guardian models that rely on static categories of harms, which can be limiting in specific applications. Releasing a large-scale dataset like DynaBench is also a valuable contribution to the field.
*   **Significance:** DynaGuard and DynaBench have the potential to significantly impact the development of more flexible and adaptable LLM safety mechanisms. Allowing users to define custom policies opens up new possibilities for applying LLMs in diverse domains with specific requirements, such as medical or financial contexts. The interpretability feature of providing natural language explanations for policy violations could also facilitate improved LLM agent behavior and human understanding of model outputs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a clear and relevant problem in LLM safety.
    *   Introduces a novel approach to dynamic guardrails.
    *   Provides a valuable dataset for training and evaluation (DynaBench).
    *   Demonstrates strong performance compared to existing models.
    *   The interpretability aspect with natural language explanations is a key advantage.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation focuses primarily on automated metrics, and could benefit from more qualitative analyses, including human evaluation of the explanations generated by DynaGuard and their effectiveness in improving LLM agent behavior.
    *   The paper could explore the limitations and potential biases of the DynaBench dataset in more detail.
    *   The paper could discuss how DynaGuard might handle conflicting or ambiguous policies. The method for combining single rule tasks into a policy may have limitations.
    *   The inference time reduction offered by the non-CoT option could be quantified, and more directly compared with CoT based outputs, to establish a clear quantitative trade-off.
*   **Influence:** The paper could influence future research on LLM safety, particularly in developing more adaptable and interpretable guardrail models. DynaBench could become a standard dataset for evaluating the effectiveness of dynamic policy approaches.
*   **Rigorous Rationale for Score:** The score assigned is based on the assessment that the paper makes a tangible contribution to the problem of LLM safety, offering a novel way to dynamically and interpretabl enforce custom policies. Although the quantitative analysis of explanations is not included, the inclusion of these is critical to real-world use cases of guardrail models. DynaBench will be an important resource to the community.

### 2nd Place Solution for CVPR2024 E2E Challenge: End-to-End Autonomous Driving Using Vision Language Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02659v1)
- **Authors**: Zilong Guo, Yi Luo, Long Sha, Dongxu Wang, Panqu Wang, Chenyang Xu, Yi Yang
- **Abstract**: End-to-end autonomous driving has drawn tremendous attention recently. Many works focus on using modular deep neural networks to construct the end-to-end archi-tecture. However, whether using powerful large language models (LLM), especially multi-modality Vision Language Models (VLM) could benefit the end-to-end driving tasks remain a question. In our work, we demonstrate that combining end-to-end architectural design and knowledgeable VLMs yield impressive performance on the driving tasks. It is worth noting that our method only uses a single camera and is the best camera-only solution across the leaderboard, demonstrating the effectiveness of vision-based driving approach and the potential for end-to-end driving tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents the 2nd place solution for the CVPR 2024 End-to-End Driving at Scale Challenge. It uses a vision language model (VLM) for autonomous driving based solely on a single camera input. The architecture consists of encoders for camera input (Vision Transformer), ego history (MLP), and navigation/text prompts (text tokenizer). An LLM (InternLM 4B) autoregressively generates output tokens, which are decoded into a trajectory vector (location, velocity, acceleration) and accompanying text. The model is trained on the nuPlan dataset using L2 loss for trajectory and next-token prediction loss for text. The key claim is that combining end-to-end architecture design with a knowledgeable VLM yields impressive driving performance, achieving top camera-only results in the challenge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the specific architecture and training approach used to integrate VLMs into an end-to-end driving system, *specifically* using only a single camera. The use of VLMs in autonomous driving is not entirely new, as many existing works have explored this avenue [5,14-16,18,20,21,23]. However, the simplification and the focus on minimal sensor input (single camera) is a contribution. The system leverages existing components (InternLM, Vision Transformers) in a novel way.
*   **Significance:** Achieving second place in a competitive challenge like the CVPR 2024 End-to-End Driving at Scale is, by itself, a significant achievement. It demonstrates that even with limited sensor data, VLM-based approaches can achieve high performance in autonomous driving. The paper highlights the advantages of end-to-end architectures: simplified framework, cost efficiency, and strong generalization. The result is significant in showing the potential of vision-based driving and end-to-end approaches.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Performance:** The excellent performance in the challenge provides empirical support for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Simplicity:** The architecture is relatively simple and easy to understand.
    *   **Emphasis on human-like driving:** The paper explicitly mentions the goal of modeling driving like a human does, which is a valuable perspective.
    *   **Single Camera:** A key strength is the camera-only approach, offering a cost-effective solution.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** The paper acknowledges the lack of comprehensive ablation studies due to time constraints. This makes it difficult to fully understand the contribution of each component to the overall performance.
    *   **Single-Frame Input:** Relying on single-frame input is a limitation in a real-world setting, as temporal information is crucial for accurate perception and prediction.
    *   **Open-Loop Evaluation:** Evaluating the model in an open-loop setting may not fully reflect its performance in a closed-loop environment.
    *   **Dependence on existing LLM:** The solution's performance is highly dependent on the pre-trained LLM (InternLM 4B). The authors could provide additional insight on how the VLM architecture is specifically optimized for driving tasks, instead of relying on pre-existing models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by demonstrating the feasibility of using VLMs for autonomous driving with minimal sensor input. It could encourage further research on developing more efficient and robust VLM-based driving systems. However, the limitations mentioned above need to be addressed for the approach to be truly impactful.

**Justification for the Score:**

Given the existing body of work in VLM-based autonomous driving, the paper doesn't introduce entirely novel concepts. However, it demonstrates a working system with strong performance using a single camera input, which simplifies the architecture and has potential for cost-effectiveness. The limitations of single-frame input and limited ablation studies temper the significance of the results. While the performance is promising, the overall novelty relative to other VLM-based driving methods is not exceptionally high.

### Efficient Training-Free Online Routing for High-Volume Multi-LLM Serving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02718v1)
- **Authors**: Fangzhou Wu, Sandeep Silwal
- **Abstract**: Increasing demand for Large Language Models (LLMs) services imposes substantial deployment and computation costs on providers. LLM routing offers a cost-efficient solution by directing queries to the optimal LLM based on model and query features. However, existing works primarily focus on offline scenarios and struggle to adapt to online settings with high query volume and constrained token budgets. In this work, we introduce the first training-free algorithm for online routing scenarios. Our algorithm leverages approximate nearest neighbor search to efficiently estimate query features and performs a one-time optimization over a small set of initial queries to learn a routing strategy that guides future routing. We provide theoretical guarantees demonstrating that our algorithm achieves a competitive ratio of $1 - o(1)$ under natural assumptions, which is further validated by extensive experiments across 3 benchmark datasets and 8 baselines, showing an average improvement of 3.55$\times$ in overall performance, 1.85$\times$ in cost efficiency, and nearly 4.25$\times$ in throughput.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel training-free online routing algorithm for large language model (LLM) serving, designed to maximize query performance under token budget constraints. The algorithm leverages approximate nearest neighbor search (ANNS) to efficiently estimate query features based on a historical dataset. It then performs a one-time optimization on a small subset of initial queries to learn routing weights that guide future decisions. The approach includes theoretical guarantees, demonstrating a competitive ratio of 1 - o(1) under mild assumptions, and extensive experimental validation across three benchmark datasets and eight baselines. The experiments show significant improvements in overall performance, cost efficiency, and throughput compared to existing methods. A key feature is the algorithm's adaptivity to dynamic LLM deployment configurations and minimal deployment overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution is its training-free approach to online LLM routing. Previous works have often relied on training complex models or computationally expensive methods, creating bottlenecks for high-volume, low-latency scenarios. The online nature of the algorithm, with theoretical guarantees, distinguishes it from prior offline approaches. Furthermore, the adaptivity to dynamic LLM deployments without retraining is a significant advancement. The combination of ANNS with a one-time optimization step to derive routing weights is also a notable and novel element. The inclusion of a control parameter in the MILP formulation to enhance generalizability is a subtle but potentially impactful addition. While some components (ANNS, MILP) have been used in related areas, their combination within this specific context and problem setting is novel.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in addressing a practical problem in LLM serving: optimizing resource utilization under budget constraints while maintaining high throughput and low latency. The algorithm's ability to adapt to dynamic LLM deployments and its cost-effectiveness makes it particularly valuable for real-world applications. By minimizing training overhead and computational complexity, the approach opens the door for more scalable and efficient LLM serving systems. The strong experimental results, showcasing significant improvements over various baselines across multiple benchmarks, further reinforces the practical relevance of the work. The theoretical guarantees provide a degree of confidence in the algorithm's performance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Training-Free Approach:** Eliminates the need for computationally intensive and time-consuming model training and retraining, making it highly adaptable to changing environments.
    *   **Online Operation:** Enables efficient routing decisions with low latency, suitable for high-volume query streams.
    *   **Theoretical Guarantees:** Provides formal guarantees on the algorithm's performance, ensuring competitive routing quality.
    *   **Adaptivity:** Designed to handle dynamic LLM deployments without requiring retraining.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** Demonstrates significant performance gains compared to existing baselines across diverse benchmarks.
    *   **Computational Scalability**: ANNS reduces computational overhead.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Assumptions:** The theoretical guarantees rely on specific assumptions regarding query arrival order and feature estimation. While these assumptions are arguably mild, their violation could impact the algorithm's performance. Further analysis of the sensitivity to these assumptions would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Historical Data Dependency:** The algorithm relies on a historical dataset for feature estimation. The quality and diversity of this dataset can significantly impact the accuracy of the routing decisions. The sensitivity to the distribution shift between the historical data and incoming query stream requires further exploration.
    *   **Limited Factor Focus:** The algorithm currently focuses primarily on performance and cost, without explicit consideration of other potentially relevant factors in LLM routing, such as privacy or fairness. The method section details how to address these constraints through additional dual variables.
    *   **Generalizability:** The performance and effectiveness of the proposed solution might be dependent on the specific LLM models and tasks selected for evaluation. Further evaluation with different LLM families and diverse task sets is warranted to assess the generalizability of the findings.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has certain limitations (particularly the reliance on assumptions and historical data), its strengths outweigh these weaknesses. The proposed algorithm addresses a crucial and practical problem in LLM serving, offers a novel and efficient solution, provides theoretical guarantees, and demonstrates strong experimental results. The adaptivity and reduced overhead are significant advancements over previous approaches. This method offers a viable and scalable solution to improve the quality of service in LLM-serving systems, especially under resource constraints. The algorithm is also mathematically sound.

### Deep Research is the New Analytics System: Towards Building the Runtime for AI-Driven Analytics
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02751v1)
- **Authors**: Matthew Russo, Tim Kraska
- **Abstract**: With advances in large language models (LLMs), researchers are creating new systems that can perform AI-driven analytics over large unstructured datasets. Recent work has explored executing such analytics queries using semantic operators -- a declarative set of AI-powered data transformations with natural language specifications. However, even when optimized, these operators can be expensive to execute on millions of records and their iterator execution semantics make them ill-suited for interactive data analytics tasks. In another line of work, Deep Research systems have demonstrated an ability to answer natural language question(s) over large datasets. These systems use one or more LLM agent(s) to plan their execution, process the dataset(s), and iteratively refine their answer. However, these systems do not explicitly optimize their query plans which can lead to poor plan execution. In order for AI-driven analytics to excel, we need a runtime which combines the optimized execution of semantic operators with the flexibility and more dynamic execution of Deep Research systems. As a first step towards this vision, we build a prototype which enables Deep Research agents to write and execute optimized semantic operator programs. We evaluate our prototype and demonstrate that it can outperform a handcrafted semantic operator program and open Deep Research systems on two basic queries. Compared to a standard open Deep Research agent, our prototype achieves up to 1.95x better F1-score. Furthermore, even if we give the agent access to semantic operators as tools, our prototype still achieves cost and runtime savings of up to 76.8% and 72.7% thanks to its optimized execution.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the need for a unified runtime for AI-driven analytics that combines the strengths of Deep Research systems (flexibility, dynamic execution) and semantic operator systems (optimized execution).  The authors build a prototype extending the Palimpzest framework with `compute` and `search` operators physically implemented using AI agents capable of planning and utilizing semantic operator programs. A new `Context` abstraction allows more dynamic data access (indexing, tools) and stores natural language descriptions of datasets. The prototype is evaluated on Kramabench and Enron email dataset queries, demonstrating improved accuracy and efficiency compared to handcrafted semantic operator programs and open Deep Research systems (namely, SmolAgents). The system leverages a ContextManager to index and reuse materialized contexts for optimization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents an interesting and timely approach to improving AI-driven analytics. The central idea of combining the flexibility of Deep Research agents with the optimized execution of semantic operators is well-motivated, addressing the limitations of each approach in isolation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a clear problem:** The paper clearly identifies a critical need in the area of AI-driven analytics: how to combine the reasoning capabilities of LLM-based agents with the performance benefits of optimized data transformations.
    *   **Well-defined prototype:** The prototype implementation builds on the existing Palimpzest framework, providing a concrete demonstration of the proposed approach. The introduction of `compute`, `search`, and the `Context` abstraction seems logical and practical.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The evaluation, while limited to two queries, provides evidence that the prototype can outperform both handcrafted semantic operator programs and open Deep Research systems on specific tasks. The performance gains (better F1 score, cost/runtime savings) are substantial in the examples provided.
    *   **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed approach, the prototype architecture, and the experimental results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited novelty in individual components:** While the *combination* is novel, the individual components (semantic operators, Deep Research agents, query optimization) are well-established areas.  The novelty primarily lies in orchestrating these components in a particular way.
    *   **Scalability concerns:** The paper does not sufficiently address scalability. The evaluation is limited to a relatively small dataset.  How well the approach scales to larger, more complex datasets and queries remains an open question.
    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The experiments are limited to two example queries and a relatively basic document processing task. The generalizability of the results is not firmly established. A more comprehensive benchmark suite would be valuable. The current experiments are not a like-for-like comparision as CodeAgent+ uses unoptimized semantic operators while the Prototype uses optimized semantic operators. Thus, it's unclear whether improvements are a result of the optimizer or the agent-based planning. The paper should've included another baseline: a CodeAgent+ with *optimized* semantic operators.
    *   **Cost analysis:** The "cost" metric is vaguely defined. It's not clear what it captures beyond runtime, and a breakdown of the cost (e.g., API calls, model usage) would be useful. The impact of expensive LLM calls isn't fully explored.
    *   **Optimization details:** The paper mentions logical and physical optimizations but doesn't delve deeply into them. A more detailed discussion of the implemented (and planned) optimization techniques would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Context maintenance:** The context maintenance section is brief. It is unclear how Contexts are indexed and how the optimizer determines the "similarity" between contexts to reuse them.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper's potential significance lies in its potential to bridge the gap between the flexibility of Deep Research and the performance of optimized data processing. If the approach can be scaled and generalized, it could lead to more efficient and effective AI-driven analytics systems. However, the limitations outlined above temper its potential impact.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses, a score of **6** is assigned. The paper addresses a relevant problem, offers a reasonable solution, and provides initial experimental support. However, the limited scope of the evaluation, scalability concerns, and lack of deep dives into the optimization strategies and context maintenance prevent it from being considered a truly groundbreaking or highly impactful contribution at this stage. A more comprehensive evaluation and further development of the prototype are needed to fully realize its potential.

### Do LLM Modules Generalize? A Study on Motion Generation for Autonomous Driving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02754v1)
- **Authors**: Mingyi Wang, Jingke Wang, Tengju Ye, Junbo Chen, Kaicheng Yu
- **Abstract**: Recent breakthroughs in large language models (LLMs) have not only advanced natural language processing but also inspired their application in domains with structurally similar problems--most notably, autonomous driving motion generation. Both domains involve autoregressive sequence modeling, token-based representations, and context-aware decision making, making the transfer of LLM components a natural and increasingly common practice. However, despite promising early attempts, a systematic understanding of which LLM modules are truly transferable remains lacking. In this paper, we present a comprehensive evaluation of five key LLM modules--tokenizer design, positional embedding, pre-training paradigms, post-training strategies, and test-time computation--within the context of motion generation for autonomous driving. Through extensive experiments on the Waymo Sim Agents benchmark, we demonstrate that, when appropriately adapted, these modules can significantly improve performance for autonomous driving motion generation. In addition, we identify which techniques can be effectively transferred, analyze the potential reasons for the failure of others, and discuss the specific adaptations needed for autonomous driving scenarios. We evaluate our method on the Sim Agents task and achieve competitive results.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Do LLM Modules Generalize? A Study on Motion Generation for Autonomous Driving" investigates the transferability of five key modules from Large Language Models (LLMs) to the task of motion generation in autonomous driving. These modules include tokenizer design, positional embedding, pre-training paradigms, post-training strategies, and test-time computation. The authors conduct extensive experiments on the Waymo Sim Agents benchmark to assess the effectiveness of adapting these LLM modules to autonomous driving. They find that while some modules, like pre-training and test-time computing strategies, can be readily transferred and improve performance, others, such as tokenizer design and positional embeddings, require domain-specific adaptations.  They demonstrate that appropriately adapted LLM modules can significantly improve autonomous driving motion generation and achieve competitive results on the Waymo Sim Agents benchmark.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic and comprehensive approach to evaluating the transferability of LLM modules to autonomous driving motion generation. While previous works have explored individual module transfers, this paper provides a holistic perspective, examining the interdependencies and adaptations required for multiple modules. The identification of specific adaptation strategies for positional embeddings (Global-DROPE) and post-training (GRPO) contributes meaningfully to the field.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to accelerate advancements in autonomous driving by leveraging the extensive research and development efforts in LLMs. Identifying which LLM techniques are readily transferable and, more importantly, how to adapt those that are not, provides valuable guidance to researchers and practitioners. Demonstrating competitive performance on the Waymo Sim Agents benchmark further validates the practicality and impact of the proposed adaptations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper's strength lies in its systematic evaluation of multiple LLM modules within a unified framework.
    *   **Domain-Specific Adaptations:** The proposed Global-DROPE and GRPO adaptations address critical challenges in applying LLM techniques to autonomous driving.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The extensive experiments on the Waymo Sim Agents benchmark provide strong empirical evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Clear Analysis:**  The authors clearly articulate the reasons for the success and failure of different module transfers, providing valuable insights into the underlying mechanisms.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The study is primarily focused on motion generation and doesn't explore the application of LLMs to other aspects of autonomous driving, such as perception or decision-making.

    *   **Computational Resources:** The experiments are limited by data and computational resources, preventing a more in-depth study of scaling behavior with larger models.

    *   **Benchmark Limitations:** The paper acknowledges limitations of the Waymo Sim Agents benchmark's likelihood-based evaluation, which can penalize safe behavior. The acknowledgement is well done but highlights a current weakness within that area of study.

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The gains, while competitive, can be viewed as incremental rather than transformative. However, this aligns with the nature of research that builds on existing LLMs and AD capabilities.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper is likely to influence future research in autonomous driving by highlighting the potential of LLM-inspired techniques and guiding the development of domain-specific adaptations.
    *   The proposed Global-DROPE and GRPO methods could become standard techniques for positional embedding and post-training in motion generation models.
    *   The systematic evaluation framework could serve as a template for future studies on module transfer in autonomous driving.

* **Justification**
The paper provides a comprehensive exploration of the effectiveness of directly and indirectly transferring knowledge and approaches from Large Language Models (LLMs) to Autonomous Driving. While the performance gains are not leaps and bounds above existing approaches, and therefore not groundbreaking, the study is systematic, well-reasoned, and methodically explores several key LLM components within the AD context. The adaptations are crucial to the successful application of LLM modules to AD, and represent novel developments. The findings are likely to strongly influence a key research area by guiding future research and development in adapting LLMs for autonomous driving.

### Optimizing Geometry Problem Sets for Skill Development
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02758v1)
- **Authors**: Michael Bouzinier, Sergey Trifonov
- **Abstract**: This article describes an ontology and methodology for annotating and organizing Euclidean Geometry problems, developed in the early 1990s and implemented as a software tool. While the majority of this work -- including the ontology and solution graph paradigm -- was completed over thirty years ago, we argue that it has renewed relevance in the context of modern artificial intelligence. In particular, we explore the hypothesis that this established framework can facilitate automated solution validation and feedback when paired with contemporary large language models, thereby supporting teachers and self-learners in geometry education. We document the original architecture and its enduring value, and outline pathways for bridging historical educational resources with next-generation AI techniques.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper discusses an ontology and methodology developed in the early 1990s for annotating and organizing Euclidean geometry problems. This system was implemented as a software tool aimed at helping teachers create problem sets for skill development. The authors argue that this historical work has renewed relevance in the context of modern artificial intelligence, particularly in facilitating automated solution validation and feedback when combined with large language models (LLMs). They describe the original architecture, its enduring value, and potential pathways for integrating it with next-generation AI techniques to improve geometry education.  The paper highlights the use of solution graphs (directed acyclic graphs) to represent solutions and emphasize the integration of AI-driven annotation with current proof validation tools.  The paper concludes by discussing interactive input modes for proof solutions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core ontology and methodology are not novel in themselves, as they were developed in the 1990s. The novelty lies in the re-evaluation and application of this existing framework to modern AI techniques. The paper suggests that a well-structured, historically proven approach can enhance and streamline current efforts in automated solution validation. The idea to combine classic educational resources with modern AI could be considered novel, however this is something researchers in the field are likely already considering.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is debatable. The authors present a retrospective view, suggesting how their earlier work could be valuable now. There's a real potential to reduce teachers' workloads and enhance learning, and the paper proposes a path forward. However, it lacks concrete results or proof-of-concept implementations demonstrating the effectiveness of integrating the ontology with LLMs. The idea is intriguing, but the paper needs validation. The description of the ontology itself feels somewhat high-level and not sufficiently detailed to allow immediate implementation. Also, the application of ontologies in education is not a new idea.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly outlines the components of the original system.
    *   It provides a rationale for why Euclidean geometry, despite being less prominent, remains a valuable subject for developing logical reasoning skills.
    *   The paper highlights key milestones, like computer-aided validation and interactive feedback for self-learners.
    *   It suggests concrete strategies for combining solution graphs with proof validation tools and leveraging AI for annotation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper is largely theoretical. It discusses potential benefits without providing empirical evidence.
    *   The level of detail regarding the ontology's implementation is not sufficient.
    *   The claims about the system's ability to significantly aid in automating solution validation lack demonstrable support. There are no experiments to justify the claims.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more thorough comparison with existing ontologies and educational resources in geometry, demonstrating the unique value proposition of the proposed approach.
*   The field of AI and education is rapidly evolving, and the paper would benefit from showing a more detailed knowledge of what AI systems already provide, to then show a clear advantage of their approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research if it motivates the development of a working prototype that validates the proposed approach. It could also serve as a historical reference point for understanding the evolution of educational tools in geometry.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable historical perspective and a potential application of older techniques. The recontextualization within the realm of modern AI is somewhat novel. However, the absence of experimental results, the lack of detailed implementation specifications for the ontology, and the fact that other groups are already considering similar approaches limits the paper's overall significance. Furthermore, while the ideas are clearly presented, the justification for why this *specific* ontology is superior to other approaches or why it addresses a critical gap in existing systems isn't strong enough.

- **Score**: 5/10

### PixFoundation 2.0: Do Video Multi-Modal LLMs Use Motion in Visual Grounding?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02807v1)
- **Authors**: Mennatullah Siam
- **Abstract**: Multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) have shown impressive generalization across tasks using images and text modalities. While their extension to video has enabled tasks such as video question answering and video captioning, their pixel-level visual grounding abilities are less studied. In this work, we raise the pertinent question of whether motion is used in pixel-level visual grounding and whether video MLLMs can segment objects based on natural language expressions describing their motion patterns. We identify the shortcomings in the current benchmarks, where we show that a single frame can often suffice for capturing the motion referring expression without any temporal reasoning. To address this, we introduce four motion-centric probing techniques, particularly designed for the visual grounding task, to study video MLLMs' ability to identify true motion from a fake one and their ability to grasp the motion order. Consequently, we provide a motion-centric benchmark, MoCentric-Bench. It ensures that video MLLMs are evaluated towards leveraging the interaction between motion and language rather than being dominated by static appearance cues emphasized in existing visual grounding datasets. We further establish strong single-image baselines that are on par with or outperform prior methods. Finally, we explore simple motion-centric adaptation techniques that provide state-of-the-art performance on our MoCentric-Bench. Our motion-centric benchmark, evaluation and findings challenge future models to improve dense spatiotemporal grounding and pixel-level understanding within videos. Code and datasets will be made publicly available at https://github.com/MSiam/PixFoundation-2.0.git.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PixFoundation 2.0: Do Video Multi-Modal LLMs Use Motion in Visual Grounding?"

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether video Multi-Modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) effectively use motion information for pixel-level visual grounding.  The authors argue that existing benchmarks for video grounding are insufficient because models can often solve the tasks using static cues from single frames, neglecting temporal reasoning.  To address this, they introduce four motion-centric probing techniques and a corresponding benchmark called MoCentric-Bench. This benchmark specifically tests a video MLLM's ability to distinguish between true and fake motion (created by repeating a single frame) and to understand the order of motion events (forward vs. reverse videos). The paper then presents single-image baselines, demonstrates their surprisingly strong performance on standard datasets (outperforming prior methods), and shows that current video MLLMs struggle on the proposed MoCentric-Bench.  Finally, the authors explore simple motion-centric adaptation techniques (LoRA finetuning of the vision encoder) and show that this can improve performance.  The code and datasets are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in identifying a significant weakness in current video MLLM benchmarks related to motion understanding. The MoCentric-Bench and the associated probing techniques are a valuable contribution. Specifically, the idea of using static keyframes to "fool" the models and testing order understanding is a strong and novel approach. Showing the weakness in temporal understanding within dense pixel-level tasks is impactful. The exploration of using LoRA to finetune a vision encoder for motion-centric tasks contributes to the field.

*   **Significance:** The work has moderate significance. By highlighting that current video MLLMs often rely on static cues, the paper challenges the community to develop models that truly leverage temporal information for fine-grained video understanding.  The MoCentric-Bench offers a practical tool for evaluating and driving progress in this direction.  However, the paper's impact is limited by the relatively simple adaptation techniques explored (LoRA with synthetic data). While it shows a promising direction, it doesn't provide a fundamental breakthrough in incorporating motion understanding into MLLM architectures.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem statement and motivation.
    *   Clearly articulated and implemented probing techniques.
    *   MoCentric-Bench provides a valuable resource for the community.
    *   Thorough experiments that expose the limitations of existing models.
    *   Public availability of code and datasets promotes reproducibility and further research.
    *   Rigorous empirical analysis of shortcomings of Video MLLMs.
    *   Highlighting the importance of motion in pixel-level tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The adaptation techniques are relatively simple and don't address the core architectural challenges of incorporating motion.
    *   The improvement from finetuning, while noticeable, isn't groundbreaking, suggesting the need for more sophisticated approaches.
