    *   The synthetic data generation approach could be expanded on and potentially be improved with more sophisticated strategies that involve modeling the motion in a structured fashion.
    *   Limited qualitative results in Figure 5 and 4 to showcase how the various adaptations enable the model to capture motion in videos.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely influence the development and evaluation of future video MLLMs.  The MoCentric-Bench could become a standard evaluation dataset. The findings will encourage researchers to focus on developing models that can genuinely leverage temporal information and lead to more robust and reliable video grounding.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:** The paper introduces a novel and well-designed benchmark that effectively exposes the limitations of current video MLLMs in understanding motion for visual grounding. The probing techniques are sound, and the experimental results are convincing. However, the impact is somewhat limited by the incremental nature of the adaptation techniques, which primarily involve existing finetuning approaches. While important, those don't necessarily propose solutions to improve MLLM understanding of motion inherently. Also, the synthetic data approach is relatively straightforward and lacks the complexity needed for more impactful advancements in the field. Therefore, while the paper makes a valuable contribution by identifying a crucial weakness and providing a useful benchmark, it doesn't offer a transformative solution, justifying a score below a 7.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Unlearning That Lasts: Utility-Preserving, Robust, and Almost Irreversible Forgetting in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02820v1)
- **Authors**: Naman Deep Singh, Maximilian Müller, Francesco Croce, Matthias Hein
- **Abstract**: Unlearning in large language models (LLMs) involves precisely removing specific information from a pre-trained model. This is crucial to ensure safety of LLMs by deleting private data or harmful knowledge acquired during pre-training. However, existing unlearning methods often fall short when subjected to thorough evaluation. To overcome this, we introduce JensUn, where we leverage the Jensen-Shannon Divergence as the training objective for both forget and retain sets for more stable and effective unlearning dynamics compared to commonly used loss functions. In extensive experiments, JensUn achieves better forget-utility trade-off than competing methods, and even demonstrates strong resilience to benign relearning. Additionally, for a precise unlearning evaluation, we introduce LKF, a curated dataset of lesser-known facts that provides a realistic unlearning scenario. Finally, to comprehensively test unlearning methods, we propose (i) employing an LLM as semantic judge instead of the standard ROUGE score, and (ii) using worst-case unlearning evaluation over various paraphrases and input formats. Our improved evaluation framework reveals that many existing methods are less effective than previously thought.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Unlearning That Lasts: Utility-Preserving, Robust, and Almost Irreversible Forgetting in LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the critical issue of unlearning in Large Language Models (LLMs), focusing on achieving robust and practically irreversible forgetting while preserving the model's utility.  It identifies shortcomings in existing unlearning methods related to evaluation and resilience. To overcome these problems, the paper introduces several key contributions:

1.  **JensUn:** A novel unlearning method based on the Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) as a training objective. This aims for more stable and effective unlearning dynamics than methods using cross-entropy losses.
2.  **LKF Dataset:** A new, high-quality unlearning dataset called Lesser Known Facts (LKF), specifically designed to assess the removal of factual knowledge. LKF focuses on niche historical topics to provide a realistic unlearning scenario.
3.  **Improved Evaluation Framework:**  The paper advocates for replacing the standard ROUGE score with an LLM as a "semantic judge" for factual correctness. Further enhancements include using "worst-case" unlearning evaluation over paraphrases and in-context examples to test robustness and prevent superficial suppression of knowledge.
4.  **Robustness to Relearning:** The paper also investigates the ability of unlearned models to resist "benign relearning" (re-acquiring forgotten information through fine-tuning on unrelated data), and finds that JensUn has strong resilience against it.

The authors demonstrate JensUn's superior forget-utility trade-off compared to competing methods through extensive experiments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses an extremely important problem in the field of LLMs: ensuring that models can truly and reliably forget sensitive or harmful information.  The weaknesses of existing methods in this area have serious implications for safety and ethical deployment of LLMs.  The paper's contributions are significant in addressing this gap.

*   **Novelty:**  The use of the Jensen-Shannon Divergence for unlearning *is* novel. While JSD has been used in other contexts (GAN training, noisy labels), this paper successfully applies it to the specific challenges of LLM unlearning. The LKF dataset is also a valuable contribution, as it offers a new benchmark better suited for rigorous factual unlearning evaluation than existing datasets. The evaluation approach is well-thought-out, with the emphasis on semantic judgment by an LLM and worst-case examples representing important steps toward creating practical unlearning methods.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is high. The improved unlearning method, robust evaluation framework, and the LKF dataset pave the way for more reliable unlearning in LLMs. This has direct implications for:

    *   **Privacy:**  Protecting sensitive user data used in training.
    *   **Safety:**  Removing harmful or biased knowledge from LLMs.
    *   **Compliance:**  Meeting regulatory requirements for data handling.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and motivation.
    *   Well-defined contributions and methodology.
    *   Thorough and comprehensive experimental evaluation.
    *   Strong results demonstrating the effectiveness of JensUn.
    *   The introduction of LKF is invaluable for future research in this area.
    * The paper is generally clearly written and well-organized.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    * The experimental setup could be limited in terms of how it compares to true real world use cases. The LKF dataset is constructed from niche historical examples, whereas often the need to be forgotten concepts are more commonplace.
    *The computation cost of generating paraphrases is not thoroughly considered. While the evaluations offer improvements, often a large computational cost is added to do so.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the direction of research in LLM unlearning. The proposed method and evaluation framework offer concrete improvements over existing approaches, and the LKF dataset provides a valuable resource for the community. Future work is likely to build upon these contributions to develop even more robust and practically applicable unlearning techniques. The emphasis on robustness against relearning is also a particularly important contribution.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to LLM unlearning by addressing limitations in existing methods and evaluation protocols. The introduction of JensUn provides a strong method for unlearning, and with the LKF data set the field will benefit greatly. The paper falls short of being perfect in that it may over-optimize for a niche task. The computational burden associated with paraphrasing could also be restrictive, as may restrict scalability. Overall, the contributions made in method and evaluation is strong and merits a score of 8.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Clustering Discourses: Racial Biases in Short Stories about Women Generated by Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02834v1)
- **Authors**: Gustavo Bonil, João Gondim, Marina dos Santos, Simone Hashiguti, Helena Maia, Nadia Silva, Helio Pedrini, Sandra Avila
- **Abstract**: This study investigates how large language models, in particular LLaMA 3.2-3B, construct narratives about Black and white women in short stories generated in Portuguese. From 2100 texts, we applied computational methods to group semantically similar stories, allowing a selection for qualitative analysis. Three main discursive representations emerge: social overcoming, ancestral mythification and subjective self-realization. The analysis uncovers how grammatically coherent, seemingly neutral texts materialize a crystallized, colonially structured framing of the female body, reinforcing historical inequalities. The study proposes an integrated approach, that combines machine learning techniques with qualitative, manual discourse analysis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates racial biases in short stories generated by the LLAMA 3.2-3B language model, focusing on narratives about Black and white women in Portuguese. It employs a mixed-methods approach, combining computational clustering of story embeddings with qualitative discourse analysis. The study identifies three dominant discursive representations: social overcoming, ancestral mythification, and subjective self-realization. The analysis reveals that seemingly neutral texts can reinforce historical inequalities by perpetuating crystallized, colonially-structured framings of the female body based on race. The paper argues for the importance of human expertise, specifically from discourse analysts, in mitigating biases present in LLMs, particularly concerning language's role in sustaining social hierarchies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper offers a solid contribution to the growing body of work examining biases in large language models. Its strengths lie in:

*   **Methodological Rigor:** The use of a mixed-methods approach—clustering embeddings followed by close qualitative reading—allows for both large-scale pattern identification and nuanced interpretation. Applying discourse analysis to outputs derived from LLMs is a valuable and necessary perspective.
*   **Focus on Portuguese Language:** The research fills a gap in the literature by specifically examining bias in Portuguese-language LLM outputs. This is important, as biases can be language-specific and require dedicated investigation in different cultural contexts.
*   **Clear Identification of Discursive Patterns:** The paper persuasively identifies distinct narrative patterns associated with Black and white women. These patterns are well-supported by textual evidence and connected to broader societal discourses.
*   **Addressing Systemic Inequality:** The authors emphasize that LLM bias is not merely a technical flaw but a reflection and amplification of systemic social inequality. This positions the work within a broader critical framework.

However, there are areas for potential improvement:

*   **Limited Novelty of Bias Findings:** The general finding that LLMs exhibit racial biases is not entirely novel. The paper builds on existing research in this area. While the application to Portuguese and the specific discursive patterns identified are new, the core finding is incremental.
*   **Generalizability of Findings:** The study focuses on a single LLM (LLAMA 3.2-3B) and a specific prompt format. It is not clear how generalizable the identified biases are to other models or different prompts. The limited scope affects the impact of the paper.
*   **Depth of Discourse Analysis:** While the analysis identifies key themes and patterns, the analysis could offer deeper insight into the intricacies of how language concretely constructs racialized representations. For example, expanding on the theoretical framework used to classify and contextualize these findings could significantly improve the impact.
*   **Lack of Countermeasures:** The paper effectively identifies and analyzes the biases, but it does not offer any concrete solutions or countermeasures for mitigating these biases in LLMs. Addressing this aspect, by suggesting a pipeline or specific strategies, would increase the study’s practical impact.

In summary, the paper offers a valuable contribution through its language-specific analysis, rigorous methodology, and critical framing of LLM bias. However, the incremental nature of the findings regarding bias itself and the limitations in generalizability and depth of discourse analysis, reduces its overall impact.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:**

A score of 6 reflects the paper's methodological strengths and contributions to a less-explored language context. However, it also acknowledges the lack of groundbreaking novelty and the scope of analysis that restricts the overall impact and the lack of strategies to mitigate the biases it reveals. It's a solid piece of research, but it doesn't significantly shift the direction of the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Towards Reasoning for PDE Foundation Models: A Reward-Model-Driven Inference-Time-Scaling Algorithm
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02846v1)
- **Authors**: Siddharth Mansingh, James Amarel, Ragib Arnab, Arvind Mohan, Kamaljeet Singh, Gerd J. Kunde, Nicolas Hengartner, Benjamin Migliori, Emily Casleton, Nathan A. Debarledeben, Ayan Biswas, Diane Oyen, Earl Lawrence
- **Abstract**: Partial Differential Equations (PDEs) are the bedrock for modern computational sciences and engineering, and inherently computationally expensive. While PDE foundation models have shown much promise for simulating such complex spatio-temporal phenomena, existing models remain constrained by the pretraining datasets and struggle with auto-regressive rollout performance, especially in out-of-distribution (OOD) cases. Furthermore, they have significant compute and training data requirements which hamper their use in many critical applications. Inspired by recent advances in ``thinking" strategies used in large language models (LLMs), we introduce the first test-time computing (TTC) strategy for PDEs that utilizes computational resources during inference to achieve more accurate predictions with fewer training samples and smaller models. We accomplish this with two types of reward models that evaluate predictions of a stochastic based model for spatio-temporal consistency. We demonstrate this method on compressible Euler-equation simulations from the PDEGym benchmark and show that TTC captures improved predictions relative to standard non-adaptive auto-regressive inference. This TTC framework marks a foundational step towards more advanced reasoning algorithms or PDE modeling, inluding building reinforcement-learning-based approaches, potentially transforming computational workflows in physics and engineering.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel test-time computation (TTC) approach for improving the accuracy of PDE foundation models.  Inspired by reasoning strategies used in large language models (LLMs), TTC dynamically allocates computational resources during inference to evaluate and select among multiple candidate solutions. The approach leverages two types of reward models: analytical reward models (ARMs), based on physical constraints, and learned process reward models (PRMs), trained to assess spatio-temporal consistency. The method is demonstrated on compressible Euler-equation simulations from the PDEGym benchmark. The results show that TTC enhances prediction accuracy compared to standard autoregressive inference, especially when fine-tuning data is limited and model sizes are reduced.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The idea of adapting "reasoning" techniques from LLMs to PDE foundation models is a genuinely novel direction.  Specifically, the concept of inference-time computation tailored for PDEs, using reward models to guide the selection of better solutions during rollout, is a fresh contribution. The introduction of *both* analytical and learned reward models provides an interesting comparative analysis. The application of beam-search style sampling in this context is innovative.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in PDE foundation models: the difficulty in achieving accurate long-term predictions, particularly in out-of-distribution scenarios, and the dependence on extensive fine-tuning datasets. TTC provides a potentially impactful solution by increasing sample efficiency and permitting the use of smaller, computationally less demanding models. This could significantly broaden the applicability of PDE foundation models in resource-constrained settings or when downstream data is scarce. Demonstrating this on a non-trivial benchmark like compressible Euler equations significantly strengthens the claim. The method's potential to generalize scientific modeling and influence physics and engineering computational workflows is also quite notable. The analysis of improving mass and energy conservation is an added benefit.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Statement:**  The paper clearly articulates the challenges it addresses and provides a well-defined problem statement.
    * **Novel Approach:**  The TTC framework and its adaptation from LLM reasoning strategies are innovative.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The method is rigorously evaluated on a relevant PDE benchmark (PDEGym), with comparisons against baselines.  The use of multiple datasets (pretraining and downstream) and different reward models strengthens the analysis.
    * **Practical Implications:** The demonstration of improved sample efficiency and the ability to use smaller models have direct practical implications.
    * **Reproducibility:** The inclusion of initial conditions and base implementations makes reproducibility easier.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Theoretical Analysis:**  The paper could benefit from a deeper theoretical analysis of why TTC works. While empirical results are strong, a more formal understanding of the method's convergence properties or the factors influencing the effectiveness of the reward models would be valuable.
    * **Reward Model Selection:**  While the paper explores both analytical and learned reward models, the process of choosing or designing an appropriate reward function for a given PDE problem is not fully addressed. More guidance on this aspect would enhance the practicality of the approach.
    * **Beam Search Limitations:** The beam search is relatively simple, and does not employ more sophisticated planning strategies.
    * **Limited Ablation Studies:** More ablation studies on the impact of individual components of the TTC framework would further refine the understanding of its behavior. For example, a study on the effect of dropout rate during inference would be valuable. The tradeoff between the accuracy of the base model and the benefits of TTC would have been very informative.
    * **Lack of comparison to predictor-corrector algorithms:** The introduction mentions how predictor-corrector algorithms are different but lacks a comparison.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to stimulate further research in several directions:
    * **Adaptive Reasoning:**  Exploring more advanced reasoning algorithms (e.g., reinforcement learning-based approaches) for PDE modeling.
    * **Reward Function Design:** Developing more robust and generalizable reward functions for assessing PDE solutions.
    * **Integration with Physics-Informed Machine Learning:** Combining TTC with existing physics-informed machine learning techniques.
    * **Application to Other Scientific Domains:** Adapting TTC to other scientific domains where data-driven modeling of complex systems is prevalent.

Overall, the paper presents a compelling and significant contribution to the field of PDE foundation models. While some aspects could be strengthened, the novelty of the approach, the thorough evaluation, and the potential for practical impact justify a high score.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Multi-Scale Deep Learning for Colon Histopathology: A Hybrid Graph-Transformer Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02851v1)
- **Authors**: Sadra Saremi, Amirhossein Ahmadkhan Kordbacheh
- **Abstract**: Colon cancer also known as Colorectal cancer, is one of the most malignant types of cancer worldwide. Early-stage detection of colon cancer is highly crucial to prevent its deterioration. This research presents a hybrid multi-scale deep learning architecture that synergizes capsule networks, graph attention mechanisms, transformer modules, and residual learning to advance colon cancer classification on the Lung and Colon Cancer Histopathological Image Dataset (LC25000) dataset. The proposed model in this paper utilizes the HG-TNet model that introduces a hybrid architecture that joins strength points in transformers and convolutional neural networks to capture multi-scale features in histopathological images. Mainly, a transformer branch extracts global contextual bonds by partitioning the image into patches by convolution-based patch embedding and then processing these patches through a transformer encoder. Analogously, a dedicated CNN branch captures fine-grained, local details through successive Incorporation these diverse features, combined with a self-supervised rotation prediction objective, produce a robust diagnostic representation that surpasses standard architectures in performance. Results show better performance not only in accuracy or loss function but also in these algorithms by utilizing capsule networks to preserve spatial orders and realize how each element individually combines and forms whole structures.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a hybrid multi-scale deep learning architecture called HG-TNet for colon cancer classification using histopathological images from the LC25000 dataset. HG-TNet combines capsule networks, graph attention mechanisms, transformer modules, and residual learning.  It has a transformer branch to extract global contextual bonds, a CNN branch to capture fine-grained local details, and then fuses these features. The model is trained with a self-supervised rotation prediction objective to improve robustness. The results demonstrate good performance, surpassing standard architectures in accuracy and loss function, by preserving spatial order and realizing element combinations.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

This paper attempts to address the challenging problem of colon cancer classification by integrating different deep learning techniques into a single hybrid model. It aims to leverage the strengths of transformers (global context), CNNs (local features), and graph attention mechanisms (relationships between features) for a more robust and accurate classification.

**Strengths:**

*   **Hybrid Approach:** Combining transformers, CNNs, and graph attention networks is potentially beneficial, addressing different aspects of the image data.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes standard evaluation metrics (accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, ROC curves), providing a good understanding of the model's performance.
*   **Dataset:**  Utilizes a well-established and publicly available dataset (LC25000) allowing for reproducibility and comparisons.
*   **Clear Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of individual architectures and provides a rationale for why a hybrid approach is expected to be superior.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the combination of techniques is potentially useful, the novelty is incremental.  Many previous studies have explored hybrid CNN-Transformer architectures for medical image analysis. The specific contribution in terms of architecture or training strategy needs to be more significant.
*   **Lack of Comparative Analysis:** The paper focuses on the performance of HG-TNet but lacks a direct, detailed comparison with other state-of-the-art methods specifically designed for the LC25000 dataset.  Simply stating the Mangal et al. achieved 96% accuracy is not a rigorous comparison without controlling for training and hyperparameter settings.
*   **Limited Technical Depth:** The architectural details and the specific implementation of the graph attention mechanism need more elaboration.  It's unclear how the graph structure is constructed and how the attention mechanism operates on it.
*   **Justification for Capsule Networks**: The paper mentions that capsule networks realize how each element individually combines and forms whole structures. The justification on why the specific type of architectures being combined were chosen could be explained with more detail.

**Significance:**

The paper presents a practical approach for colon cancer classification, potentially contributing to improved diagnostic tools. However, its significance is limited by the incremental nature of its novelty and the lack of comprehensive comparative results.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper demonstrates a reasonable attempt to improve colon cancer classification using a hybrid deep learning model. However, its incremental novelty and the need for a more thorough comparative analysis hold back its significance. The technical details also require further clarification.

**Score: 5**

**Rationale:**

The paper's novelty is incremental, being an extension of existing hybrid architectures applied to a standard dataset. It lacks a convincing comparison with other state-of-the-art methods and lacks specific technical details regarding its model. This places it at a moderate score reflecting a competent, albeit not groundbreaking, contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 5/10

### IDEAlign: Comparing Large Language Models to Human Experts in Open-ended Interpretive Annotations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02855v1)
- **Authors**: Hyunji Nam, Lucia Langlois, James Malamut, Mei Tan, Dorottya Demszky
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to open-ended, interpretive annotation tasks, such as thematic analysis by researchers or generating feedback on student work by teachers. These tasks involve free-text annotations requiring expert-level judgments grounded in specific objectives (e.g., research questions or instructional goals). Evaluating whether LLM-generated annotations align with those generated by expert humans is challenging to do at scale, and currently, no validated, scalable measure of similarity in ideas exists. In this paper, we (i) introduce the scalable evaluation of interpretive annotation by LLMs as a critical and understudied task, (ii) propose IDEAlgin, an intuitive benchmarking paradigm for capturing expert similarity ratings via a "pick-the-odd-one-out" triplet judgment task, and (iii) evaluate various similarity metrics, including vector-based ones (topic models, embeddings) and LLM-as-a-judge via IDEAlgin, against these human benchmarks. Applying this approach to two real-world educational datasets (interpretive analysis and feedback generation), we find that vector-based metrics largely fail to capture the nuanced dimensions of similarity meaningful to experts. Prompting LLMs via IDEAlgin significantly improves alignment with expert judgments (9-30% increase) compared to traditional lexical and vector-based metrics. These results establish IDEAlgin as a promising paradigm for evaluating LLMs against open-ended expert annotations at scale, informing responsible deployment of LLMs in education and beyond.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenging problem of evaluating large language model (LLM) generated annotations for open-ended, interpretive tasks, where assessing the alignment of LLM outputs with expert human judgments is difficult due to the lack of scalable and validated similarity measures. It introduces IDEAlign, a novel benchmarking paradigm that uses a pick-the-odd-one-out task to capture expert similarity ratings in a relative, rather than absolute, manner. The paper then compares various similarity metrics, including vector-based methods and LLM-as-a-judge, against these human benchmarks on two real-world educational datasets (interpretive analysis and feedback generation).  The key finding is that traditional vector-based metrics often fail to capture nuanced similarity dimensions meaningful to experts. Moreover, prompting LLMs via IDEAlign significantly improves alignment with expert judgments compared to traditional metrics. This work establishes IDEAlign as a promising framework for evaluating LLMs against open-ended expert annotations at scale, informing responsible LLM deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *IDEAlign paradigm* itself.  While comparative judgment is not a completely new concept in ML evaluation, its application to the nuanced domain of open-ended, expert-level annotation, and *specifically the pick-the-odd-one-out triplet format for capturing idea-level similarity*, is a significant contribution. Also the paper addresses the current lack of validated scalable measurements for evaluating the similarity of ideas.
    The application of this paradigm to evaluating *LLM-as-a-judge* is also novel, allowing a direct comparison between human and model similarity judgments within the same framework.  The finding that prompting LLMs with the triplet format significantly improves alignment with expert judgments is a valuable, actionable insight.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial gap in LLM evaluation, particularly relevant to educational applications. As LLMs are increasingly used to generate open-ended annotations, it's paramount to have robust, scalable methods for assessing their alignment with human expertise. IDEAlign directly tackles this challenge by:

    *   Providing a *practical methodology* for collecting reliable human similarity judgments.
    *   *Validating a pathway* to scalable annotation via LLMs, once sufficient agreement with human experts is established.

The paper's significance is further amplified by its findings demonstrating the limitations of traditional vector-based metrics in capturing nuanced similarity. This highlights the need for more sophisticated evaluation approaches like IDEAlign. The experiments are well-designed, utilizing real-world educational datasets and comparing a range of metrics.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear articulation of the challenges in evaluating LLM annotations.
    *   The IDEAlign paradigm is intuitive, easily implementable, and addresses the cognitive challenges of absolute similarity ratings.
    *   Rigorous experimental design with real-world educational datasets and comparison of diverse similarity metrics.
    *   Actionable results showing the benefits of IDEAlign prompting for LLMs.
    *   Thorough exploration of potential limitations, such as the need for domain expertise in LLM judgment.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper explores different LLMs as judges, it does not address the computational cost of utilizing LLM as judges at a large scale.
    *   While the method demonstrates improvement, the correlation numbers are still not near the oracle, which indicates there's still room for improvement.
    *   The study is limited to two educational tasks, and generalizability to other domains requires further validation.
    *   The paper does not directly explore why vector-based metrics fail but only show that they do fail. In addition, a more thorough discussion on potential reasons for this failure could add to the novelty of the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:**  IDEAlign has the potential to become a widely adopted benchmarking paradigm for evaluating LLMs in open-ended, interpretive tasks. It can inform the development of more reliable and trustworthy LLM applications, particularly in sensitive domains like education. The paper will likely spur further research on developing more sophisticated similarity metrics that better capture human judgments. Also, future research can explore what other domains this method can be applied to.

**Overall:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by introducing IDEAlign as an effective evaluation method for idea-level annotation similarity that addresses several challenges in the field. It fills an existing gap in evaluating LLM performance, has significant implications, and offers strong potential for future research.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Cut Costs, Not Accuracy: LLM-Powered Data Processing with Guarantees
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02896v1)
- **Authors**: Sepanta Zeighami, Shreya Shankar, Aditya Parameswaran
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are being increasingly used as a building block in data systems to process large text datasets. To do so, LLM model providers offer multiple LLMs with different sizes, spanning various cost-quality trade-offs when processing text at scale. Top-of-the-line LLMs (e.g., GPT-4o, Claude Sonnet) operate with high accuracy but are prohibitively expensive when processing many records. To avoid high costs, more affordable but lower quality LLMs (e.g., GPT-4o-mini, Claude Haiku) can be used to process records, but we need to ensure that the overall accuracy does not deviate substantially from that of the top-of-the-line LLMs. The model cascade framework provides a blueprint to manage this trade-off, by using the confidence of LLMs in their output (e.g., log-probabilities) to decide on which records to use the affordable LLM. However, existing solutions following this framework provide only marginal cost savings and weak theoretical guarantees because of poor estimation of the quality of the affordable LLM's outputs. We present BARGAIN, a method that judiciously uses affordable LLMs in data processing to significantly reduce cost while providing strong theoretical guarantees on the solution quality. BARGAIN employs a novel adaptive sampling strategy and statistical estimation procedure that uses data and task characteristics and builds on recent statistical tools to make accurate estimations with tight theoretical guarantees. Variants of BARGAIN can support guarantees on accuracy, precision, or recall of the output. Experimental results across 8 real-world datasets show that BARGAIN reduces cost, on average, by up to 86% more than state-of-the-art, while providing stronger theoretical guarantees on accuracy of output, with similar gains when guaranteeing a desired level of precision or recall.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of cost-efficient LLM-powered data processing, specifically when using model cascades.  It introduces BARGAIN, a new method to judiciously use affordable LLMs while providing strong theoretical guarantees on solution quality (accuracy, precision, or recall). BARGAIN employs a novel adaptive sampling strategy and statistical estimation procedure that incorporates data and task characteristics.  The authors show that BARGAIN significantly reduces cost (oracle usage) compared to state-of-the-art methods while maintaining or improving answer quality. It is presented across accuracy target (AT), precision target (PT) and recall target (RT) queries for LLMs used in binary and multi-class classification tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in the combination of several techniques:  (1) the adaptive sampling method tailored to the specific task and data distribution, and (2) the use of recent statistical hypothesis testing tools (Waudby-Smith and Ramdas's result) to improve the accuracy of threshold estimation, leading to stronger theoretical guarantees with a low failure probability (3) a threshold estimation algorithm is proposed that uses tolerance parameter.
    The adaptive sampling differentiates BARGAIN from existing works that use uniform or proxy score-based sampling. Using tighter bounds compared to simpler inequalities like Hoeffding's inequality contributes to a more accurate estimation, and, consequently, better cost savings.
    While adaptive sampling and model cascades are individually known, the synergistic combination with tighter concentration bounds for LLMs classification represents a significant contribution, making the estimation and selection methods task- and data-aware.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact on the field is substantial.  LLMs are becoming increasingly integral to data systems, but their cost is a major bottleneck.  BARGAIN provides a practical and theoretically sound way to significantly reduce those costs without sacrificing accuracy or quality. The paper's significance stems from its potential to make LLM-powered data processing more accessible and scalable, especially for organizations with budget constraints.
    The empirical results are convincing, demonstrating substantial cost reductions compared to state-of-the-art methods across a variety of real-world datasets. Moreover, the rigorous proofs bolster confidence in BARGAIN's ability to provide robust quality guarantees that asymptotically weaker statistical bounds fail to give.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong theoretical foundations:  The paper provides clear theoretical analyses and proofs for its algorithms, offering rigorous guarantees.
    *   Practical applicability: The methods are designed to be practically implementable and effective in real-world scenarios.
    *   Empirical Validation: Extensive experiments on a diverse set of datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of BARGAIN.
    *   Clear Presentation: The paper is well-written and clearly explains the algorithms, analyses, and experimental results.
    *   Careful Parameter Analysis: A detailed parameter sensitivity study contributes to the practical usability of the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Complexity: The technical details of the algorithms and proofs might be daunting for readers unfamiliar with statistical estimation and hypothesis testing.
    *   Dependence on Proxy Model:  The performance of BARGAIN relies on the quality of the proxy LLM.  If the proxy model is poorly calibrated or lacks sufficient accuracy, the benefits of BARGAIN may be diminished. This limitation is acknowledged, but further investigation into handling poorly calibrated proxies could be beneficial.
    *   Computational Cost of Estimation: Although the paper reduces the oracle call cost, the computational cost from sampling can be higher for some tasks. How this compares to not doing estimation is hard to derive from results presented.
    *   Limitations on Generalization: In RT the paper proves that certain relaxation is necessary due to impossibility, which means that there are scenarios under which some quality constraint will be missed.

*   **Potential Influence:**  BARGAIN has the potential to influence the design of future LLM-powered data processing systems. It provides a blueprint for how to effectively manage cost-quality trade-offs in these systems and could be widely adopted by researchers and practitioners.  Frameworks and commercial offerings could incorporate this method to provide budget-aware and robust quality guarantees for LLM-driven data tasks.

*   **Rigorous Rationales for Score:**
    The following are included:
    * The combination of adaptive sampling, statistical hypothesis testing tools, and model cascades is the key driver for efficient quality LLM tasks.
    * Guarantees are now possible for cases where recall, accuracy, or precision is required to be close or meet oracle levels, whereas previously best effort methods did not achieve it.
    * Experiments showing substantial cost savings while ensuring theoretically better outcomes provide practical evidence for adoption in commercial and academic projects.
    * Limitations relating to calibration problems and guarantees in specific scenarios are areas for improvement.

**Score: 8**

While the paper does build upon existing concepts and has specific limitations, the innovative combination of techniques, the rigorous theoretical analysis, and the strong empirical validation of BARGAIN warrants a high score. It has the potential to become a core approach to cost-efficient and quality-assured LLM data processing.

- **Classification**: cs.DB
- **Score**: 8/10

### Advancing Minority Stress Detection with Transformers: Insights from the Social Media Datasets
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02908v1)
- **Authors**: Santosh Chapagain, Cory J Cascalheira, Shah Muhammad Hamdi, Soukaina Filali Boubrahimi, Jillian R. Scheer
- **Abstract**: Individuals from sexual and gender minority groups experience disproportionately high rates of poor health outcomes and mental disorders compared to their heterosexual and cisgender counterparts, largely as a consequence of minority stress as described by Meyer's (2003) model. This study presents the first comprehensive evaluation of transformer-based architectures for detecting minority stress in online discourse. We benchmark multiple transformer models including ELECTRA, BERT, RoBERTa, and BART against traditional machine learning baselines and graph-augmented variants. We further assess zero-shot and few-shot learning paradigms to assess their applicability on underrepresented datasets. Experiments are conducted on the two largest publicly available Reddit corpora for minority stress detection, comprising 12,645 and 5,789 posts, and are repeated over five random seeds to ensure robustness. Our results demonstrate that integrating graph structure consistently improves detection performance across transformer-only models and that supervised fine-tuning with relational context outperforms zero and few-shot approaches. Theoretical analysis reveals that modeling social connectivity and conversational context via graph augmentation sharpens the models' ability to identify key linguistic markers such as identity concealment, internalized stigma, and calls for support, suggesting that graph-enhanced transformers offer the most reliable foundation for digital health interventions and public health policy.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This paper investigates the effectiveness of transformer-based architectures for detecting minority stress in online social media discourse, specifically on Reddit. It benchmarks various transformer models like ELECTRA, BERT, RoBERTa, and BART against traditional machine learning baselines and graph-augmented variants. The study evaluates the performance of zero-shot and few-shot learning paradigms and analyzes the impact of integrating graph structure to capture conversational context and social connectivity. The experiments are conducted on two publicly available Reddit corpora and repeated over multiple runs for robustness. The results demonstrate that incorporating graph structure improves detection performance, and supervised fine-tuning with relational context outperforms zero-shot and few-shot approaches. The paper also includes interpretability analyses to understand the models' decision-making process.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the application of transformer models to minority stress detection is not entirely new, the comprehensive evaluation across different architectures, the inclusion of graph-augmented models, and the zero-shot/few-shot analysis contribute to the novelty of the work. The use of two Reddit datasets of varying characteristics adds another layer of novelty. The interpretability analysis, while employing standard techniques, is valuable for understanding model behavior in this specific domain. The novelty of graph augmentation on top of transformer models to leverage the inherent relational features from Reddit to better minority stress detection.

*   **Significance:** Minority stress is a significant issue impacting the health and well-being of LGBTQ+ individuals. Accurate detection of minority stress in online discourse can enable early interventions and support for at-risk individuals. The results of this paper provide insights into the most effective approaches for building such detection systems, potentially informing the development of digital health interventions and public health policy. The paper uses a labeled dataset to leverage the power of supervised learning with transformer models and it contributes to the existing literature for a critical challenge. Also, the paper compares the baseline models with current transformer models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive evaluation of different transformer architectures.
    *   Inclusion of graph-augmented models, leveraging social context.
    *   Zero-shot and few-shot learning analysis, assessing generalizability.
    *   Robust experimental design with multiple runs.
    *   Interpretability analyses to understand model behavior.
    *   Clear presentation of results and insights.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper relies on Reddit data, which might not be representative of the entire LGBTQ+ population or the full spectrum of minority stress experiences.
    *   The improvement from graph augmentation is statistically significant on MiSSOM+ but not on the larger Saha dataset.
    *   The datasets used in the paper may contain biases that are not fully addressed, and this should be examined to reduce misclassification.
    *   The zero-shot and few-shot results were somewhat disappointing, indicating the need for further research in this area.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more effective minority stress detection systems, which can be used in digital health interventions and public health policy. The insights into the importance of graph structure and the limitations of zero-shot/few-shot learning can guide future research in this area. However, the limitations regarding dataset representativeness and potential biases should be addressed to ensure responsible and ethical application of these technologies.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by providing a comprehensive evaluation of transformer-based architectures for minority stress detection. The inclusion of graph-augmented models is a valuable addition, and the interpretability analyses help to understand model behavior. However, the limitations regarding dataset representativeness and the lack of significant improvement on the Saha dataset temper the overall impact. A score of 7 reflects the paper's clear contribution and potential for further research, while acknowledging its limitations and the need for more robust validation. The significance of the outcome could have larger impact in the broader community with the proposed framework.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Basic B*** Effect: The Use of LLM-based Agents Reduces the Distinctiveness and Diversity of People's Choices
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02910v1)
- **Authors**: Sandra C. Matz, C. Blaine Horton, Sofie Goethals
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly act on people's behalf: they write emails, buy groceries, and book restaurants. While the outsourcing of human decision-making to AI can be both efficient and effective, it raises a fundamental question: how does delegating identity-defining choices to AI reshape who people become? We study the impact of agentic LLMs on two identity-relevant outcomes: interpersonal distinctiveness - how unique a person's choices are relative to others - and intrapersonal diversity - the breadth of a single person's choices over time. Using real choices drawn from social-media behavior of 1,000 U.S. users (110,000 choices in total), we compare a generic and personalized agent to a human baseline. Both agents shift people's choices toward more popular options, reducing the distinctiveness of their behaviors and preferences. While the use of personalized agents tempers this homogenization (compared to the generic AI), it also more strongly compresses the diversity of people's preference portfolios by narrowing what they explore across topics and psychological affinities. Understanding how AI agents might flatten human experience, and how using generic versus personalized agents involves distinctiveness-diversity trade-offs, is critical for designing systems that augment rather than constrain human agency, and for safeguarding diversity in thought, taste, and expression.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "The Basic B*** Effect: The Use of LLM-based Agents Reduces the Distinctiveness and Diversity of People's Choices."

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) as agentic AIs impacts the distinctiveness and diversity of human choices. The authors hypothesize that LLMs, by favoring popular or statistically frequent options, will lead to a reduction in both interpersonal distinctiveness (how unique a person's choices are compared to others) and intrapersonal diversity (the breadth of a single person's choices over time). Using a dataset of 110,000 real-world choices made by 1,000 individuals on Facebook, they compare the choices made by generic and personalized LLM agents to a human baseline. The results indicate that both agents, particularly the generic one, reduce interpersonal distinctiveness by steering choices toward more normative options.  Interestingly, personalized agents were found to compress the diversity of people's preference portfolios more strongly than generic AIs by narrowing what they explore. The authors conclude that relying on agentic AI poses a risk of flattening human experience and that there is a trade-off between using generic vs. personalized agents. Generic agents homogenize choices across people, while personalized agents limit individual diversity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses an important and timely question: how does delegating decision-making to AI impact individual identity, choice and diversity? As LLMs become increasingly prevalent in our lives, understanding their potential influence on our preferences and behaviors is crucial. The paper's strengths lie in its empirical approach, using a large, real-world dataset to test its hypotheses. The comparison of generic and personalized agents is also a valuable contribution, revealing nuanced effects that might be missed if only one type of agent were considered.

**Novelty:**

The paper makes a novel contribution by shifting the focus from how LLMs influence collective behavior to how they affect individual choices and identity. While prior research has explored the homogenization of creative outputs and the reduction in diversity across groups using LLMs, this study specifically examines the effects on interpersonal distinctiveness and intrapersonal diversity for individual users. Analyzing the trade-off between generic and personalized AIs adds further nuance to the existing literature.

**Significance:**

The findings have important implications for the design and deployment of AI agents. The paper suggests that we need to be mindful of the potential for these systems to reduce individual diversity and distinctiveness, even when they are designed to be personalized. It also highlights the importance of considering how design choices, such as the degree of personalization, can influence these effects. As noted by the authors, further work would be useful to expand these methods to additional scenarios and to consider how different individuals are affected by these agents.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Platform-Specificity:** The study relies solely on Facebook data, which may limit the generalizability of the findings to other domains.
*   **Behavioral Measure:** The study relies on Facebook pages followed as a proxy measure of preferences. In the current Facebook landscape, this activity is far less a declaration of preference and may instead reflect a variety of network or social pressures.
*   **Lack of Behavioral Choice Data:** The study does not test whether individuals would actually accept the AI-generated choices. The authors sidestep the question with a restriction choice, but the lack of behavioral data undermines claims regarding the impact of the B-effect.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper is well-executed and addresses a relevant question, the limitations described above temper the overall significance. The platform-specific nature of the data, the reliance on the pages followed as a weak form of self-expression, and the lack of behavioral adoption data raise questions about the generalizability and real-world impact of the findings. The study is a valuable contribution to the field of AI ethics and design, but its limitations prevent it from being a groundbreaking contribution.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Single Domain Generalization in Diabetic Retinopathy: A Neuro-Symbolic Learning Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02918v1)
- **Authors**: Midhat Urooj, Ayan Banerjee, Farhat Shaikh, Kuntal Thakur, Sandeep Gupta
- **Abstract**: Domain generalization remains a critical challenge in medical imaging, where models trained on single sources often fail under real-world distribution shifts. We propose KG-DG, a neuro-symbolic framework for diabetic retinopathy (DR) classification that integrates vision transformers with expert-guided symbolic reasoning to enable robust generalization across unseen domains. Our approach leverages clinical lesion ontologies through structured, rule-based features and retinal vessel segmentation, fusing them with deep visual representations via a confidence-weighted integration strategy. The framework addresses both single-domain generalization (SDG) and multi-domain generalization (MDG) by minimizing the KL divergence between domain embeddings, thereby enforcing alignment of high-level clinical semantics. Extensive experiments across four public datasets (APTOS, EyePACS, Messidor-1, Messidor-2) demonstrate significant improvements: up to a 5.2% accuracy gain in cross-domain settings and a 6% improvement over baseline ViT models. Notably, our symbolic-only model achieves a 63.67% average accuracy in MDG, while the complete neuro-symbolic integration achieves the highest accuracy compared to existing published baselines and benchmarks in challenging SDG scenarios. Ablation studies reveal that lesion-based features (84.65% accuracy) substantially outperform purely neural approaches, confirming that symbolic components act as effective regularizers beyond merely enhancing interpretability. Our findings establish neuro-symbolic integration as a promising paradigm for building clinically robust, and domain-invariant medical AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KG-DG, a neuro-symbolic learning framework designed to enhance domain generalization in diabetic retinopathy (DR) classification.  KG-DG integrates vision transformers with expert-guided symbolic reasoning based on clinical lesion ontologies and retinal vessel segmentation.  By minimizing the KL divergence between domain embeddings constructed from symbolic features, the framework aims to align high-level clinical semantics across different datasets (domains), improving out-of-distribution robustness.  The approach is evaluated on four public DR datasets (APTOS, EyePACS, Messidor-1, Messidor-2) in both single-domain generalization (SDG) and multi-domain generalization (MDG) settings.  The results show significant improvements compared to baseline vision transformer models and other domain generalization techniques.  Ablation studies highlight the importance of lesion-based symbolic features as effective regularizers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques to address domain generalization in DR. While neuro-symbolic learning and domain generalization are established areas, the framework's design, which leverages clinical lesion ontologies through structured rules and couples them with Vision Transformers using a confidence-weighted integration strategy, is a unique contribution. Explicitly minimizing the KL divergence between domain embeddings derived from symbolic features to enforce clinical semantics is also a noteworthy element.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from the practical challenge of domain shift in medical imaging. DR classification models often suffer from poor performance when applied to datasets different from the one they were trained on due to variations in imaging devices and patient demographics. KG-DG addresses this problem by integrating clinical knowledge, making the models more robust to distribution shifts. The improvement in accuracy, especially in SDG settings, is important. Furthermore, the interpretability afforded by the symbolic component is crucial for clinical acceptance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper thoroughly evaluates the proposed method in both SDG and MDG settings, across multiple datasets, and against a range of established baselines, including state-of-the-art domain generalization techniques.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contributions of different components, particularly the symbolic lesion features. The high accuracy achieved by the symbolic-only model is especially compelling.
    *   **Clinically Relevant:** The integration of clinical knowledge and the resulting improvement in interpretability are major strengths, potentially facilitating clinical adoption.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the methodology, experimental setup, and results.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The use of readily available datasets, open-source components (YOLOv11, U-Net), and adherence to established protocols like DomainBed enhance reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Lesion Annotations:** A key limitation is the reliance on accurate lesion-level annotations. While YOLOv11 is used for lesion detection, the initial training/fine-tuning of this module still requires labeled data, potentially limiting applicability in scenarios where such annotations are unavailable or expensive to obtain. Although the authors validate the modules in question and employ annotators with domain expertise, there could still be a discrepancy of understanding of the extracted biomarkers for the YOLOv11 and U-Net implementations with relation to clinical guidelines.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Fusion Strategies:** While the paper explores a few fusion strategies, further investigation into adaptive or learned fusion methods could potentially improve performance. More detailed discussion on the choice of AdamW as the optimizer, especially given alternative robust optimizers such as Adabelief could also be explored.
    *   **Domain Shift Characterization:** Although experiments demonstrated a clear robustness to domain shift, there is little characterization of the nature of the shift, which makes it difficult to understand how to best transfer symbolic knowledge to other medical tasks. The definition of a dataset as a "domain" is vague in this setting.
    *   **Scalability:** The computational cost and complexity of training both the deep learning and symbolic components, along with the fusion mechanism, could pose challenges for large-scale datasets or real-time applications.

*   **Potential Impact:**  KG-DG has the potential to influence the development of more robust and reliable medical AI systems, particularly in areas where domain shift is a significant concern. The emphasis on integrating clinical knowledge could lead to more interpretable and trustworthy models, fostering greater adoption in clinical practice. However, the reliance on annotated data and other limitations should be noted.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

KG-DG is a well-executed research paper that makes a valuable contribution to the field of medical image analysis. The neuro-symbolic approach effectively addresses the challenge of domain generalization in diabetic retinopathy classification. The comprehensive evaluation, clear presentation, and clinical relevance of the work are strengths. However, the dependence on lesion annotations, the somewhat limited exploration of fusion strategies, and potential scalability issues prevent it from achieving a higher score. The novelty, while present, is incremental rather than transformative. The impact is likely to be significant, especially in the domain of DR and medical image analysis in general, encouraging greater application of neuro-symbolic methods in a robust manner. However, widespread adoption might be limited by the above-mentioned weaknesses.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### KEPT: Knowledge-Enhanced Prediction of Trajectories from Consecutive Driving Frames with Vision-Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02966v1)
- **Authors**: Yujin Wang, Tianyi Wang, Quanfeng Liu, Wenxian Fan, Junfeng Jiao, Christian Claudel, Yunbing Yan, Bingzhao Gao, Jianqiang Wang, Hong Chen
- **Abstract**: Accurate short-horizon trajectory prediction is pivotal for safe and reliable autonomous driving, yet existing vision-language models (VLMs) often fail to effectively ground their reasoning in scene dynamics and domain knowledge. To address this challenge, this paper introduces KEPT, a knowledge-enhanced VLM framework that predicts ego trajectories directly from consecutive front-view driving frames. KEPT couples a temporal frequency-spatial fusion (TFSF) video encoder, trained via self-supervised learning with hard-negative mining, with a scalable k-means + HNSW retrieval stack that supplies scene-aligned exemplars. Retrieved priors are embedded into chain-of-thought (CoT) prompts with explicit planning constraints, while a triple-stage fine-tuning schedule incrementally aligns the language head to metric spatial cues, physically feasible motion, and temporally conditioned front-view planning. Evaluated on nuScenes dataset, KEPT achieves state-of-the-art performance across open-loop protocols: under NoAvg, it achieves 0.70m average L2 with a 0.21\% collision rate; under TemAvg with lightweight ego status, it attains 0.31m average L2 and a 0.07\% collision rate. Ablation studies show that all three fine-tuning stages contribute complementary benefits, and that using Top-2 retrieved exemplars yields the best accuracy-safety trade-off. The k-means-clustered HNSW index delivers sub-millisecond retrieval latency, supporting practical deployment. These results indicate that retrieval-augmented, CoT-guided VLMs offer a promising, data-efficient pathway toward interpretable and trustworthy autonomous driving.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KEPT (Knowledge-Enhanced Prediction of Trajectories), a novel framework for improving trajectory prediction in autonomous driving using vision-language models (VLMs). KEPT leverages a combination of: 1) a specialized temporal frequency-spatial fusion (TFSF) video encoder, 2) a scalable k-means + HNSW retrieval stack to provide scene-aligned exemplars, 3) chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting with explicit planning constraints, and 4) a three-stage fine-tuning schedule to align the language head to metric spatial cues. The framework retrieves similar driving scenarios from a knowledge base and uses them as context for the VLM to predict future trajectories. Experiments on the nuScenes dataset demonstrate state-of-the-art performance in open-loop trajectory prediction protocols.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Relevance:** Trajectory prediction is a crucial task in autonomous driving, and the paper tackles the challenge of improving VLMs' performance in this domain, which is of significant practical importance.
    *   **Methodological Novelty:** KEPT integrates several components in a novel way. The TFSF encoder captures spatiotemporal dynamics, while the retrieval mechanism brings in valuable contextual information. The CoT prompting approach helps guide the VLM's reasoning process, and the fine-tuning strategy further improves the model's grounding in the driving domain. The combination of these elements is a strength.
    *   **Performance:** The experimental results demonstrate that KEPT achieves state-of-the-art performance on the nuScenes dataset, outperforming existing methods. This provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Data Efficiency:** The paper emphasizes a data-efficient pathway for model training. It is claimed that relatively few samples are needed to train this model.
    *   **Real-Time Retrieval:** The work proposes a retrieval strategy that can be performed in sub-milliseconds.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on nuScenes:** The experiments are primarily conducted on the nuScenes dataset. While nuScenes is a widely used benchmark, it is limited in scope and might not fully capture the diversity of real-world driving scenarios. Generalization to other datasets or real-world environments needs to be verified.
    *   **Retrieval Bias:** The performance of KEPT heavily relies on the quality and representativeness of the knowledge base used for retrieval. If the knowledge base is biased or incomplete, the model might not perform well in novel or rare scenarios.
    *   **Complexity:** The KEPT framework is quite complex, involving multiple components and training stages. The overall framework appears to be more of a combination of existing methods to achieve better results, rather than developing one or two new novel building blocks.
    *   **Limited Baseline Comparison:** The comparative experiments are limited to a few existing methods. It would have been valuable to compare KEPT against a wider range of baselines.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** Ablation is limited, only one ablation on fine-tuning is performed and only one ablation is performed on the top-k.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The primary novelty lies in the integrated approach, combining a specific video encoder, retrieval strategy, prompting method, and fine-tuning schedule. While individual components might be based on existing techniques, the specific configuration and combination are novel.
    *   The significance is in improving the accuracy and safety of trajectory prediction using VLMs, which has direct implications for the development of more reliable autonomous driving systems.
    *   The efficiency aspect, in terms of both data requirements and computational cost, is also significant.

*   **Potential Impact:** The proposed KEPT framework has the potential to influence the development of more robust and reliable trajectory prediction systems. The approach of using retrieval-augmented VLMs with CoT prompting could be adopted by other researchers and practitioners in the field.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a strong overall framework and achieves state-of-the-art performance, several limitations prevent a higher score. The dependence on nuScenes, potential retrieval biases, and complex framework are considerable drawbacks. However, the novel integration of diverse methods, performance improvements, and data/computational efficiency justify a positive evaluation. The paper is well-executed but presents more of an evolutionary improvement rather than a revolutionary one. The significance of trajectory prediction for autonomous vehicles lends to a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### AR-KAN: Autoregressive-Weight-Enhanced Kolmogorov-Arnold Network for Time Series Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02967v1)
- **Authors**: Chen Zeng, Tiehang Xu, Qiao Wang
- **Abstract**: Conventional neural networks frequently face challenges in spectral analysis of signals. To address this challenge, Fourier neural networks (FNNs) and similar approaches integrate components of Fourier series into the structure of neural networks. Nonetheless, a significant hurdle is often overlooked: the superposition of periodic signals does not necessarily result in a periodic signal. For example, when forecasting almost periodic functions composed of signals with incommensurate frequencies, traditional models such as Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average (ARIMA) frequently outperform most neural networks including large language models (LLMs). To tackle this goal, we propose Autoregressive-Weight-Enhanced AR-KAN, a hybrid model that combines the benefits of both methods. Using the Universal Myopic Mapping Theorem, we apply a Kolmogorov-Arnold Network (KAN) for the static nonlinear part and include memory through a pre-trained AR component, which can be explained to retain the most useful information while eliminating redundancy. Experimental data indicates that AR-KAN delivers superior results on $72\%$ of real-world datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AR-KAN, a novel hybrid model for time series forecasting. AR-KAN combines the strengths of two distinct approaches: traditional autoregressive (AR) models and Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs). The AR component, pre-trained and used as a memory module, captures linear temporal dependencies and enhances spectral bias.  The KAN, leveraging the Universal Myopic Mapping Theorem, models the static nonlinear part of the time series, offering improved expressivity.  Experiments on synthetic almost-periodic functions and a collection of real-world datasets demonstrate that AR-KAN achieves superior or comparable performance to existing methods, including deep learning models and traditional ARIMA models.  The paper emphasizes AR-KAN's ability to capture complex temporal dynamics and its generalizability across diverse datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the architecture of AR-KAN. While AR and KANs themselves are not new, the **hybrid approach** of combining them in a specific way, where a pre-trained AR model acts as a feature extractor (memory module) for the KAN, is a distinctive contribution.  The use of the AR model to retain key information while minimizing redundancy also adds to the novelty. Further, the connection of the method to Universal Myopic Mapping Theorem provides a theoretical justification for the architecture.

**Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its ability to address a specific weakness of deep learning models in time series forecasting: their struggle with almost-periodic functions where standard deep learning models fail to outperform classical ARIMA. AR-KAN offers a solution that matches or exceeds the performance of ARIMA while retaining the adaptability of neural networks.

**Strengths:**

*   **Principled design:** AR-KAN's architecture is theoretically grounded in the Universal Myopic Mapping Theorem, which helps to justify the approach and lends credibility to the model. The redundancy elimination in the memory module is also a data-driven but interpretable approach.
*   **Strong empirical results:** The experiments demonstrate AR-KAN's effectiveness on both synthetic and real-world datasets. The claim of achieving state-of-the-art results on a substantial proportion (72%) of datasets is a compelling argument for the model's practical value.
*   **Addresses a specific limitation:** The paper clearly identifies and addresses a weakness of existing deep learning approaches, increasing its impact.
*   **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-written and structured, explaining the model and experiments clearly.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited theoretical analysis:** While the authors claim to prove it "optimally preserves predictive information and minimizing redundancy," a more rigorous proof of this specific aspect would strengthen the paper. It would be beneficial to have a more comprehensive analysis of the AR memory module's feature extraction capabilities.
*   **Computational cost:** The computational cost compared to ARIMA is likely higher, even if lower than some large language models. While not a primary focus, some discussion of the computational demands would be appropriate.
*   **Ablation studies:** Including ablation studies to investigate the impact of different components of the model. In particular, it would be worthwhile to determine the contribution of pretraining the AR module vs. having it untrained or not having AR at all.

**Potential Influence:**

AR-KAN has the potential to influence the field by:

*   **Inspiring hybrid approaches:** It could encourage researchers to explore hybrid models that combine the strengths of classical methods and deep learning techniques.
*   **Re-emphasizing classical techniques:** By demonstrating that classical models can still be competitive, it might prompt a re-evaluation of established time series methods.
*   **Providing a benchmark:** AR-KAN's strong performance could establish it as a new benchmark for evaluating time series forecasting models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and effective model for time series forecasting, addressing a known weakness of existing methods and demonstrating strong empirical results. The connection to the Universal Myopic Mapping Theorem provides a theoretical basis, although a more rigorous analysis of certain aspects (particularly redundancy minimization) would improve the paper. While the incremental increase is not transformative, the paper offers a valuable contribution to the field. The strengths outweigh the weaknesses, and the model's potential impact is considerable.
