The experimental validation offers encouraging support for its claims, but there are critical areas that require further investigation (unlearning resistance, fingerprint transferability, the quality of perturbations).
    *   The gray-box setting is more closely aligned with practical constraints, which is a key strength.
    *   The emphasis on stealth and resilience is relevant and distinguishes EverTracer from much of the prior art.

**Score: 7**

*Justification:* EverTracer's use of MIA repurposing and probability variation for gray-box fingerprinting showcases significant novelty and potential in LLM security. While the experimental results are encouraging, the limited exploration of unlearning defenses and fingerprint transferability keeps the score from being higher. The reliance on subtle probability variations, which might be more sensitive than trigger-based approaches, also requires deeper examination. A score of 7 reflects the promise of the approach, but also highlights the need for continued research to fully understand and address the remaining limitations.
- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Loong: Synthesize Long Chain-of-Thoughts at Scale through Verifiers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03059v1)
- **Authors**: Xingyue Huang, Rishabh, Gregor Franke, Ziyi Yang, Jiamu Bai, Weijie Bai, Jinhe Bi, Zifeng Ding, Yiqun Duan, Chengyu Fan, Wendong Fan, Xin Gao, Ruohao Guo, Yuan He, Zhuangzhuang He, Xianglong Hu, Neil Johnson, Bowen Li, Fangru Lin, Siyu Lin, Tong Liu, Yunpu Ma, Hao Shen, Hao Sun, Beibei Wang, Fangyijie Wang, Hao Wang, Haoran Wang, Yang Wang, Yifeng Wang, Zhaowei Wang, Ziyang Wang, Yifan Wu, Zikai Xiao, Chengxing Xie, Fan Yang, Junxiao Yang, Qianshuo Ye, Ziyu Ye, Guangtao Zeng, Yuwen Ebony Zhang, Zeyu Zhang, Zihao Zhu, Bernard Ghanem, Philip Torr, Guohao Li
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown that their reasoning capabilities can be significantly improved through Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Reward (RLVR), particularly in domains like mathematics and programming, where ground-truth correctness can be automatically evaluated. However, extending this success to other reasoning-intensive domains remains challenging due to the scarcity of high-quality, verifiable datasets and the high cost of human supervision. In this work, we introduce the Loong Project: an open-source framework for scalable synthetic data generation and verification across a diverse range of reasoning-intensive domains. The framework consists of two key components: (1) LoongBench, a curated seed dataset containing 8,729 human-vetted examples across 12 domains (e.g., Advanced Mathematics, Chemistry, Logic), each paired with executable code and rich metadata; and (2) LoongEnv, a modular synthetic data generation environment that supports multiple prompting strategies to produce new question-answer-code triples. Together, these components form an agent-environment loop that enables reinforcement learning, where an LLM-based agent is rewarded for generating Chain-of-Thought (CoT) solutions that align with code-executed answers. Empirically, we benchmark LoongBench on a broad suite of both open-source and proprietary LLMs to evaluate domain coverage and reveal performance bottlenecks. In addition, we conduct a comprehensive analysis of synthetic data generated by LoongEnv, examining correctness, difficulty, and diversity. Code and documentation are available at https://github.com/camel-ai/loong.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Loong: Synthesize Long Chain-of-Thoughts at Scale through Verifiers" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Loong Project, an open-source framework designed for scalable synthetic data generation and verification for reasoning-intensive domains.  It aims to address the scarcity of high-quality, verifiable datasets in areas beyond mathematics and programming, where Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has shown success. The framework consists of two main components: LOONGBENCH, a curated seed dataset of 8,729 human-vetted examples across 12 diverse domains; and LOONGENV, a modular synthetic data generation environment that supports various prompting strategies for creating new question-answer-code triples. The paper benchmarks LOONGBENCH against several LLMs, evaluates the synthetic data generated by LOONGENV, and analyzes its correctness, difficulty, and diversity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable and innovative system for generating and verifying synthetic data in reasoning-intensive domains. While the individual components (LLMs, code execution, verification) are not entirely new, the combination and orchestration of these elements within a modular and scalable framework, the creation of the diverse LOONGBENCH dataset, and the systematic evaluation are significant contributions. The explicit focus on generating *verifiable* CoT examples at scale is a noteworthy aspect.

*   **Significance:** The project addresses a critical bottleneck in scaling RLVR to a wider range of domains: the lack of high-quality, verifiable data. Loong has the potential to accelerate research in reasoning and problem-solving by providing a readily available and expandable resource for training and evaluating LLMs. The benchmarking of various models on LOONGBENCH offers valuable insights into domain-specific performance and reveals areas for improvement. The analysis of synthetic data generation techniques also helps understand the trade-offs between diversity, correctness, and difficulty.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** Loong offers a complete pipeline, from data generation to verification, enabling end-to-end research and development.
    *   **LOONGBENCH Dataset:** The curated seed dataset provides a strong foundation for synthetic data generation and model evaluation, encompassing a wide range of reasoning domains.
    *   **Modularity and Extensibility:** The modular design of LOONGENV allows for easy integration of new prompting strategies, verification methods, and domains.
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper includes thorough benchmarking and analysis, providing valuable insights into model performance and data quality.
    *   **Open-Source:** The project's open-source nature promotes collaboration and further development by the research community.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM-as-a-Judge:** Relying on LLMs for semantic equivalence judgements, whilst somewhat mitigated with code execution, still can suffer from bias. Domain-specific verifiers would be an improvement.
    *   **Limited RLVR:**  The paper primarily focuses on synthetic data generation and evaluation but doesn't fully explore the integration of LOONGENV with RLVR. The promise of using this framework to train agents directly via reinforcement learning is a key part of the project's goal.
    *   **Reliance on Proprietary LLMs:** While some open-source LLMs are used, the framework relies heavily on proprietary LLMs (like GPT-4 and Claude) for generation and evaluation, which might limit accessibility for some researchers and impact reproducibility if APIs change.

*   **Potential Influence:** Loong has the potential to become a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners working on reasoning and problem-solving with LLMs. It could enable the development of more robust and generalizable models by providing a scalable and verifiable training data source. The insights from the benchmark and data generation analysis could also guide future research directions.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** The Loong Project offers a significant contribution to the field of AI by addressing the crucial need for high-quality, verifiable data for reasoning-intensive tasks. The creation of LOONGBENCH and the modular design of LOONGENV are valuable assets. The paper's strengths are the breadth of the framework, the curated dataset, the modular design, and systematic evaluation. While there are limitations, such as the reliance on LLM-as-a-judge and proprietary LLMs, the project's open-source nature and potential for impact in scaling RLVR to new domains justify a score of 8. It has the potential to substantially influence future research in this area and is a significant step towards more capable and reliable AI systems.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Are We SOLID Yet? An Empirical Study on Prompting LLMs to Detect Design Principle Violations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03093v1)
- **Authors**: Fatih Pehlivan, Arçin Ülkü Ergüzen, Sahand Moslemi Yengejeh, Mayasah Lami, Anil Koyuncu
- **Abstract**: Traditional static analysis methods struggle to detect semantic design flaws, such as violations of the SOLID principles, which require a strong understanding of object-oriented design patterns and principles. Existing solutions typically focus on individual SOLID principles or specific programming languages, leaving a gap in the ability to detect violations across all five principles in multi-language codebases. This paper presents a new approach: a methodology that leverages tailored prompt engineering to assess LLMs on their ability to detect SOLID violations across multiple languages. We present a benchmark of four leading LLMs-CodeLlama, DeepSeekCoder, QwenCoder, and GPT-4o Mini-on their ability to detect violations of all five SOLID principles. For this evaluation, we construct a new benchmark dataset of 240 manually validated code examples. Using this dataset, we test four distinct prompt strategies inspired by established zero-shot, few-shot, and chain-of-thought techniques to systematically measure their impact on detection accuracy. Our emerging results reveal a stark hierarchy among models, with GPT-4o Mini decisively outperforming others, yet even struggles with challenging principles like DIP. Crucially, we show that prompt strategy has a dramatic impact, but no single strategy is universally best; for instance, a deliberative ENSEMBLE prompt excels at OCP detection while a hint-based EXAMPLE prompt is superior for DIP violations. Across all experiments, detection accuracy is heavily influenced by language characteristics and degrades sharply with increasing code complexity. These initial findings demonstrate that effective, AI-driven design analysis requires not a single best model, but a tailored approach that matches the right model and prompt to the specific design context, highlighting the potential of LLMs to support maintainability through AI-assisted code analysis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents an empirical study evaluating the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to detect violations of SOLID design principles in code.  It addresses the lack of benchmarks for this task by creating a new dataset of manually validated code snippets in Java, Python, Kotlin, and C#, covering all five SOLID principles at varying difficulty levels. The study tests four leading LLMs (CodeLlama:70B, DeepSeekCoder: 33B, Qwen2.5 Coder:32B, and GPT-40 Mini) using four different prompt strategies (DEFAULT, EXAMPLE, SMELL, and ENSEMBLE) inspired by zero-shot, few-shot, and chain-of-thought techniques. The results show a performance hierarchy among models, with GPT-40 Mini outperforming the others, and that prompt strategy and language characteristics significantly impact detection accuracy.  The paper highlights the potential of LLMs for AI-assisted code analysis but emphasizes the need for a tailored approach, matching the right model and prompt to the specific design context.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a significant gap in the field by providing the first systematic empirical benchmark for LLM performance on SOLID principle violation detection. This is a novel application of LLMs in a crucial software engineering domain.
*   **Dataset Creation:** The creation of a publicly available, manually validated benchmark dataset is a major contribution. The dataset includes both violating and refactored code versions in multiple languages, adding to its value.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study thoroughly evaluates multiple LLMs, programming languages, and prompt strategies, providing a comprehensive analysis of the factors affecting LLM performance. The methodical approach strengthens the validity of the results.
*   **Prompt Engineering:** The thoughtful design and evaluation of different prompt strategies offer valuable insights into how to effectively leverage LLMs for this task. The findings regarding the complementary nature of different prompts (e.g., ENSEMBLE for OCP, EXAMPLE for DIP) are particularly insightful.
*   **Reproducibility:**  The availability of a replication package enhances the reproducibility and transparency of the research, allowing other researchers to build upon the findings.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper's findings have practical implications for the development of AI-driven code analysis tools that can assist developers in improving code quality and maintainability.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Synthetic Dataset:** The use of a synthetic dataset, while necessary to control for factors like code complexity and the presence of violations, may not fully represent the complexities of real-world industrial codebases. The LLMs might perform differently on code with more nuanced or ambiguous violations.
*   **Model Selection:** While the study includes several leading LLMs, the rapidly evolving landscape of LLMs means that the specific model rankings may change over time. It would be beneficial to have a section discussing the potential for newer models to improve on these results.
*   **Manual Labeling:** The reliance on manual labeling for a significant portion of the model outputs (37%) raises potential concerns about subjectivity and introduces a potential source of bias. A discussion of the steps taken to mitigate bias in the manual labeling process would be beneficial.
*   **Limited Scope of Refactoring:** The study focuses on detection, but does not extend to automated refactoring. While acknowledging this as future work, demonstrating even a limited refactoring capability would significantly enhance the paper's practical impact.
*   **Generalizability of Prompts:** While the prompt strategies are well-designed, their effectiveness may vary depending on the specific code style, project conventions, or complexity of the design violation within a real-world codebase. The prompt engineering may need to be adapted in practice.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the understanding of LLM capabilities in software engineering. It provides a valuable benchmark dataset and offers practical insights into prompt engineering for SOLID principle violation detection. The findings highlight the potential of LLMs to support code quality and maintainability but also emphasize the need for a context-aware approach. The paper will likely stimulate further research in this area, leading to the development of more effective AI-driven code analysis tools.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper's strengths lie in its novelty, comprehensive evaluation, and the creation of a valuable benchmark dataset. The empirical study addresses a critical gap in the literature. However, the reliance on a synthetic dataset, the potential for bias in manual labeling, and the lack of refactoring experiments limit the paper's overall impact. The score reflects the significant contribution of the paper while acknowledging its limitations and the need for further research to address these issues.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Measuring Scalar Constructs in Social Science with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03116v1)
- **Authors**: Hauke Licht, Rupak Sarkar, Patrick Y. Wu, Pranav Goel, Niklas Stoehr, Elliott Ash, Alexander Miserlis Hoyle
- **Abstract**: Many constructs that characterize language, like its complexity or emotionality, have a naturally continuous semantic structure; a public speech is not just "simple" or "complex," but exists on a continuum between extremes. Although large language models (LLMs) are an attractive tool for measuring scalar constructs, their idiosyncratic treatment of numerical outputs raises questions of how to best apply them. We address these questions with a comprehensive evaluation of LLM-based approaches to scalar construct measurement in social science. Using multiple datasets sourced from the political science literature, we evaluate four approaches: unweighted direct pointwise scoring, aggregation of pairwise comparisons, token-probability-weighted pointwise scoring, and finetuning. Our study yields actionable findings for applied researchers. First, LLMs prompted to generate pointwise scores directly from texts produce discontinuous distributions with bunching at arbitrary numbers. The quality of the measurements improves with pairwise comparisons made by LLMs, but it improves even more by taking pointwise scores and weighting them by token probability. Finally, finetuning smaller models with as few as 1,000 training pairs can match or exceed the performance of prompted LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Measuring Scalar Constructs in Social Science with LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenges of using Large Language Models (LLMs) to measure scalar constructs (i.e., constructs that exist on a continuum, like emotional intensity or political leaning) in social science texts. It investigates various prompting and fine-tuning methods, evaluating their effectiveness on three datasets from political science, each annotated with human-generated pairwise comparisons. The paper compares direct pointwise scoring, pairwise comparisons, token-probability-weighted pointwise scoring, and fine-tuning approaches. It identifies potential pitfalls of directly prompting LLMs for scalar measurements, such as "heaped" distributions and poor calibration. The authors found that token-probability weighting improves pointwise scoring and that fine-tuning smaller models on relatively small datasets (around 1000 labeled pairs) can outperform prompted LLMs, even much larger ones. The paper concludes with practical recommendations for researchers aiming to quantify scalar constructs using LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic comparison of various LLM-based approaches to scalar construct measurement within the specific context of social science datasets. While individual techniques like pairwise comparison and token-probability weighting aren't brand new, their comprehensive evaluation and comparison within this specific application are a valuable contribution. The emphasis on readily available political datasets and relatively straightforward implementations enhances its appeal to social scientists who might be new to LLMs.  The paper highlights the nuanced performance differences between approaches, demonstrating that the 'best' method depends on factors like model size, available training data, and desired level of precision (RMSE vs. rank correlation).

**Significance:** The paper is significant because it directly addresses a practical challenge faced by social scientists adopting LLMs for text analysis.  Many social science constructs are naturally continuous, yet directly applying LLMs for measurement can lead to unreliable and biased results. By offering a rigorous evaluation of different methodologies, the paper helps bridge the gap between advanced NLP techniques and the specific needs of social science research. The findings about the effectiveness of fine-tuning smaller models on limited data are particularly useful, as they offer a more computationally accessible alternative to relying solely on large, prompted LLMs.  The paper also correctly points out the problems that arise from simply adopting techniques from other fields (like LLM as a judge), without adequate consideration of the biases or specific task requirements.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough comparison of different methodologies (prompting strategies, fine-tuning techniques, and model sizes) on multiple datasets.
*   **Practical Recommendations:** The paper offers clear, actionable guidelines for researchers aiming to measure scalar constructs in social science.
*   **Emphasis on Real-World Data:** The use of datasets from existing social science studies enhances the paper's relevance and impact.
*   **Clear Identification of Pitfalls:** The paper highlights common problems, like heaped distributions and calibration issues, associated with using LLMs for scalar measurement.
*   **Open Source Data & Code**: Upon publication, the source code and datasets will be made available, increasing the impact and reproducibility of the paper.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Constructs:** While the paper uses diverse datasets, they all fall under the broader domain of political communication.  It is uncertain how well the lessons generalized to other fields (e.g., literature, psychology).
*   **Limited Exploration of Prompt Engineering:** While different prompting strategies were compared (pairwise vs. pointwise), there was minimal discussion on the prompt templates used, and more intricate CoT prompting was ignored.
*   **Lack of theoretical foundation for some observations:** Some of the observations, particularly regarding the superiority of certain models for particular tasks, are empirically supported but lack a robust theoretical explanation. While discussing known issues such as overconfidence in the prompt/model responses, the underlying causes need better analysis, particularly as it is connected to the observation of model size and capabilities.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence how social scientists use LLMs to measure scalar constructs, leading to more robust and reliable results. Its emphasis on fine-tuning smaller models could democratize access to these techniques. The identification of potential pitfalls could prevent researchers from drawing misleading conclusions.

**Justification for Score:**

This paper offers a valuable and timely contribution to the intersection of LLMs and social science. The rigorous evaluation of different methodologies and the clear identification of practical recommendations make it a useful resource for researchers. While the scope of constructs and prompt engineering could be broader, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### PromptCOS: Towards System Prompt Copyright Auditing for LLMs via Content-level Output Similarity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03117v1)
- **Authors**: Yuchen Yang, Yiming Li, Hongwei Yao, Enhao Huang, Shuo Shao, Bingrun Yang, Zhibo Wang, Dacheng Tao, Zhan Qin
- **Abstract**: The rapid progress of large language models (LLMs) has greatly enhanced reasoning tasks and facilitated the development of LLM-based applications. A critical factor in improving LLM-based applications is the design of effective system prompts, which significantly impact the behavior and output quality of LLMs. However, system prompts are susceptible to theft and misuse, which could undermine the interests of prompt owners. Existing methods protect prompt copyrights through watermark injection and verification but face challenges due to their reliance on intermediate LLM outputs (e.g., logits), which limits their practical feasibility. In this paper, we propose PromptCOS, a method for auditing prompt copyright based on content-level output similarity. It embeds watermarks by optimizing the prompt while simultaneously co-optimizing a special verification query and content-level signal marks. This is achieved by leveraging cyclic output signals and injecting auxiliary tokens to ensure reliable auditing in content-only scenarios. Additionally, it incorporates cover tokens to protect the watermark from malicious deletion. For copyright verification, PromptCOS identifies unauthorized usage by comparing the similarity between the suspicious output and the signal mark. Experimental results demonstrate that our method achieves high effectiveness (99.3% average watermark similarity), strong distinctiveness (60.8% greater than the best baseline), high fidelity (accuracy degradation of no more than 0.58%), robustness (resilience against three types of potential attacks), and computational efficiency (up to 98.1% reduction in computational cost). Our code is available at GitHub https://github.com/LianPing-cyber/PromptCOS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PromptCOS: Towards System Prompt Copyright Auditing for LLMs via Content-level Output Similarity":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the growing concern of system prompt copyright infringement in Large Language Models (LLMs). Existing watermarking techniques often rely on accessing intermediate LLM outputs (logits), which are not always available in real-world scenarios where only the final generated content is accessible. The authors propose PromptCOS, a novel content-only prompt auditing method. It embeds watermarks by optimizing the prompt, a verification query, and a signal mark (desired output). It uses cyclic output signals for effectiveness, auxiliary tokens for fidelity, and cover tokens for robustness against attacks. Copyright verification involves submitting the verification query to a suspicious application and comparing the generated output with the signal mark using a sliding window similarity calculation. Experimental results demonstrate the method's effectiveness, distinctiveness, fidelity, robustness, and computational efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in addressing prompt copyright auditing in a **content-only scenario**, a significant limitation of existing logits-based approaches. The specific techniques employed, such as cyclic output signals, auxiliary tokens, and cover tokens, while not entirely novel in isolation, are combined in a new and targeted way to achieve effective content-only watermarking.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it expands the applicability of prompt copyright protection to a wider range of real-world LLM applications where access to internal model states is restricted. Protecting system prompts as valuable intellectual property is increasingly important as LLMs become more prevalent. Addressing potential copyright issues enhances the ethical development of LLMs. The paper adequately motivates the problem and situates it within the context of existing limitations.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a practical limitation:**  The content-only scenario is a realistic and significant constraint.
    *   **Comprehensive approach:** PromptCOS tackles effectiveness, distinctiveness, fidelity, and robustness.
    *   **Strong experimental results:** The experimental evaluation is thorough, covering various LLMs, datasets, and attack scenarios, demonstrating the superiority of PromptCOS over baseline methods.
    *   **Detailed methodology:** The paper provides a clear and well-explained methodology, including optimization objectives and strategies.
    *   **Open Source Code:** The availability of the code increases the reproducibility and adoption.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The method involves multiple optimization steps and carefully tuned parameters, potentially making it difficult to implement and adapt for other use cases without significant effort.
    *   **Signal Mark limitations:** While the method improves upon existing baselines for protecting Copyrights, it does have known limitations. As the paper states itself, "although the signal mark can reliably distinguish watermarked outputs from normal ones through adaptive distinctiveness optimization, its ability to correspond to specific copyright information remains limited." Future work will need to address this challenge for greater signal fidelity.
    *   **Limited Attack Scenarios:** While robustness against several attacks is demonstrated, the attacks considered are relatively basic (adding redundant tokens, basic content modification). An evaluation against more sophisticated and adaptive attacks designed specifically to circumvent content-only watermarking would enhance the robustness assessment.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence future research in prompt copyright protection by shifting focus towards content-only solutions. It introduces a practical and effective approach that can be adopted and extended by others.  It could also stimulate research into more sophisticated attack and defense mechanisms for content-only watermarking.

*   **Score Rationale:** While the techniques are not revolutionary, the paper's contribution in addressing the practical content-only scenario, the comprehensive approach, the strong experimental validation, and the publicly available code make it a valuable advancement. There is room for improvement in the complexity of deployment and robusteness, therefore this paper will not receive a top score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Evaluation to Defense: Constructing Persistent Edit-Based Fingerprints for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03122v1)
- **Authors**: Yue Li, Xin Yi, Dongsheng Shi, Yongyi Cui, Gerard de Melo, Xiaoling Wang, Linlin Wang
- **Abstract**: The intellectual property (IP) protection of Large Language Models (LLMs) is increasingly critical. Injecting specialized fingerprints into LLMs through instruction tuning is a common IP protection technique. However, this may significantly degrade model performance, requires substantial computational resources, and exhibits poor persistence under model modifications. We argue that knowledge editing offers a lightweight alternative that is more suitable for fingerprint injection. Accordingly, we apply knowledge editing to fingerprint injection for the first time and demonstrate its strong capability. Despite using scrambled text as fingerprints to prevent them from being overwritten during fine-tuning, degradation still occurs under large-scale fine-tuning. To address this, we propose Fingerprint Subspace-aware Fine-Tuning (FSFT), which reduces fingerprint degradation by constraining the update of the fingerprint subspace. The performance of FSFT exceeds fine-tuning by 10% even in the worst-case scenario. Additionally, we observe that the fingerprint-injected models struggle to distinguish between fingerprints and similar texts due to the high similarity of their features. This finding underscores the urgent need for more robust and fine-grained fingerprinting injection methods for LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the intellectual property (IP) protection of Large Language Models (LLMs) by exploring knowledge editing as a lightweight and more persistent alternative to instruction tuning for fingerprint injection. They identify the limitations of fine-tuning-based methods (performance degradation, high resource requirements, and poor persistence) and propose using knowledge editing instead. To further improve the persistence of edit-based fingerprints under downstream fine-tuning, they introduce Fingerprint Subspace-aware Fine-Tuning (FSFT), which constrains updates to the fingerprint subspace. The paper presents experiments on Llama and Qwen models, comparing various fingerprinting injection methods across effectiveness, robustness, harmlessness, efficiency, and persistence.  The results indicate that knowledge editing methods, particularly RLEdit, are well-suited for fingerprint injection. They also show that FSFT significantly improves fingerprint persistence compared to standard fine-tuning. They further investigate the similarity between fingerprints and similar texts, revealing a need for finer-grained fingerprinting methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using knowledge editing for fingerprint injection in LLMs is indeed a novel and promising direction. While knowledge editing itself isn't new, its application to IP protection and fingerprinting is a significant contribution. Identifying and addressing the fingerprint subspace for persistence is also a novel approach.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical need in the LLM ecosystem: IP protection. By offering a lightweight and persistent fingerprinting method, it contributes to enabling ownership verification and reducing unauthorized model use. The FSFT technique is important for ensuring that fingerprints don't get easily erased during downstream adaptation of LLMs, which is common in real-world scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The problem addressed is highly relevant and timely.
    *   The approach (knowledge editing + FSFT) is well-motivated and offers a practical solution to the limitations of existing fine-tuning-based fingerprinting methods.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive, covering different LLMs, knowledge editing methods, and evaluation dimensions.
    *   The analysis of fingerprint robustness and the discovery of the fingerprint subspace provide valuable insights.
    *   The paper clearly defines the problem, the proposed solution, and the experimental setup.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The robustness evaluation shows that fingerprint injected models are still vulnerable to similar prompt. This area needs significant improvements to achieve better protection from other adversaries.
    *   The paper assumes a somewhat cooperative setting where model modifications are done through standard fine-tuning. A more adversarial scenario where an attacker *actively* tries to remove or overwrite the fingerprint is not fully addressed.
    *   While FSFT improves persistence, the experiments could benefit from more diverse downstream fine-tuning datasets and tasks to further test its generalizability.
    *   The visual analysis of the latent space reveals a significant weakness in differentiating adversarial texts. This is not addressed with a defense mechanism and requires more analysis and potentially a novel approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper can significantly influence the LLM IP protection research area. By promoting knowledge editing and FSFT, it provides practical solutions that researchers and practitioners can build upon. The analysis of fingerprint robustness and the characterization of the fingerprint subspace open new avenues for investigation and future research. It can also encourage the development of more robust and fine-grained fingerprinting injection methods.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper's novelty lies in applying knowledge editing for fingerprinting and introducing FSFT. The findings are significant because they offer a more efficient and persistent approach to LLM IP protection. While there are weaknesses, like robustness issues, the strengths of the paper outweigh them, especially considering its potential to influence the field. The paper needs improvement with regards to security, the latent representation analysis, and evaluation of broader downstream tasks.

### Adaptive KV-Cache Compression without Manually Setting Budget
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03136v1)
- **Authors**: Chenxia Tang, Jianchun Liu, Hongli Xu, Liusheng Huang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) inference relies heavily on KV-caches to accelerate autoregressive decoding, but the resulting memory footprint grows rapidly with sequence length, posing significant efficiency challenges. Current KV-cache compression methods suffer from a Procrustes' bed problem: they force diverse workloads into fixed compression ratios, leading to suboptimal resource allocation and inference performance. To this end, we present GVote, an adaptive KV-cache compression scheme that eliminates manual budget specification while achieving superior accuracy-efficiency trade-offs. GVote operates on the principle that the important keys are the aggregation of keys required by future queries. The method predicts future query attention demands by Monte-Carlo style sampling potential queries and aggregating selected keys to determine the optimal cache budget without manual specification. Experimental evaluation demonstrates GVote's effectiveness across multiple benchmarks, including GSM8K, RULER and Longbench. Compared to baselines, GVote exhibits 2$\times$ memory reduction while the accuracy maintains higher or comparable.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GVote, a novel KV-cache compression scheme for large language models (LLMs). Unlike existing methods that rely on manually setting a fixed compression ratio (a "Procrustes' bed problem"), GVote adaptively determines the optimal cache budget for each request. It operates by sampling potential future queries using a Monte Carlo approach based on the statistical distribution of hidden states and then aggregating the keys required by these sampled queries. This avoids the need for manual budget specification and improves the accuracy-efficiency trade-off compared to fixed-budget methods. The authors demonstrate GVote's effectiveness across various benchmarks, showing memory reduction with maintained or improved accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of adaptively determining the KV-cache budget per request based on predicted future query requirements is relatively novel.  Existing works focus primarily on optimizing *within* a given budget, rather than dynamically determining the budget itself. The use of Monte Carlo sampling and statistical properties of hidden states to estimate future queries is a clever approach. The formulation of the fixed-budget problem as a "Procrustes' bed" is a good framing of the limitations of previous works.

* **Significance:** KV-cache compression is a crucial area for improving the efficiency and scalability of LLM inference. GVote tackles a fundamental limitation of current approaches by eliminating the need for manual budget tuning, which is often a cumbersome and sub-optimal process.  If GVote delivers on its promise, it could significantly simplify the deployment of LLMs in resource-constrained environments. The empirical results presented in the paper, showing improved accuracy and memory usage compared to baselines, support the potential significance of the work.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Adaptive Budgeting:**  The primary strength is the adaptive nature of GVote, which eliminates the need for manual budget tuning and better handles diverse workloads.
    * **Principled Approach:**  The method is grounded in a principled approach based on estimating future query requirements and leveraging the statistical properties of hidden states.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper includes a comprehensive set of experiments across various benchmarks and models, demonstrating the effectiveness of GVote compared to state-of-the-art baselines. The analysis validating the synthetic query approach is also a notable strength.
    * **Clear Implementation:** The paper provides sufficient detail on the GVote algorithm and implementation. The discussion of the parameters is helpful.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Overhead:** While the paper mentions the one-time overhead during the prefill phase, a more detailed analysis of the computational cost of the sampling and aggregation process is needed.  How does the runtime overhead vary with the number of samples and the sequence length? This should be quantified.
    * **Parameter Sensitivity:** The sensitivity analysis of parameters `pnuc` and `S` could be more extensive.  While the paper provides recommendations, a more in-depth investigation of their impact on different types of tasks and models would be valuable. More details about the experiments shown in [ref] are also desirable.
    * **Limited Comparison with Oracle:** It would be useful to compare the performance of GVote against an "oracle" that has perfect knowledge of future query requirements (e.g., using the actual future queries for budget allocation). This would provide a more rigorous upper bound on the achievable performance.
    * **Gaussian Assumption:** The paper relies on the assumption that hidden states exhibit approximately Gaussian distributions. While the authors provide empirical evidence to support this assumption, it might not hold true for all models and tasks. How sensitive is GVote to deviations from this assumption?

* **Potential Influence:**  GVote has the potential to influence the field of LLM inference by providing a more practical and efficient approach to KV-cache compression. The adaptive budgeting strategy could be adopted by other researchers and practitioners, leading to further improvements in LLM deployment.

**Overall:**

GVote presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of KV-cache compression.  The adaptive budgeting strategy addresses a key limitation of existing methods, and the empirical results demonstrate its effectiveness.  While there are some limitations regarding the overhead analysis and parameter sensitivity, the overall quality of the paper is high.

### Temporally-Aware Diffusion Model for Brain Progression Modelling with Bidirectional Temporal Regularisation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03141v1)
- **Authors**: Mattia Litrico, Francesco Guarnera, Mario Valerio Giuffrida, Daniele Ravì, Sebastiano Battiato
- **Abstract**: Generating realistic MRIs to accurately predict future changes in the structure of brain is an invaluable tool for clinicians in assessing clinical outcomes and analysing the disease progression at the patient level. However, current existing methods present some limitations: (i) some approaches fail to explicitly capture the relationship between structural changes and time intervals, especially when trained on age-imbalanced datasets; (ii) others rely only on scan interpolation, which lack clinical utility, as they generate intermediate images between timepoints rather than future pathological progression; and (iii) most approaches rely on 2D slice-based architectures, thereby disregarding full 3D anatomical context, which is essential for accurate longitudinal predictions. We propose a 3D Temporally-Aware Diffusion Model (TADM-3D), which accurately predicts brain progression on MRI volumes. To better model the relationship between time interval and brain changes, TADM-3D uses a pre-trained Brain-Age Estimator (BAE) that guides the diffusion model in the generation of MRIs that accurately reflect the expected age difference between baseline and generated follow-up scans. Additionally, to further improve the temporal awareness of TADM-3D, we propose the Back-In-Time Regularisation (BITR), by training TADM-3D to predict bidirectionally from the baseline to follow-up (forward), as well as from the follow-up to baseline (backward). Although predicting past scans has limited clinical applications, this regularisation helps the model generate temporally more accurate scans. We train and evaluate TADM-3D on the OASIS-3 dataset, and we validate the generalisation performance on an external test set from the NACC dataset. The code will be available upon acceptance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel 3D Temporally-Aware Diffusion Model (TADM-3D) for predicting future brain MRI scans, focusing on modeling brain progression. The core idea is to predict the voxel-wise intensity *differences* between baseline and follow-up scans, rather than generating complete future scans directly. To improve temporal awareness, the model conditions on the age *difference* between scans (rather than absolute age), uses a pre-trained Brain-Age Estimator (BAE) to guide the diffusion process, and employs a Back-In-Time Regularization (BITR) technique where the model is trained to predict both forward and backward in time. The model is trained and tested on the OASIS-3 dataset and validated on an external NACC dataset. Results show improved performance compared to existing methods on image-based and volumetric metrics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has several aspects of novelty, combining techniques to address limitations in existing methods.

    *   The focus on predicting *intensity differences* rather than full scans is a sensible approach for mitigating artifact generation.
    *   Conditioning on the *age difference* between scans is a useful strategy to handle age-imbalanced datasets, a common challenge in longitudinal studies.
    *   The BAE integration to guide the diffusion model adds a useful constraint for temporal consistency.
    *   BITR, while conceptually simple, offers a potential way to enforce better temporal dynamics.

    However, the individual components (diffusion models, brain age estimation, UNet architectures) are not novel in themselves. The main novelty lies in the *combination* of these techniques and the specific architecture design and training methodology.

*   **Significance:** Accurate brain progression models have significant clinical potential for diagnostics, prognostics, and personalized treatment planning in neurodegenerative diseases. The paper claims improved performance compared to state-of-the-art approaches, indicating that this method could contribute to the field. The use of 3D volumes rather than 2D slices is a significant factor for achieving realistic results, and addressing the age-imbalance in available datasets is an important advance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive experiments: The paper presents results on both an internal and an external dataset, providing evidence for generalizability.
    *   Ablation Studies: The ablation studies are well-designed to assess the impact of each component of the model, strengthening the validity of the proposed techniques.
    *   Clear presentation: The method is well-described, and the results are clearly presented in tables and figures.
    *   Addresses Practical Issue: Targeting age-imbalanced datasets highlights the practical relevance of the approach.
    *   Open-Source Implementation: Access to code contributes to the reproducibility and adoption of the technique in the research community.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Incremental Novelty: The novelty, although present, is primarily incremental building on existing techniques rather than a radical departure. The main novelty lies in *how* they are combined.
    *   Limited Scope of Data Modalities: The method currently only uses structural MRI, potentially limiting its ability to capture more complex disease progression. Incorporating other modalities (e.g., genetics, cognitive scores, other imaging modalities) could be beneficial.
    *   Limited Discussion of Limitations: While mentioned, the limitations related to long-term prediction and lack of other clinical factors could be discussed more extensively. The inconsistency shown in Figure 3 is important and warrants further discussion.
    *   Limited analysis of failure cases.
    *   Lack of statistical significance measures

*   **Potential Influence:** If the performance gains demonstrated in the paper hold up in further studies and clinical applications, this method could be a valuable tool for researchers and clinicians working on neurodegenerative diseases.

*   **Concerns and Potential Improvements:**
    *   Provide more detailed analysis on where and when it fails.
    *   Address inconsistencies in long-time predictions.
    *   Include more clinical factors in the model.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of brain progression modeling. While the individual components of the method are not entirely novel, the specific combination and training strategy, the 3D architecture, the handling of age imbalance, and the improvements over existing methods justify a solid score. The strengths of the paper (comprehensive experiments, ablation studies) outweigh its weaknesses (incremental novelty, limited scope of data modalities). The proposed method has clear potential for impact in clinical applications.

### Domain Adaptation of LLMs for Process Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03161v1)
- **Authors**: Rafael Seidi Oyamada, Jari Peeperkorn, Jochen De Weerdt, Johannes De Smedt
- **Abstract**: In recent years, Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as a prominent area of interest across various research domains, including Process Mining (PM). Current applications in PM have predominantly centered on prompt engineering strategies or the transformation of event logs into narrative-style datasets, thereby exploiting the semantic capabilities of LLMs to address diverse tasks. In contrast, this study investigates the direct adaptation of pretrained LLMs to process data without natural language reformulation, motivated by the fact that these models excel in generating sequences of tokens, similar to the objective in PM. More specifically, we focus on parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniques to mitigate the computational overhead typically associated with such models. Our experimental setup focuses on Predictive Process Monitoring (PPM), and considers both single- and multi-task predictions. The results demonstrate a potential improvement in predictive performance over state-of-the-art recurrent neural network (RNN) approaches and recent narrative-style-based solutions, particularly in the multi-task setting. Additionally, our fine-tuned models exhibit faster convergence and require significantly less hyperparameter optimization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the domain adaptation of Large Language Models (LLMs) for process data, specifically within the context of Predictive Process Monitoring (PPM).  Rather than relying on prompt engineering or transforming event logs into natural language narratives (which are common approaches), the study focuses on directly fine-tuning pre-trained LLMs using parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) techniques.  The authors replace the language-based tokenization with a task-specific tokenization of activity labels and evaluate different PEFT methods in both single-task (next activity, remaining time prediction separately) and multi-task (both jointly) prediction scenarios. The results show that fine-tuned LLMs can achieve improved predictive performance compared to traditional RNN-based methods and recent narrative-style approaches, especially in the multi-task setting, with faster convergence and reduced hyperparameter optimization requirements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces a valuable and novel approach by directly adapting LLMs to structured process data without relying on natural language intermediaries.  This is a departure from the common practice of prompt engineering or transforming process data into narrative forms for LLM consumption. The exploration of PEFT techniques, such as LoRA, for this specific application is also a significant contribution. The paper moves beyond simply applying LLMs to process mining tasks; it investigates how to best adapt them to the unique characteristics of process data.  However, the novelty is somewhat limited by the fact that other papers have used LLMs for PPM, but in different setups.

* **Significance:** The potential significance of this work is substantial. If LLMs can be effectively fine-tuned for PPM tasks, it could lead to more accurate and flexible predictive models. The reduced hyperparameter optimization burden and faster convergence times offered by the presented approach can substantially reduce the time and resources required for building and deploying PPM systems. The paper addresses the limitations of current LLM applications in PM, which often rely on narrative-style datasets. By adapting LLMs directly to the structured process data, this paper enhances PPM accuracy and flexibility.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing approaches and motivates the need for domain adaptation.
    * **Well-Defined Methodology:** The methodology is clearly described, including the components (input, backbone, output layers, PEFT), the chosen LLMs, the datasets, and the experimental setup.
    * **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper performs a systematic evaluation of different PEFT techniques and compares them to strong baselines.
    * **Insightful Results:** The results are insightful, highlighting the potential benefits of LLMs for PPM but also revealing their limitations (e.g., difficulty with regression tasks in their original form).
    * **Comprehensive Hyperparameter Optimization:** The implementation of a grid search methodology ensures that the most reliable and efficient parameters are used, thus resulting in a robust and insightful validation of the results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The study focuses primarily on next activity and remaining time prediction. It would be valuable to investigate other PPM tasks (e.g., outcome prediction, anomaly detection).
    * **Open-Source Models Only:**  Due to the need for model access for fine-tuning, the study is limited to open-source LLMs. It would be interesting to see if larger, closed-source models could further improve performance, though this is a practical limitation.
    * **Hyperparameter Optimization for RNNs:** The paper mentions extensive hyperparameter tuning for RNNs but uses mostly default settings or LoRA's recommended ones for LLMs. A more comprehensive hyperparameter search for the LLMs might reveal even better performance.
    * **Narrative Prompting Inconsistencies**: The narrative dataset approach was slightly inconsistent compared to the other models.
* **Potential Impact:** The paper opens up new avenues for research in PPM and could potentially influence the development of more effective process mining tools and techniques. The findings highlight the importance of domain adaptation for LLMs and suggest that direct fine-tuning can be a viable alternative to prompt engineering and narrative-style approaches.

* **Justification for Score:** The paper demonstrates significant novelty and contributes valuable insights to the application of LLMs in process mining. It is well-written, with a clear methodology and convincing results. Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper merits a score that acknowledges its contributions while recognizing its limitations.

### SinhalaMMLU: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Evaluating Multitask Language Understanding in Sinhala
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03162v1)
- **Authors**: Ashmari Pramodya, Nirasha Nelki, Heshan Shalinda, Chamila Liyanage, Yusuke Sakai, Randil Pushpananda, Ruvan Weerasinghe, Hidetaka Kamigaito, Taro Watanabe
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) demonstrate impressive general knowledge and reasoning abilities, yet their evaluation has predominantly focused on global or anglocentric subjects, often neglecting low-resource languages and culturally specific content. While recent multilingual benchmarks attempt to bridge this gap, many rely on automatic translation, which can introduce errors and misrepresent the original cultural context. To address this, we introduce SinhalaMMLU, the first multiple-choice question answering benchmark designed specifically for Sinhala, a low-resource language. The dataset includes over 7,000 questions spanning secondary to collegiate education levels, aligned with the Sri Lankan national curriculum, and covers six domains and 30 subjects, encompassing both general academic topics and culturally grounded knowledge. We evaluate 26 LLMs on SinhalaMMLU and observe that, while Claude 3.5 sonnet and GPT-4o achieve the highest average accuracies at 67% and 62% respectively, overall model performance remains limited. In particular, models struggle in culturally rich domains such as the Humanities, revealing substantial room for improvement in adapting LLMs to low-resource and culturally specific contexts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SinhalaMMLU, a new comprehensive benchmark specifically designed to evaluate multitask language understanding in Sinhala, a low-resource language. This benchmark comprises over 7,000 multiple-choice questions spanning six domains and 30 subjects, aligning with the Sri Lankan national curriculum from secondary to collegiate education levels. The authors evaluate 26 different LLMs on this benchmark, revealing that while certain models (Claude 3.5 Sonnet and GPT-4o) achieve relatively high accuracy, overall performance remains limited, especially in culturally rich domains like the Humanities. The paper highlights the importance of culturally relevant benchmarks for low-resource languages and identifies areas for improvement in adapting LLMs to these contexts. The dataset is publicly available on GitHub and Hugging Face. They explored the use of varying levels of difficult of questions and observed the responses of different models. The team also explored questions framed with negations and suboptions and compared their efficiency. The paper concluded with a cultural knowledge analysis as well.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key strength of this paper lies in its creation of a new benchmark tailored to a specific low-resource language and cultural context. Existing benchmarks often rely on translations, which can be problematic for nuanced understanding and cultural sensitivity. Creating a dataset from Sri Lankan national and provincial exams makes the benchmark more culturally appropriate. In that sense, SinhalaMMLU fills a genuine gap. The dataset is unique and contextually designed.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant as they demonstrate the limitations of existing LLMs in understanding and reasoning about culturally specific knowledge within low-resource languages. The paper underscores the need for benchmarks that go beyond mere translation and capture the cultural nuances of a language. The analysis of LLM performance across different domains and difficulty levels offers valuable insights for future research in culturally aware NLP. SinhalaMMLU has potential to contribute towards developing LLMs that perform better on low-resource languages, ultimately promoting inclusivity in NLP.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The dataset is well-motivated by the identified gap in the evaluation of LLMs for low-resource languages and cultural understanding.
    *   The data creation process, using native Sri Lankan exams, ensures relevance and cultural appropriateness.
    *   The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of a variety of LLMs, offering valuable performance comparisons.
    *   The analysis of performance across domains and difficulty levels offers insightful observations and directions for future research.
    *   Public availability of the dataset promotes further research and development.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the alignment with the national curriculum is a strength, it also limits the scope to the specific educational context of Sri Lanka. This might impact the generalizability of the benchmark to other Sinhala-speaking communities or broader cultural understanding.
    *   The analysis is primarily quantitative. While this provides a good overview, a more in-depth qualitative error analysis would be beneficial. For instance, analysis of why the models fail in particular culturally sensitive questions can be included.
    *   The human evaluation aspect is noted as a limitation. Given the culturally nuanced nature of the benchmark, a human evaluation component could bolster its validity.
    *   The method to asses the questions with negations and sub options is very basic, with string matching. Considering the complexity of language, it is possible that the method might have not captured all of the nuances.

*   **Potential Influence:** SinhalaMMLU can influence the research community by:

    *   Encouraging the development of LLMs specifically designed for Sinhala and other low-resource languages.
    *   Motivating the creation of culturally relevant benchmarks for other languages.
    *   Providing a valuable resource for researchers interested in evaluating and improving the cultural understanding capabilities of LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 7.  The creation of SinhalaMMLU is a substantial contribution to the field, filling a gap and promoting more inclusive and culturally sensitive NLP. The paper's detailed analysis provides valuable insights into the capabilities and limitations of current LLMs in this context. The primary weakness is the limited qualitative error analysis and lack of direct human evaluation which reduces the impact of the findings. While the paper contributes significantly to the field, the limitations prevent a higher score.

### OPRA-Vis: Visual Analytics System to Assist Organization-Public Relationship Assessment with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03164v1)
- **Authors**: Sangbong Yoo, Seongbum Seo, Chanyoung Yoon, Hyelim Lee, Jeong-Nam Kim, Chansoo Kim, Yun Jang, Takanori Fujiwara
- **Abstract**: Analysis of public opinions collected from digital media helps organizations maintain positive relationships with the public. Such public relations (PR) analysis often involves assessing opinions, for example, measuring how strongly people trust an organization. Pre-trained Large Language Models (LLMs) hold great promise for supporting Organization-Public Relationship Assessment (OPRA) because they can map unstructured public text to OPRA dimensions and articulate rationales through prompting. However, adapting LLMs for PR analysis typically requires fine-tuning on large labeled datasets, which is both labor-intensive and knowledge-intensive, making it difficult for PR researchers to apply these models. In this paper, we present OPRA-Vis, a visual analytics system that leverages LLMs for OPRA without requiring extensive labeled data. Our framework employs Chain-of-Thought prompting to guide LLMs in analyzing public opinion data by incorporating PR expertise directly into the reasoning process. Furthermore, OPRA-Vis provides visualizations that reveal the clues and reasoning paths used by LLMs, enabling users to explore, critique, and refine model decisions. We demonstrate the effectiveness of OPRA-Vis through two real-world use cases and evaluate it quantitatively, through comparisons with alternative LLMs and prompting strategies, and qualitatively, through assessments of usability, effectiveness, and expert feedback.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OPRA-Vis, a visual analytics system designed to assist public relations (PR) experts in assessing organization-public relationships using large language models (LLMs). OPRA-Vis leverages LLMs (specifically Gemma, with BERT for sentiment analysis) and Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting to analyze public opinion data.  A key feature is incorporating PR expertise directly into the LLM reasoning process through carefully designed prompts and visualizations. The system addresses the challenge of limited labeled data in PR by using few-shot examples (FSEs) and providing interactive visualizations that expose the LLM's reasoning, allowing users to refine model decisions. The system comprises three interconnected views: OPRA Concept Space, Data Management, and LLM Reasoning & Editing. The paper evaluates OPRA-Vis using real-world datasets (Amazon, Google Local, IMDB reviews), comparing it to baseline LLMs and prompting strategies.  Qualitative assessments through expert feedback and usability tests are also presented.  The results indicate OPRA-Vis can effectively integrate PR theory, improve labeling accuracy compared to human coding (in some dimensions), and provides valuable insights for understanding and correcting LLM reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable contribution by integrating visual analytics with LLMs and PR theory to address a practical problem: assessing organization-public relationships without relying on extensive labeled data. While LLMs and CoT prompting are not new, their application to PR analysis, coupled with interactive visualization for understanding and refining LLM reasoning, is a novel combination. The continuous encoding of CoC values and their use in a gravity model visualization is also a unique design element.

*   **Significance:** The system holds promise for improving PR practices by providing a more efficient and insightful way to analyze public opinion. By making LLM reasoning transparent and allowing experts to incorporate their knowledge, OPRA-Vis can help overcome the limitations of black-box models. The tool addresses the current scarcity of AI/ML application in PR, paving the way for a better understanding of public expectation, leading to better organizational policy and legitimacy management.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem addressed:** Tackles a significant issue in PR research—the need for scalable and theoretically grounded analysis of public opinion data.
    *   **Integration of theory and practice:** Effectively combines PR theory (OPRA) with state-of-the-art NLP techniques (LLMs, CoT prompting).
    *   **Emphasis on explainability:** The system prioritizes transparency by visualizing LLM reasoning and allowing user intervention.
    *   **Rigorous evaluation:** The paper includes quantitative comparisons with baselines, qualitative usability tests, and expert feedback.
    *   **Practical Application:** Demonstration of the utility with real-world dataset and real-use scenarios.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** While the framework is promising, application is limited to OPRA.
    *   **Dependence on Prompt Engineering:** The system's performance relies heavily on the quality of the prompts. Creating effective FSEs requires expertise in both PR and LLMs, which may pose a barrier to adoption. The system addresses this to some degree by allowing prompt editing, but the iterative process of refinement may still be time-consuming.
    *   **Limited generalizability:** It needs more extensive testing on different kinds of data.
    *   **Small User Study Population:** The usability study uses a fairly small number of participants, limiting the generalizability of its findings. A larger and more diverse user group would provide stronger evidence of the system's usability.
    *   **Diminished Control Mutuality Accuracy** There is some evidence of unintended consequence of applying LLM/Viz solutions, highlighted by diminished performance on Control Mutuality and the inherent bias in selection of examples.

*   **Potential Influence:** If widely adopted, OPRA-Vis could significantly change how PR professionals conduct public opinion research, leading to more informed strategies and improved relationships between organizations and their publics. The paper's focus on explainability and user control could also influence the design of other AI-assisted tools in various domains.

*   **Score Justification:** The paper presents a novel and well-evaluated system that addresses a practical problem in a theoretically grounded way. The emphasis on explainability and user control is particularly valuable. However, the limitations related to prompt engineering, scope, and a small user study temper the overall assessment. The potential for influence in the field is high, but requires broader testing and further development.
