### AIVA: An AI-based Virtual Companion for Emotion-aware Interaction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03212v1)
- **Authors**: Chenxi Li
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have significantly improved natural language understanding and generation, enhancing Human-Computer Interaction (HCI). However, LLMs are limited to unimodal text processing and lack the ability to interpret emotional cues from non-verbal signals, hindering more immersive and empathetic interactions. This work explores integrating multimodal sentiment perception into LLMs to create emotion-aware agents. We propose \ours, an AI-based virtual companion that captures multimodal sentiment cues, enabling emotionally aligned and animated HCI. \ours introduces a Multimodal Sentiment Perception Network (MSPN) using a cross-modal fusion transformer and supervised contrastive learning to provide emotional cues. Additionally, we develop an emotion-aware prompt engineering strategy for generating empathetic responses and integrate a Text-to-Speech (TTS) system and animated avatar module for expressive interactions. \ours provides a framework for emotion-aware agents with applications in companion robotics, social care, mental health, and human-centered AI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AIVA, an AI-based virtual companion designed for emotion-aware interaction. AIVA integrates a Multimodal Sentiment Perception Network (MSPN) with a large language model (LLM) to capture and understand users' emotional states from both textual and visual cues.  MSPN employs a cross-modal fusion transformer and prototype-level supervised contrastive learning for fine-grained emotion recognition. The extracted emotional cues are then incorporated into the LLM through Emotion-aware Prompt Engineering (EPE), allowing AIVA to generate contextually appropriate and empathetic responses. A Text-to-Speech (TTS) system and animated avatar module (Live2D) further enhance the interaction by providing expressive verbal and visual feedback. The paper presents experimental results validating both the sentiment recognition performance of MSPN and the empathetic interaction capabilities of the complete AIVA framework on several multimodal sentiment analysis datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the *integration* of existing technologies (multimodal sentiment analysis and LLMs) into a cohesive, functioning virtual companion. While the components themselves (e.g., cross-modal transformers, sentiment classification, prompt engineering) aren't necessarily groundbreaking in isolation, the *combination* and the specific way they are orchestrated to achieve emotion-aware HCI present a valuable contribution.

    *   **Strengths:** The MSPN architecture seems well-designed for multimodal fusion.  The integration of sentiment prototypes and supervised contrastive learning is a smart approach for fine-grained emotion recognition. The emotion-aware prompt engineering strategy also is a useful addition that enables LLMs to produce empathetic responses.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The core MSPN architecture, though described, does not include extensive comparisons with state-of-the-art multimodal sentiment analysis architectures, which limits the claim of significant advancements over existing methods. Without more detailed comparisons, it is difficult to assess the true contribution of the MSPN itself, versus just its inclusion in AIVA.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its potential applications in various domains, including companion robotics, social care, and mental health.  The ability to create AI agents capable of understanding and responding to human emotions in a natural and empathetic way has the potential to significantly improve user experiences and promote human well-being.

    *   **Strengths:** The paper clearly articulates the potential impact of AIVA on creating more human-centered AI applications. The demonstration of the complete system with TTS and an animated avatar strengthens the argument for its practical usability.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The experimental evaluation, while providing evidence of the system's functionality, could be more comprehensive.  Specifically, the qualitative results are limited, and there are no explicit human subject tests or user studies to evaluate the effectiveness of the complete AIVA system on various tasks. It's unclear whether users perceive it as truly empathetic, and how it compares to other virtual assistants regarding user satisfaction.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** While the integration is useful, the MSPN and the prompt engineering strategies, while beneficial, may not fully justify the high score. A more in-depth ablation study on the MSPN or a comparison with alternative multimodal sentiment analysis models would enhance the analysis and make this contribution stronger. Additional human subject testing is needed to improve confidence in AIVA's capabilities.

**Score: 6.5/10**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a well-integrated framework, AIVA, which successfully combines multimodal sentiment analysis with LLMs to create an emotion-aware virtual companion. The MSPN architecture, incorporating cross-modal fusion and prototype-based learning, is a novel contribution. However, it lacks more thorough experimental comparisons. AIVA's design, integration of components, and clear articulation of its potential impacts within HCI (and other fields such as robotics, social care, and mental health) are notable. The key weakness is a lack of more rigorous evaluation (especially more user studies) of the entire AIVA system in terms of its overall perceived empathy.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Exploring persuasive Interactions with generative social robots: An experimental framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03231v1)
- **Authors**: Stephan Vonschallen, Larissa Julia Corina Finsler, Theresa Schmiedel, Friederike Eyssel
- **Abstract**: Integrating generative AI such as large language models into social robots has improved their ability to engage in natural, human-like communication. This study presents a method to examine their persuasive capabilities. We designed an experimental framework focused on decision making and tested it in a pilot that varied robot appearance and self-knowledge. Using qualitative analysis, we evaluated interaction quality, persuasion effectiveness, and the robot's communicative strategies. Participants generally experienced the interaction positively, describing the robot as competent, friendly, and supportive, while noting practical limits such as delayed responses and occasional speech-recognition errors. Persuasiveness was highly context dependent and shaped by robot behavior: participants responded well to polite, reasoned suggestions and expressive gestures, but emphasized the need for more personalized, context-aware arguments and clearer social roles. These findings suggest that generative social robots can influence user decisions, but their effectiveness depends on communicative nuance and contextual relevance. We propose refinements to the framework to further study persuasive dynamics between robots and human users.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the potential of Generative Social Robots (GSRs), powered by Large Language Models (LLMs), to persuade humans. It introduces a novel experimental framework for studying persuasive interactions between humans and GSRs, focusing on decision-making tasks across three scenarios (donation, education, nutrition). A pilot study was conducted to validate the framework's feasibility and gain initial insights into robot persuasion effectiveness and behavior. The study varied robot appearance (clothing) and self-knowledge configuration. Results suggest that GSRs can influence resource allocation decisions, with user compliance varying by scenario and potentially influenced by robot self-knowledge. The robot primarily used reasoned arguments, and participants generally had positive perceptions of the interaction, although technical limitations were noted. The paper concludes by outlining potential improvements to the framework and suggesting avenues for future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel and significant contribution by offering a framework designed to evaluate the persuasive dynamics in GSR interactions. The methodology is novel because it moves beyond pre-scripted interactions common in traditional HRI studies by incorporating LLM-generated dialogue and decision tasks, which is new within a persuasion research context. The adaptation of the Gazali et al.'s framework by including additional scenarios, fictitious charities, and autonomous LLM persuasion strategies enhance the approach of examining persuasion effectiveness. Moreover, the paper's use of resource allocation as an objective measure of persuasion effects contributes to innovative research practices.

**Significance:** The significance of this work lies in addressing a gap in the HRI literature by specifically investigating persuasion effectiveness in GSRs. It acknowledges the ethical implications of persuasive AI, like deception and manipulation and aims to provide methodological tools for further study. This is especially relevant given the increasing integration of LLMs into social robots. The results from the pilot study, while limited by sample size, provide valuable insights into the factors influencing robot persuasiveness, such as contextual relevance, robot self-knowledge, and the perceived qualities of the robot, such as trustworthiness and sympathy. The framework provides a practical tool for researchers to conduct controlled experiments, paving the way for a deeper understanding of the persuasive capabilities of GSRs and their impact on human behavior.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Framework:** The experimental framework is well-designed, adapting existing methodologies to the specific challenges and opportunities presented by GSRs.
*   **Clear Research Questions:** The study addresses clear and relevant research questions related to the effectiveness and mechanics of persuasion in human-GSR interactions.
*   **Pilot Study and Qualitative Approach:** The pilot study offers valuable initial insights, and the qualitative analysis provides a nuanced understanding of participant perceptions and robot behavior.
*   **Focus on Ethical Considerations:** The paper acknowledges the ethical implications of persuasive AI, demonstrating a responsible approach to the research.
*   **Practical Implications:** The findings can inform the design of GSRs for various applications, such as education, healthcare, and customer service, while also promoting ethical considerations in their development.
*   **Comprehensive Data Analysis:** The paper conducts detailed analyses of quantitative and qualitative data using a well-defined content analysis approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Sample Size:** The small sample size of the pilot study limits the generalizability of the findings and the possibility of statistical inferences.
*   **Technological Constraints:** Technical limitations of the Pepper robot (speech recognition errors, long response times) impacted the quality of interactions.
*   **Lack of Randomization:** Scenarios were not randomized, which may have introduced order effects.
*   **Potential Bias:** Participants lacked experience with HRI, which could lead to initial uncertainty that biased the study.
*   **Limited Persuasion Analysis:** A more in-depth analysis of the persuasive strategies employed by the LLM, beyond simple reasoning, would strengthen the study.
*   **Robot Personality and Persuasiveness:** Lack of the incorporation of a robot personality into the LLM, limiting a deeper exploration of the persuasiveness of GSRs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by introducing a novel experimental framework and providing initial insights into the complex dynamics of persuasive interactions with GSRs. While the pilot study has limitations, the framework's design, the clear research questions, and the focus on ethical considerations make it a promising tool for future research. The pilot study also produced tangible initial insights into how a robot's features can affect the persuasion capabilities of the robot. However, the study is also held back due to a small sample size, technological challenges, and other limitations.

Therefore, based on the novelty of the approach, the significance of the topic, and its potential impact on future research, the following score is proposed.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### TeRA: Vector-based Random Tensor Network for High-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03234v1)
- **Authors**: Yuxuan Gu, Wuyang Zhou, Giorgos Iacovides, Danilo Mandic
- **Abstract**: Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) methods, such as Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), have significantly reduced the number of trainable parameters needed in fine-tuning large language models (LLMs). Subsequent developments of LoRA-style adapters have diverged into two main directions: (1) enhancing model expressivity with high-rank adapters, and (2) pushing for further parameter reduction, as exemplified by vector-based methods. However, these approaches present a trade-off, as achieving the expressivity of high-rank weight updates typically comes at the cost of sacrificing the extreme parameter efficiency offered by vector-based techniques. To address this issue, we propose a vector-based random \underline{\textbf{Te}}nsor network for high-\underline{\textbf{R}}ank \underline{\textbf{A}}daptation (TeRA), a novel PEFT method that achieves high-rank weight updates while retaining the parameter efficiency of vector-based PEFT adapters. This is achieved by parameterizing the tensorized weight update matrix as a Tucker-like tensor network (TN), in which large randomly initialized factors are frozen and shared across layers, while only small layer-specific scaling vectors, formed by entries in diagonal factor matrices, are trained. This design effectively decouples the rank of the weight update matrix from the number of trainable parameters. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that TeRA matches or even outperforms high-rank adapters, while requiring a trainable parameter count similar to vector-based methods. Theoretical analysis and ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of our approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TeRA (Vector-based Random Tensor Network for High-Rank Adaptation), a novel Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) method for adapting large language models (LLMs). TeRA aims to address the trade-off between expressivity (high-rank weight updates) and parameter efficiency in existing PEFT methods like LoRA and VeRA.  TeRA achieves this by representing the weight update matrix as a Tucker-like tensor network. The key idea is to freeze large, randomly initialized factors within this tensor network and only train small, layer-specific scaling vectors. This decoupling allows for high-rank updates with a significantly smaller number of trainable parameters, comparable to vector-based methods.  The paper presents theoretical analysis, ablation studies, and experimental results on commonsense reasoning and generation tasks, demonstrating TeRA's superior performance compared to existing methods in terms of both accuracy and parameter efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of using a Tucker-like tensor network with frozen factors and trainable scaling vectors to parameterize weight updates is relatively novel in the context of PEFT. While tensor networks have been explored in other domains of machine learning, their application to PEFT with this particular design appears to be a contribution.  The decoupling of rank and parameter count is a compelling aspect.  However, the novelty is somewhat incremental, building upon existing LoRA-style adapters and tensor decomposition techniques.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in addressing a crucial trade-off in PEFT methods: achieving high expressivity without sacrificing parameter efficiency. TeRA demonstrates a promising approach to achieve both, making it potentially valuable for resource-constrained environments and applications requiring complex adaptation. The experimental results on commonsense reasoning tasks (Llama-3) are particularly compelling, showcasing TeRA's competitive accuracy with high-rank methods while maintaining a small parameter footprint. However, the paper would be strengthened by broader experiments on additional tasks like classification, summarization, and natural language inference.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The core idea of decoupling rank and parameter count using a tensor network is well-motivated.
    *   The theoretical analysis provides some guarantees about the rank of the weight update matrix and the trade-off between parameter efficiency and approximation error.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of TeRA across multiple tasks and model architectures.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the impact of different design choices, like tensorization schemes and initialization methods.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The theoretical analysis, while present, is not deeply profound and could be expanded to provide stronger guarantees.
    *   The experimental evaluation could be more comprehensive. The focus on commonsense reasoning and personalized dialogue generation is somewhat limited. Including a broader range of tasks and datasets would further strengthen the claims.
    *   Comparison with some other state-of-the-art high rank adapters is limited.
    *   While the paper mentions zero inference overhead, more detailed analysis of latency with TeRA would be beneficial.
*   **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the methodology, theoretical analysis, and experimental results. The diagrams and figures are helpful in understanding the core concepts.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7.5 to this paper. The novelty is present but incremental, and the significance is tied to addressing a practical challenge in PEFT. The strengths lie in the well-motivated design, decent theoretical analysis, and compelling experimental results. The limitations are primarily in the scope of the experimental evaluation and the depth of the theoretical analysis. While the paper provides a useful contribution, the improvement over the existing best adapter isn't significant. More diverse experiments and enhanced theoretical grounding would be required for a higher score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### SynBT: High-quality Tumor Synthesis for Breast Tumor Segmentation by 3D Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03267v1)
- **Authors**: Hongxu Yang, Edina Timko, Levente Lippenszky, Vanda Czipczer, Lehel Ferenczi
- **Abstract**: Synthetic tumors in medical images offer controllable characteristics that facilitate the training of machine learning models, leading to an improved segmentation performance. However, the existing methods of tumor synthesis yield suboptimal performances when tumor occupies a large spatial volume, such as breast tumor segmentation in MRI with a large field-of-view (FOV), while commonly used tumor generation methods are based on small patches. In this paper, we propose a 3D medical diffusion model, called SynBT, to generate high-quality breast tumor (BT) in contrast-enhanced MRI images. The proposed model consists of a patch-to-volume autoencoder, which is able to compress the high-resolution MRIs into compact latent space, while preserving the resolution of volumes with large FOV. Using the obtained latent space feature vector, a mask-conditioned diffusion model is used to synthesize breast tumors within selected regions of breast tissue, resulting in realistic tumor appearances. We evaluated the proposed method for a tumor segmentation task, which demonstrated the proposed high-quality tumor synthesis method can facilitate the common segmentation models with performance improvement of 2-3% Dice Score on a large public dataset, and therefore provides benefits for tumor segmentation in MRI images.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SynBT, a 3D diffusion model for synthesizing high-quality breast tumor (BT) images in contrast-enhanced MRI.  The proposed model employs a patch-to-volume autoencoder to compress high-resolution MRIs into a compact latent space. A mask-conditioned diffusion model is then used to generate realistic breast tumors within specified regions. The paper evaluates the method on a tumor segmentation task, demonstrating improved performance (2-3% Dice score) on a public dataset. The authors argue that SynBT enables controllable tumor characteristics, facilitating the training of machine learning models for improved segmentation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the specific application of a diffusion model framework to breast tumor synthesis within contrast-enhanced MRI images and the focus on maintaining a large field-of-view. While diffusion models themselves are not new, their application to this specific medical imaging task, particularly with the patch-to-volume autoencoder component, presents a reasonably novel configuration. Existing tumor synthesis methods often focus on smaller patches or other modalities, so addressing the large FOV requirement of breast MRI is a practical contribution. The two-step VQ-VAE implementation can also be considered a novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate. Synthesizing medical images to augment training data is a well-established technique.  The benefit of this work is the potential to improve breast tumor segmentation, a clinically relevant task. The reported Dice score improvement of 2-3% is a positive, but perhaps not revolutionary, result. The value lies in the practical improvements to a standard task. Providing a synthetic data pipeline for training segmentation models is an important contribution and addresses the challenges faced by researchers and clinicians.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a practical problem (breast tumor segmentation).
    *   Demonstrates improved performance on a public dataset.
    *   Combines a patch-to-volume approach and diffusion models effectively.
    *   The design is clearly explained, and the implementation details are readily accessible.
    *   Emphasizes the benefits of using large FOV MRI, an important practical detail.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reported performance gains, while positive, are relatively small. A more substantial improvement would strengthen the impact.
    *   The method is largely dependent on the quality of the initial MRI images.
    *   The synthetic data is only tested on one dataset.  A more comprehensive evaluation including different types of MRI protocols and equipment would be helpful.
    *   The paper doesn't thoroughly analyze or quantify the characteristics of the generated tumors in terms of their visual similarity to real tumors. Visual inspection is useful, but a quantitative comparison (e.g., Radiomics) would be more compelling.

*   **Potential Influence:** The work has the potential to influence the development of more robust and accurate breast tumor segmentation models. The code's public release and the dataset availability will support and promote future research and applications. It also provides a valuable baseline for comparison for future research on breast tumor segmentation.

*   **Rigorous Justification:** The score reflects a cautious appraisal of the paper's impact. While it builds on established techniques, it does so in a practical and well-executed way to address a relevant clinical problem. The limitations, such as the relatively small performance gains and limited generalizability assessment, prevent a higher rating. The novelty lies more in the specific implementation and integration of techniques rather than a groundbreaking theoretical contribution.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond Quantification: Navigating Uncertainty in Professional AI Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03271v1)
- **Authors**: Sylvie Delacroix, Diana Robinson, Umang Bhatt, Jacopo Domenicucci, Jessica Montgomery, Gael Varoquaux, Carl Henrik Ek, Vincent Fortuin, Yulan He, Tom Diethe, Neill Campbell, Mennatallah El-Assady, Soren Hauberg, Ivana Dusparic, Neil Lawrence
- **Abstract**: The growing integration of large language models across professional domains transforms how experts make critical decisions in healthcare, education, and law. While significant research effort focuses on getting these systems to communicate their outputs with probabilistic measures of reliability, many consequential forms of uncertainty in professional contexts resist such quantification. A physician pondering the appropriateness of documenting possible domestic abuse, a teacher assessing cultural sensitivity, or a mathematician distinguishing procedural from conceptual understanding face forms of uncertainty that cannot be reduced to percentages. This paper argues for moving beyond simple quantification toward richer expressions of uncertainty essential for beneficial AI integration. We propose participatory refinement processes through which professional communities collectively shape how different forms of uncertainty are communicated. Our approach acknowledges that uncertainty expression is a form of professional sense-making that requires collective development rather than algorithmic optimization.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper argues that current approaches to uncertainty quantification (UQ) in AI systems, particularly those used in professional domains like healthcare, education, and law, are insufficient.  The authors contend that many consequential forms of uncertainty in these domains are inherently non-quantifiable and that forcing them into probabilistic frameworks can undermine professional judgment and expertise. They distinguish between epistemic and hermeneutic uncertainty, with the latter encompassing ethical uncertainty which is crucial but often overlooked. The paper proposes a shift from algorithmic optimization of UQ to participatory refinement processes, where professional communities collectively shape how uncertainty is expressed in AI systems. This approach emphasizes community deliberation, epistemological development, and the preservation of the spirit of inquiry rather than simply maximizing predictive accuracy. Finally, it outlines a research agenda for developing participatory technical architectures that support this dynamic category-formation process.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty:* The paper's novelty lies primarily in its emphasis on the limitations of purely quantitative approaches to uncertainty in professional AI systems and the proposal for participatory, community-driven refinement of uncertainty expression. While the concept of human-centered AI design isn't new, the specific focus on *uncertainty* as a site for collective epistemological development is relatively novel. The distinction between epistemic and hermeneutic uncertainty is not new in philosophy but its application to the specific problem of uncertainty representation in AI is innovative. The emphasis on maintaining dialogue and avoiding premature convergence in how AI treats uncertainty represents a departure from purely optimization-focused approaches.

*Significance:* The paper addresses a crucial problem that is increasingly relevant as AI systems are deployed in high-stakes professional settings. The potential for AI to erode professional judgment by oversimplifying complex, context-dependent uncertainties is a significant concern. By advocating for participatory design, the paper attempts to safeguard human expertise and promote responsible AI development.  It also highlights the ethical dimension of uncertainty, often neglected in purely technical discussions.  The research agenda outlined is valuable, providing concrete directions for future work.  The paper connects to current trends of AI fairness, accountability, and transparency but offers a nuanced perspective by focusing on the *process* of knowledge construction and uncertainty navigation.

*Strengths:*

*   Strong articulation of the problem: The paper clearly identifies a significant and often overlooked challenge in the deployment of AI systems in professional domains.
*   Well-reasoned arguments: The authors provide compelling arguments for the limitations of quantitative approaches to uncertainty, drawing on examples from various professional fields.
*   Concrete proposal: The concept of participatory refinement offers a practical alternative to purely algorithmic solutions.
*   Clear research agenda: The paper outlines specific research priorities that can guide future work in this area.
*   Interdisciplinary approach: The paper effectively integrates insights from AI, ethics, and social sciences.

*Weaknesses:*

*   Lack of empirical validation: The paper is primarily conceptual and lacks empirical evidence to support its claims. While the arguments are plausible, it would be strengthened by studies that demonstrate the effectiveness of participatory refinement in practice.
*   Implementation challenges: The paper acknowledges the organisational integration challenges associated with participatory refinement, but it could provide more concrete guidance on how to overcome these challenges.  It could also explore the scalability of participatory approaches: how to ensure broad and meaningful participation without making the process prohibitively time-consuming and resource-intensive.
*   Risk of subjective bias: While participatory refinement aims to mitigate the limitations of algorithmic bias, it also carries the risk of introducing subjective biases from the participating communities. The paper could address how to ensure that these processes are inclusive and representative.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel perspective on uncertainty quantification in AI, emphasizing the importance of participatory design and community-driven refinement processes. Its arguments are well-reasoned, and the research agenda provides valuable directions for future work. However, the lack of empirical validation and the acknowledgment of significant implementation challenges temper the overall impact. While the conceptual contribution is strong, the practical feasibility and scalability of the proposed approach require further investigation. Therefore, the paper deserves a score of **7**.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Empowering Lightweight MLLMs with Reasoning via Long CoT SFT
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03321v1)
- **Authors**: Linyu Ou
- **Abstract**: While Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards has enhanced the reasoning of large-scale language models (LLMs), its efficacy for lightweight multimodal language models (MLLMs) with fewer than seven billion parameters remains underexplored. This paper investigates the role of long Chain-of-Thought (long CoT) data in enhancing the reasoning abilities of such MLLMs. Our findings demonstrate that Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) with long CoT data significantly improves MLLM reasoning. Furthermore, we observe that after this initial SFT phase, MLLMs can achieve additional performance gains through a subsequent RL stage. We conclude that a SFT stage with long CoT data is a critical prerequisite for developing the reasoning capabilities of lightweight MLLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores how to improve the reasoning capabilities of lightweight Multimodal Language Models (MLLMs) – specifically, models with fewer than seven billion parameters. It argues that simply applying Reinforcement Learning (RL) after Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), a common technique used for larger models, doesn't work well for smaller MLLMs because these models lack the foundational reasoning capacity.  The core contribution is demonstrating that SFT using *long* Chain-of-Thought (CoT) data is a crucial prerequisite. Furthermore, the paper finds that the difficulty of the SFT data is important; the best results are obtained by training on data that is challenging but solvable for the model (i.e., falls within the model's Zone of Proximal Development, ZPD). The authors validate these findings on 7B and, importantly, an even smaller 3B-parameter model. They release their curated datasets and data generation code.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Long CoT SFT for Lightweight MLLMs:** The finding that long CoT SFT is beneficial isn't entirely new. Previous studies have hinted at this for larger models and other tasks. However, *extensively validating this on both a 7B and, crucially, a 3B parameter MLLM significantly strengthens the argument that it's effective for smaller models where resources are limited.* This is a tangible contribution.
*   **Emphasis on Data Difficulty (ZPD):** The *systematic* analysis of how SFT data difficulty affects reasoning performance *is* a novel and insightful contribution. Framing this in terms of the Zone of Proximal Development (ZPD) provides a solid theoretical basis and a practical way to improve SFT efficiency.  The experiments demonstrating that the model learns best from challenging but solvable samples is a significant finding. This aspect is a clear strength of the paper.
*   **Release of Datasets/Code:** Sharing datasets and data generation code adds practical value to the research.

**Significance:**

*   **Addressing a Key Problem:** Improving the reasoning ability of smaller, more accessible MLLMs is a significant problem. Many real-world applications require models that can reason effectively but don't need the massive scale of the largest models.
*   **Practical Implications:** The insights on data difficulty have practical implications for training MLLMs. By focusing on the appropriate level of challenge, researchers and practitioners can improve performance more efficiently.
*   **Impact on the Field:**  The paper's findings could shift the focus of research from directly applying RL to lightweight MLLMs to first investing in carefully curated, difficult but achievable SFT datasets. This is particularly important for multimodal reasoning tasks.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear and well-written.
*   Strong experimental validation, including results on a relatively small 3B model.
*   Novel insights regarding the importance of data difficulty.
*   Release of resources to facilitate future research.
*   Acknowledges limitations and directions for future work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The paper would benefit from comparisons with other data augmentation techniques for SFT.
*   While they mention using a proxy for problem difficulty, the assumption that success rate from N attempts serves as this proxy is simplistic. This could be better justified.
*   The argument about KL-divergence and catastrophic policy shifts being less of a risk is presented without sufficient evidence. A more rigorous justification or some additional experiments would be helpful. It's based on "hypothesizing" and "assuming".
*   Although they describe their reward and penalty function modifications with "clip-higher and overlong sequence filtering," the specific configurations used are not well explained.
*   Limited focus on RL experiments. Given the focus on SFT's role, the RL section is somewhat limited.

**Overall:**

This is a solid contribution to the field.  The findings are well-supported by experiments and provide valuable guidance for training lightweight MLLMs.  The systematic exploration of data difficulty and the emphasis on high-quality long CoT data are key strengths. While there are some weaknesses in the justification of specific design choices (especially regarding KL-divergence), the overall impact and novelty are sufficient to warrant a positive assessment. The study's impact on future work will likely be substantial, due to its focus on more accessible models and a strong data-centric approach.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents novel and significant findings about improving reasoning in lightweight MLLMs, particularly the role of data difficulty in supervised fine-tuning. The experimental validation is robust, and the open-source resources are valuable. However, some design choices lack rigorous justification, and there are areas for improvement in the experimental design (comparisons with other data augmentation). The score reflects a strong contribution with identified weaknesses.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Heatmap Guided Query Transformers for Robust Astrocyte Detection across Immunostains and Resolutions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03323v1)
- **Authors**: Xizhe Zhang, Jiayang Zhu
- **Abstract**: Astrocytes are critical glial cells whose altered morphology and density are hallmarks of many neurological disorders. However, their intricate branching and stain dependent variability make automated detection of histological images a highly challenging task. To address these challenges, we propose a hybrid CNN Transformer detector that combines local feature extraction with global contextual reasoning. A heatmap guided query mechanism generates spatially grounded anchors for small and faint astrocytes, while a lightweight Transformer module improves discrimination in dense clusters. Evaluated on ALDH1L1 and GFAP stained astrocyte datasets, the model consistently outperformed Faster R-CNN, YOLOv11 and DETR, achieving higher sensitivity with fewer false positives, as confirmed by FROC analysis. These results highlight the potential of hybrid CNN Transformer architectures for robust astrocyte detection and provide a foundation for advanced computational pathology tools.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a hybrid CNN-Transformer model for robust astrocyte detection in histological images. It addresses the challenges of astrocyte detection stemming from their complex morphology, stain variability, and dense clustering. The model combines a CNN backbone for local feature extraction with a Transformer module for global contextual reasoning. A key component is a heatmap-guided query mechanism that generates spatially grounded anchors to improve detection of small and faint astrocytes. The model is evaluated on ALDH1L1 and GFAP stained astrocyte datasets, demonstrating superior performance compared to Faster R-CNN, YOLOv11, and DETR. The results highlight the potential of CNN-Transformer architectures for automated astrocyte detection in computational pathology.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a Relevant Problem:** Astrocyte detection is a crucial task in neuropathological research, and the paper tackles the inherent challenges of automated detection due to cell variability and image complexities.
    *   **Novelty in Architecture:** The hybrid CNN-Transformer approach, coupled with heatmap-guided query initialization, is a well-motivated architecture for this specific task. It effectively addresses limitations of both CNN-only and DETR-style approaches.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation, comparing the proposed model against established object detection methods on multiple datasets (ALDH1L1 and GFAP) and image resolutions. The use of COCO metrics and FROC analysis provides a rigorous performance assessment.
    *   **Clear Improvement:** The experimental results consistently demonstrate superior performance of the proposed model compared to the baselines, especially on higher-resolution images. The analysis of AP and AR metrics offers a granular understanding of the model's strengths in precision and recall.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** Includes visual examples to highlight the advantages of the proposed approach.
    *   **Reproducibility Considerations:** The authors use a public dataset and provide clear details about the implementation and training procedures, which facilitates reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Advance:** While the proposed architecture is novel, it builds upon existing CNN and Transformer-based object detection frameworks. The core components are not entirely revolutionary, though their integration and specific application to astrocyte detection are innovative.
    *   **Limited Scope of Generalization:** The model is trained and evaluated on specific astrocyte stains (ALDH1L1 and GFAP) and image resolutions. Further studies are needed to assess its generalizability to other stains, tissue types, and imaging modalities.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis Could Be More Extensive:** While present, further, more detailed qualitative comparisons with baselines could solidify the argument for improved detection capabilities.
    *   **Scalability to Whole-Slide Images:** The current framework operates on image patches. The paper acknowledges the need for scaling the method to whole-slide images, which presents computational and memory challenges.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Potential Impact on Research:** An accurate and robust automated astrocyte detection method can significantly accelerate research in neuropathology and drug discovery.
    *   **Foundation for Future Work:** The paper provides a solid foundation for future extensions, such as incorporating segmentation branches to delineate astrocytic processes and exploring domain adaptation techniques.
    *   **Encourages Hybrid Architectures:** The work promotes the use of hybrid CNN-Transformer models for biomedical image analysis, demonstrating their potential to overcome limitations of individual architectures.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a well-designed and evaluated CNN-Transformer model that demonstrates clear improvements in astrocyte detection compared to existing methods. The novelty lies primarily in the hybrid architecture and the heatmap-guided query mechanism. While the components are not groundbreaking in isolation, their integration and adaptation to the specific task are significant. The paper has the potential to impact neuropathological research and lays a promising foundation for future development. The limitations regarding generalization and scalability are acknowledged, but they do not diminish the overall contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CV

### InfraDiffusion: zero-shot depth map restoration with diffusion models and prompted segmentation from sparse infrastructure point clouds
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03324v1)
- **Authors**: Yixiong Jing, Cheng Zhang, Haibing Wu, Guangming Wang, Olaf Wysocki, Brian Sheil
- **Abstract**: Point clouds are widely used for infrastructure monitoring by providing geometric information, where segmentation is required for downstream tasks such as defect detection. Existing research has automated semantic segmentation of structural components, while brick-level segmentation (identifying defects such as spalling and mortar loss) has been primarily conducted from RGB images. However, acquiring high-resolution images is impractical in low-light environments like masonry tunnels. Point clouds, though robust to dim lighting, are typically unstructured, sparse, and noisy, limiting fine-grained segmentation. We present InfraDiffusion, a zero-shot framework that projects masonry point clouds into depth maps using virtual cameras and restores them by adapting the Denoising Diffusion Null-space Model (DDNM). Without task-specific training, InfraDiffusion enhances visual clarity and geometric consistency of depth maps. Experiments on masonry bridge and tunnel point cloud datasets show significant improvements in brick-level segmentation using the Segment Anything Model (SAM), underscoring its potential for automated inspection of masonry assets. Our code and data is available at https://github.com/Jingyixiong/InfraDiffusion-official-implement.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper "InfraDiffusion: zero-shot depth map restoration with diffusion models and prompted segmentation from sparse infrastructure point clouds." Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces InfraDiffusion, a novel zero-shot framework to address the challenge of segmenting masonry infrastructure from sparse and noisy point clouds.  It tackles this challenge by first projecting the point cloud into a depth map using a virtual camera projection. Then, it uses a modified Denoising Diffusion Null-space Model (DDNM) to restore the incomplete and noisy depth maps. A key innovation is the incorporation of boundary masks to constrain the diffusion process, preventing spurious artifact generation in regions with no valid point cloud data. Finally, the restored depth maps are fed into the Segment Anything Model (SAM) with coordinate prompts for brick-level semantic segmentation. The authors demonstrate the efficacy of their approach on masonry tunnels and bridges, showing improved segmentation accuracy compared to using SAM directly on the raw depth maps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of existing techniques (virtual camera projection, DDNM, SAM) but innovatively integrates them in a specific pipeline tailored for infrastructure inspection using point clouds. The use of boundary masks to constrain diffusion models in the context of depth map restoration from sparse point clouds is a noteworthy contribution. The application of zero-shot image restoration techniques to depth data derived from point clouds is relatively novel, addressing a real-world problem.
*   **Significance:** Segmentation of masonry structures at the brick level is crucial for automated structural health monitoring, enabling early-stage defect detection. This paper addresses a significant limitation of current methods that rely on high-resolution images, which are often impractical in challenging environments (low-light, difficult access). By leveraging sparse point clouds and the power of diffusion models, the proposed framework offers a promising approach to automated inspection and diagnosis. The use of a zero-shot approach is crucial because it avoids the time-consuming and costly process of creating large, annotated datasets. If the generalisation of SAM for segmentation tasks holds true, the implications of this work are far reaching for automated infrastructure analysis.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a real-world problem:** The paper focuses on a practical challenge in infrastructure inspection with significant economic and safety implications.
    *   **Zero-shot approach:** The ability to perform segmentation without task-specific training is a major advantage, making the method more scalable and adaptable.
    *   **Effective integration of existing techniques:** The paper demonstrates how existing methods can be combined and adapted to achieve significant results.
    *   **Boundary masking:** A well reasoned for innovative contribution that greatly improves the diffusion restoration step for meaningful results.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Qualitative Evaluation:**  While the authors provide qualitative results, the absence of quantitative comparisons against other baseline methods or evaluation metrics is a major drawback. A more rigorous evaluation of segmentation accuracy, such as pixel level accuracy metrics or a direct comparison with alternative point cloud segmentation techniques is needed to determine the performance bounds of the system. The evaluation methodology relies on visual comparisons and mIoU, which is helpful but insufficient.
    *   **Reliance on SAM:** The pipeline relies heavily on the performance of the SAM for segmentation. While SAM is a powerful tool, its performance can vary depending on the image quality and scene complexity. A more in-depth analysis of the limitations of SAM in the context of masonry segmentation and potential mitigation strategies is warranted.
    *   **Limited Datasets:** The study focuses primarily on a few datasets. Evaluating the framework on more diverse types of masonry structures and environmental conditions is necessary to assess its generalizability.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The computational cost associated with diffusion models, especially for large-scale infrastructure, is a concern. The authors could have provided more information about the inference time and resource requirements of their framework.
*   **Potential Influence:** If the method shows good generalisation capability as shown in its preliminary results, the work could influence the development of automated infrastructure inspection systems. The zero-shot approach could enable wider adoption of point cloud based damage detection tools. The proposed pipeline could also inspire further research into the use of diffusion models for other tasks in the field of civil engineering.

*   **Score:** 6

*   **Rationale:**

The paper demonstrates a promising approach to brick-level segmentation of masonry infrastructure from sparse point clouds, which could lead to advancements in structural health monitoring practices. The score of 6 reflects the paper's novelty and potential significance, tempered by the lack of rigorous quantitative evaluation, reliance on a pre-trained model (SAM), and limitations in the dataset and its potential cost. The paper integrates multiple techniques in a novel and potentially impactful way, but more empirical data is needed to validate its robustness and effectiveness compared to existing methods. Furthermore, the high reliance on SAM lowers the uniqueness of the paper given the results have the same baseline as other SAM applications.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### SESGO: Spanish Evaluation of Stereotypical Generative Outputs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03329v1)
- **Authors**: Melissa Robles, Catalina Bernal, Denniss Raigoso, Mateo Dulce Rubio
- **Abstract**: This paper addresses the critical gap in evaluating bias in multilingual Large Language Models (LLMs), with a specific focus on Spanish language within culturally-aware Latin American contexts. Despite widespread global deployment, current evaluations remain predominantly US-English-centric, leaving potential harms in other linguistic and cultural contexts largely underexamined. We introduce a novel, culturally-grounded framework for detecting social biases in instruction-tuned LLMs. Our approach adapts the underspecified question methodology from the BBQ dataset by incorporating culturally-specific expressions and sayings that encode regional stereotypes across four social categories: gender, race, socioeconomic class, and national origin. Using more than 4,000 prompts, we propose a new metric that combines accuracy with the direction of error to effectively balance model performance and bias alignment in both ambiguous and disambiguated contexts. To our knowledge, our work presents the first systematic evaluation examining how leading commercial LLMs respond to culturally specific bias in the Spanish language, revealing varying patterns of bias manifestation across state-of-the-art models. We also contribute evidence that bias mitigation techniques optimized for English do not effectively transfer to Spanish tasks, and that bias patterns remain largely consistent across different sampling temperatures. Our modular framework offers a natural extension to new stereotypes, bias categories, or languages and cultural contexts, representing a significant step toward more equitable and culturally-aware evaluation of AI systems in the diverse linguistic environments where they operate.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SESGO, a Spanish-language framework designed to evaluate social biases in large language models (LLMs) within Latin American cultural contexts.  It addresses the gap in existing bias evaluations, which are primarily US-English-centric and may not adequately capture biases embedded in different linguistic and cultural environments. SESGO adapts the "underspecified question" methodology from the BBQ dataset, using prompts derived from culturally specific expressions and sayings that encode regional stereotypes related to gender, race, socioeconomic class, and national origin. The paper also introduces a novel metric that combines accuracy with the direction of error to provide a more nuanced assessment of model performance and bias alignment. The authors evaluate several commercial LLMs, revealing varying patterns of bias manifestation and demonstrating that bias mitigation techniques optimized for English may not effectively transfer to Spanish.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its focus on a specific language (Spanish) and a particular cultural region (Latin America).  Existing bias detection datasets often rely on direct translations from English, which may miss subtle but significant cultural nuances.  The creation of a dataset grounded in Latin American sayings and expressions represents a valuable contribution. The introduction of a combined accuracy and error direction metric is also a worthwhile methodological refinement. However, the core methodology of using underspecified questions is borrowed from BBQ, which reduces the overall novelty to the *application* and *adaptation* of an existing method rather than a entirely new method.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial issue: the need for more diverse and culturally sensitive evaluations of LLMs. Given the global deployment of these models, it is essential to understand how they behave in different linguistic and cultural contexts and not just in the environments in which they are primarily tested. Demonstrating that bias mitigation techniques developed for English do not necessarily generalize to Spanish is a significant finding with practical implications. The open-sourcing of the SESGO dataset will enable further research and development in this area. One weakness is that it could include a more thorough comparison of the model's behaviour when responding to the translation of known bias prompts versus the culturally grounded prompts.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Culturally Grounded Dataset:** The development of the SESGO dataset, based on Latin American expressions and stereotypes, is a major strength.
    * **Methodological Adaptation:** Adapting the BBQ methodology and introducing a combined accuracy/error metric improves the sensitivity of bias detection.
    * **Empirical Evaluation:**  The systematic evaluation of several commercial LLMs provides valuable insights into the patterns of bias manifestation in Spanish.
    * **Clear Articulation of the Problem:** The paper clearly highlights the limitations of existing English-centric evaluation frameworks.
    * **Emphasis on transfer learning:** The research is valuable as it emphasizes the importance of region-specific bias datasets for the development of LLMs, even after bias-reducing transfer learning processes.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Methodological Dependence:**  The reliance on the BBQ methodology reduces the novelty. While the adaptation is valuable, the core approach is not entirely new.
    * **Scope Limitation:** The focus on a single language and region, while a strength, limits the generalizability of the findings. The paper could have explored more cross-lingual analysis of the same stereotypes (which is partly considered but could be stronger).
    * **Limited scope:** This paper could benefit from providing a clear plan for reducing bias in current LLMs. By offering a plan, the LLMs would be more fair and accountable.

**Overall:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field of bias detection in LLMs, particularly due to its focus on Spanish and Latin American cultural contexts. The SESGO dataset and the new metric provide valuable tools for assessing bias in multilingual models. However, the methodological dependence on existing work slightly reduces its overall novelty. Given its importance and impact within the community, the limitations of this paper are not significant enough to reduce the score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### VulnRepairEval: An Exploit-Based Evaluation Framework for Assessing Large Language Model Vulnerability Repair Capabilities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03331v1)
- **Authors**: Weizhe Wang, Wei Ma, Qiang Hu, Yao Zhang, Jianfei Sun, Bin Wu, Yang Liu, Guangquan Xu, Lingxiao Jiang
- **Abstract**: The adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) for automated software vulnerability patching has shown promising outcomes on carefully curated evaluation sets. Nevertheless, existing datasets predominantly rely on superficial validation methods rather than exploit-based verification, leading to overestimated performance in security-sensitive applications. This paper introduces VulnRepairEval, an evaluation framework anchored in functional Proof-of-Concept (PoC) exploits. Our framework delivers a comprehensive, containerized evaluation pipeline that enables reproducible differential assessment, where repair success requires the original exploit to fail execution against the modified code. The benchmark construction involved extensive data curation: we processed over 400 CVEs and approximately 2,500 potential sources to extract a collection of authentic vulnerability instances (23 Python CVEs) amenable to automated testing with working PoCs. Through VulnRepairEval, we conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 12 popular LLMs and observe a significant performance deficit: even the top-performing model successfully addresses merely 5/23 instances (about 21.7%), exposing critical weaknesses in security-focused applications. Our failure analysis reveals that most unsuccessful attempts stem from imprecise vulnerability identification and patches containing syntactic or semantic errors. Enhanced prompting strategies and multi-agent approaches yield minimal improvements, with overall effectiveness remaining largely unaffected. This work contributes a stringent, practical evaluation framework for LLM-driven vulnerability remediation and underscores the necessity for assessment protocols that authentically reflect real-world exploitation scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "VulnRepairEval: An Exploit-Based Evaluation Framework for Assessing Large Language Model Vulnerability Repair Capabilities":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VulnRepairEval, a new benchmark and evaluation framework for assessing the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically repair software vulnerabilities. Unlike existing benchmarks that primarily rely on unit tests or minimal proof-of-vulnerability triggers, VulnRepairEval uses Proof-of-Concept (PoC) exploits to rigorously validate whether a patch truly eliminates the attack surface. The framework includes an automated pipeline that generates patches, executes PoCs against both vulnerable and patched codebases within isolated Docker containers, and performs differential analysis to confirm patch efficacy. The authors curated a dataset of 23 real-world Python CVEs with working PoCs and evaluated 12 popular LLMs using VulnRepairEval. Their results reveal a significant performance gap, with even the best-performing model successfully repairing only a small fraction of the vulnerabilities. Failure analysis points to inaccurate vulnerability localization and the generation of syntactically or semantically incorrect patches as primary causes. The authors also explored the use of prompt engineering and agentic workflows, but these approaches yielded limited improvements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the exploit-based evaluation approach. Existing LLM repair benchmarks often lack the rigor of validating repairs with real-world exploits. By requiring a PoC to fail against the patched code, VulnRepairEval offers a more stringent and realistic assessment of LLM repair capabilities. The automated pipeline and containerized environment for reproducible differential assessment also contribute to the framework's novelty. The framework is a valuable addition to the testing of LLM vulnerabilities.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant because they highlight a critical gap between LLM-generated patches and actual security. The results expose that passing unit tests or achieving compilation does not equate to a reliable vulnerability fix. This has major implications for security-sensitive applications where LLM-driven patching is being considered. By demonstrating the limitations of current LLMs in genuinely addressing vulnerabilities, VulnRepairEval underscores the need for more rigorous evaluation protocols and highlights areas for future research, such as improved vulnerability localization and patch generation techniques. Further, it clarifies that caution must be practiced in relying on LLM generated security patches.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The PoC-driven validation and containerized environment offer a high degree of confidence in the results.
    *   **Detailed Failure Analysis:** The breakdown of failure modes provides valuable insights into the weaknesses of LLMs in the repair process.
    *   **Real-World Relevance:** The focus on real CVEs and working PoCs ensures that the benchmark is grounded in practical security scenarios.
    *   The research offers a clear methodology that can be easily applied by others to rigorously evaluate the security vulnerabilities introduced by LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset Size:** The dataset size of 23 CVEs, while curated with significant effort, could be expanded to improve the generalizability of the findings. However, the study explicitly mentions that the framework is designed for rigor and reproducibility, prioritizing quality over scale.
    *   **Python-Specific:** The focus on Python vulnerabilities limits the scope of the benchmark. Extending the framework to other languages would increase its broader impact.
    *   **Limited Experimentation with Prompt Engineering and Agentic Workflows:** While the authors explored some prompt engineering and agentic workflow techniques, there may be other advanced strategies that could further improve LLM performance.

*   **Impact:** VulnRepairEval is likely to have a significant impact on the field of LLM-driven software repair by:
    *   Raising awareness of the limitations of current LLMs in genuinely addressing security vulnerabilities.
    *   Establishing a more stringent and realistic evaluation standard for LLM repair tools.
    *   Guiding future research efforts toward improving vulnerability localization, patch generation, and validation techniques.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by creating an important benchmark that allows for the rigorous assessment of LLM vulnerabilities. The research methodology is strong, and it offers practical insights into the shortcomings of modern LLMs with respect to patching vulnerabilities. While the study may have limitations, such as a lack of diversity in languages and the relatively small dataset size, it provides a framework that can be scaled in future research.
    The significance of the benchmark to the field, the rigourous validation techniques employed, and the potential to facilitate the production of more secure systems based on future results are strong indications that the study will have an influence on the field of LLM security.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### EvolveSignal: A Large Language Model Powered Coding Agent for Discovering Traffic Signal Control Algorithms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03335v1)
- **Authors**: Leizhen Wang, Peibo Duan, Hao Wang, Yue Wang, Jian Xu, Nan Zheng, Zhenliang Ma
- **Abstract**: In traffic engineering, the fixed-time traffic signal control remains widely used for its low cost, stability, and interpretability. However, its design depends on hand-crafted formulas (e.g., Webster) and manual re-timing by engineers to adapt to demand changes, which is labor-intensive and often yields suboptimal results under heterogeneous or congested conditions. This paper introduces the EvolveSignal, a large language models (LLMs) powered coding agent to automatically discover new traffic signal control algorithms. We formulate the problem as program synthesis, where candidate algorithms are represented as Python functions with fixed input-output structures, and iteratively optimized through external evaluations (e.g., a traffic simulator) and evolutionary search. Experiments on a signalized intersection demonstrate that the discovered algorithms outperform Webster's baseline, reducing average delay by 20.1% and average stops by 47.1%. Beyond performance, ablation and incremental analyses reveal that EvolveSignal modifications-such as adjusting cycle length bounds, incorporating right-turn demand, and rescaling green allocations-can offer practically meaningful insights for traffic engineers. This work opens a new research direction by leveraging AI for algorithm design in traffic signal control, bridging program synthesis with transportation engineering.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, "EvolveSignal: A Large Language Model Powered Coding Agent for Discovering Traffic Signal Control Algorithms":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EvolveSignal, a framework that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically discover and optimize fixed-time traffic signal control algorithms.  EvolveSignal formulates the problem as program synthesis, representing algorithms as Python functions with fixed input-output structures. An evolutionary search process, guided by external evaluations from a traffic simulator (SUMO), iteratively refines the algorithms. The experiments demonstrate that EvolveSignal can discover algorithms that outperform Webster's method, reducing average delay and stops. The paper further explores the impact of various modifications, demonstrating how the LLM can provide interpretable and practically meaningful insights for traffic engineers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper exhibits a reasonable level of novelty by combining LLMs with evolutionary search for traffic signal control algorithm discovery, particularly for fixed-time control, which has been comparatively less explored with AI compared to adaptive control. While LLMs have been used in other areas of transportation and program synthesis before, the specific application to fixed-time traffic signal algorithm *discovery* and the focus on interpretability sets it apart.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its potential to automate and improve the design of fixed-time signal control, which remains a prevalent method due to its low cost and stability, especially in regions with limited resources and high congestion. The ability to automatically adapt algorithms to specific intersection characteristics and traffic patterns, guided by an LLM that provides understandable justifications, makes it potentially valuable. The insights gained through ablation and incremental analyses further add practical value.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly defines the problem of fixed-time signal control algorithm discovery as a program synthesis task.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The work addresses a real-world problem with practical constraints (fixed-time control, low infrastructure reliance).
    *   **Interpretability:** EvolveSignal stands out by emphasizing the interpretability of the discovered algorithms through LLM-generated explanations. This is a key advantage for adoption by traffic engineers.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The experimental results, using a well-known traffic simulator (SUMO), demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach. The incremental and ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each modification.
    *   **Well-Structured and Written:** The paper is well-written and clearly structured, making it easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are limited to a single, isolated four-leg intersection, a common but simplified scenario. Further evaluation on larger networks and more complex intersection geometries is needed.
    *   **Synthetic Traffic Data:** The experiments rely on synthetic traffic data. Testing with real-world traffic data would strengthen the results and validate the robustness of the approach.
    *   **Reliance on SUMO:** The evaluations are tied to the SUMO simulator. The performance might vary depending on the specific characteristics of the simulator used, and the approach may require re-tuning or adaptations when applied with different traffic simulators.
    *   **LLM Black Box:**  While the paper emphasizes interpretability, the internal reasoning of LLMs is still a black box.  The explanations provided by the LLM need to be rigorously evaluated for correctness and consistency. Future studies should explore ways to verify, validate, or augment LLM-generated reasoning with domain knowledge.
    *   **Complexity:** The paper doesn't fully address the computational demands associated with LLM-powered coding agents and iterative algorithm discovery. Further analysis is necessary to demonstrate if this can be used on larger real world networks.

*   **Potential Influence:** EvolveSignal has the potential to influence the field by opening a new avenue for AI-driven traffic signal control algorithm design. By bridging program synthesis with transportation engineering, it can inspire new research directions and practical applications. It is also likely to inspire more work on incorporating LLMs in traffic management.

*   **Score Justification:** Given the paper's reasonable novelty, practical relevance, and experimental validation, but considering its limited scope and synthetic data reliance, I assign a score of **7**. The strengths of the paper, especially the focus on interpretability, makes it more than just an incremental contribution. The weaknesses require future work to broaden the impact and validate claims.

- **Score**: 7/10

### AI-Driven Drug Repurposing through miRNA-mRNA Relation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03336v1)
- **Authors**: Sharanya Manoharan, Balu Bhasuran, Oviya Ramalakshmi Iyyappan, Mohamed Saleem Abdul Shukkoor, Malathi Sellapan, Kalpana Raja
- **Abstract**: miRNA mRNA relations are closely linked to several biological processes and disease mechanisms In a recent study we tested the performance of large language models LLMs on extracting miRNA mRNA relations from PubMed PubMedBERT achieved the best performance of 0.783 F1 score for miRNA mRNA Interaction Corpus MMIC Here we first applied the finetuned PubMedBERT model to extract miRNA mRNA relations from PubMed for chronic obstructive pulmonary disease COPD Alzheimers disease AD stroke type 2 diabetes mellitus T2DM chronic liver disease and cancer Next we retrieved miRNA drug relations using KinderMiner a literature mining tool for relation extraction Then we constructed three interaction networks 1 disease centric network 2 drug centric network and 3 miRNA centric network comprising 3497 nodes and 16417 edges organized as a directed graph to capture complex biological relationships Finally we validated the drugs using MIMIC IV Our integrative approach revealed both established and novel candidate drugs for diseases under study through 595 miRNA drug relations extracted from PubMed To the best of our knowledge this is the first study to systematically extract and visualize relationships among four distinct biomedical entities miRNA mRNA drug and disease
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores AI-driven drug repurposing by examining miRNA-mRNA relationships across six chronic diseases (COPD, Alzheimer's, stroke, type 2 diabetes, chronic liver disease, and cancer). It uses a fine-tuned PubMedBERT model to extract miRNA-mRNA relationships from PubMed, and KinderMiner to identify miRNA-drug interactions. The identified relationships are visualized through disease-centric, drug-centric, and miRNA-centric networks. Validation of drugs is performed using the MIMIC-IV database and external resources (DrugBank and CTD). The study aims to provide a comprehensive network-based approach for identifying promising drug candidates for repurposing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Integrative approach:** The paper combines multiple data sources (PubMed, KinderMiner, MIMIC-IV, DrugBank, CTD) and methods (LLM-based text mining, network analysis) to generate potentially novel drug repurposing leads. This multi-faceted approach adds robustness to the findings.
    *   **Validation using real-world clinical data:**  The use of the MIMIC-IV database offers a form of clinical validation, linking identified drugs to patient diagnoses and prescription patterns, which is a significant asset. However, the validation with DrugBank and CTD does not necessarily show that the drugs influence the particular disease by influencing the miRNA-mRNA interactions of interest.
    *   **Clear network visualization:**  The generation of disease-centric, drug-centric, and miRNA-centric networks provides a useful visual tool to explore the relationships between these entities, aiding in hypothesis generation.
    *   **Focus on miRNA-drug interactions:** The study focuses on the less explored area of miRNA-drug interactions, which is important as miRNAs are known to be effective drug targets, and the study considers the link to mRNA target genes and clinical data.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited depth of validation:** While MIMIC-IV provides a real-world clinical perspective, prescription data do not necessarily equate to efficacy or a causal relationship between the drug, miRNA, and disease, and can also have high levels of bias. The prescription pattern is not a direct measure of drug efficacy in miRNA modulation.
    *   **Potential biases in literature mining:** Both PubMedBERT and KinderMiner are subject to biases present in the literature they are trained on. Over-represented topics will likely yield more prominent results in the network analysis, irrespective of the actual biological relevance.
    *   **Lack of experimental validation:** The study relies solely on computational methods and data mining. There is no in vitro or in vivo experimental validation to confirm the identified drug repurposing candidates or the underlying miRNA-mRNA mechanisms.
    *   **Limited explanation of results.** The analysis lacks an extensive discussion of the results. For instance, the description of the hubs in the networks, such as those shown in Fig. 4, is not very extensive and has limited depth.
    *   **Novelty?** The paper mentions that 626 miRNAs had no relation with any of the drugs. This casts doubt on whether the drug and miRNA interactions have been clearly studied. In other words, the study is making a hypothesis on something that has not really been proven to exist.
*   **Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty of the paper lies primarily in its integrated approach to drug repurposing by combining LLM-based text mining, network analysis, and clinical data validation, focusing on miRNA-mRNA-drug relationships. However, similar approaches have been used, though perhaps not with this specific combination and emphasis. The significance is primarily for hypothesis generation, potentially identifying drug candidates for further experimental investigation and drug trials.

However, the reliance on existing literature and the lack of any experimental confirmation temper the significance. While the identified drug candidates are interesting, they remain speculative until validated in vitro or in vivo.

**Overall Score:** 5

**Justification:** The paper demonstrates a useful integrative approach to drug repurposing that provides some value to the field of genomics, but is primarily speculative because of a reliance on automated tools that can produce biases or other errors. The integrated approach, especially with its use of real-world clinical data from MIMIC-IV, demonstrates the usefulness of using tools to test relationships between drugs, disease, miRNA/mRNA, and clinical data. However, the weaknesses, particularly the need for experimental validation, prevent it from receiving a higher score. Although the work is good, it needs more support and demonstration of clear cause/effect to show that it is more than just an exercise in text mining.
